\title{Outcome annotation and minimax risk for discrete average treatment effects}

\author{CausalSmith\thanks{Machine-generated by the CausalSmith research pipeline. \maketitle

\begin{abstract}
This paper characterizes the minimax value of outcome annotation in a finite-alphabet causal experiment. A sample of \(n\) complete records \((X,A,Y)\) is augmented by \(m\) additional records \((X,A)\) from the same population, where \(X\) takes \(d\) known values, \(A\) and \(Y\) are binary, and occupied covariate cells satisfy overlap at level \(\epsilon\). For the average treatment effect under consistency and conditional exchangeability, the minimax mean-squared risk \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) satisfies, uniformly over the unrestricted cellwise model,
\[
R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\asymp_\epsilon
\min\left\{1,\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2\log^2(en)}\right\}.
\]
The rate separates outcome information from treatment--covariate marginal information: the \(n^{-1}\) term is the labeled-outcome contribution, while the alphabet-dependent term is governed by the combined \(n+m\) treatment--covariate records. The upper bound is attained by a fully specified hybrid estimator that uses polynomial correction in light cells and stabilized inverse counts in heavy cells. The lower bound uses common-marginal priors whose induced treatment--covariate tables have the same distribution across hypotheses, so the auxiliary sample is aligned with the marginal-information component. The characterization yields exact consistency, parametric-rate, strict-improvement, binary-alphabet, inverse-count-baseline, and known-marginal-limit consequences for the annotation experiment.
\end{abstract}

\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}

Chart-reviewed outcomes are often the scarce resource in observational causal studies, while baseline covariates and treatment assignments are available for many more units. This paper studies the resulting allocation problem in a finite-alphabet causal model. There are \(n\) outcome-labeled records \((X,A,Y)\) and \(m\) additional outcome-unlabeled records \((X,A)\), drawn independently from the same population. The covariate \(X\) takes values in a known alphabet of size \(d\), treatment and outcome are binary, and every occupied covariate cell satisfies overlap at level \(\epsilon\). Under the usual potential-outcome consistency and conditional-exchangeability conditions, the target is the finite-alphabet average treatment effect \(\tau(P)\) in \cref{obj:def:ate-functional}.

The main result gives the finite-sample minimax frontier for this annotation experiment. For every \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), \cref{obj:thm:sharp-annotation-frontier} proves that the minimax mean-squared risk in \cref{obj:def:minimax-risk} obeys
\[
R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\asymp_\epsilon
\min\left\{1,\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2\log^2(en)}\right\}
\]
for all \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), and \(d\ge2\). The rate has two resources. Outcome marks enter through the \(n^{-1}\) term. Treatment--covariate frequencies enter through the second term, where the relevant sample size is the pooled marginal-information size \(n+m\). Thus the theorem gives a quantitative rule for how additional treatment--covariate records change the attainable accuracy of average-treatment-effect estimation in the unrestricted finite-cell model.

The upper bound is constructive. The estimator in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} splits the labeled sample into outcome, pilot, and factorial pools, splits the auxiliary sample into pilot and factorial pools, and then combines the treatment--covariate records for marginal estimation. Cells with small pilot counts use a Chebyshev polynomial correction and a factorial-moment lift, following the polynomial-approximation ideas used in sharp discrete functional estimation \citep{JiaoVenkatHanWeissman2015,WuYang2016} and the rare-cell perspective developed in sample amplification and unseen-species prediction \citep{ValiantValiant2017,OrlitskySureshWu2016}. Cells with larger pilot counts use stabilized inverse-count weights. \Cref{obj:thm:uniform-mixed-upper} shows that this total, finitely calibrated estimator attains the frontier rate uniformly over the model class.

The matching lower bound uses a common-marginal construction. \Cref{obj:thm:common-marginal-converse} supplies priors whose induced random treatment--covariate marginal \(P_{XA}\), the full \(2d\)-cell table, has the same distribution across the two hypotheses, while the outcome regressions shift the target. This aligns the converse with the two-sample annotation design: auxiliary observations reveal the same random marginal object under both branches, and the remaining testing difficulty produces the label floor and the rare-cell approximation term. A separate parametric label floor in \cref{obj:lem:parametric-label-floor} supplies the \(n^{-1}\) component.

The frontier connects several familiar regimes. At \(m=0\), the rate becomes \(\min\{1,n^{-1}+d^2/(n^2\log^2(en))\}\). This refines the bracket of \citet[Theorems 1--2 and the discussion following Theorem 2]{ZengBalakrishnanHanKennedy2024}: their plug-in upper bound \(d^2/n^2+1/n\) holds over the unrestricted binary-outcome, discrete-covariate overlap class, while their minimax lower bound \(d^2/\{n^2\log^2 n\}+1/n\) is stated for \(d\lesssim n\log n\); they leave closing the logarithmic gap by a polynomial estimator as future work. The present result supplies that construction and, with auxiliary records, replaces the marginal-information sample size \(n\) by \(n+m\). With fixed \(n\) and \(d\), \cref{obj:thm:known-marginal-limit} shows that \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) converges as \(m\to\infty\) to the known-marginal risk in \cref{obj:def:known-marginal-risk}, whose order is \(n^{-1}\) by \cref{obj:thm:known-marginal-boundary}. For sequences, \cref{obj:thm:sharp-annotation-frontier} states that consistency is equivalent to \(d_n/\{(n+m_n)\log(en)\}\to0\), and that parametric risk is equivalent to \(d_n=O((n+m_n)\log(en)/\sqrt n)\). The same theorem gives the exact criterion under which auxiliary treatment--covariate records improve the asymptotic order relative to the labeled-only endpoint.

The paper contributes to causal estimation under potential outcomes \citep{Rubin1974,RosenbaumRubin1983,ImbensRubin2015}, semiparametric and doubly robust average-treatment-effect estimation \citep{RobinsRotnitzkyZhao1994,Hahn1998,HiranoImbensRidder2003,BangRobins2005,Farrell2015,AtheyImbensWager2018,ChernozhukovChetverikovDemirerDufloHansenNeweyRobins2018,Kennedy2016}, and auxiliary-data methods for causal and distributional estimation, including semi-supervised treatment-effect, surrogate-outcome, missing-outcome, regression, and quantile settings \citep{ChengAnanthakrishnanCai2021,ChakraborttyLuCaiLi2019,ChakraborttyDai2024,ChakraborttyDaiCarroll2022,KallusMao2025,AnniwaerZhang2025,XuWittenShojaie2025}. Those literatures characterize identification, regular asymptotics, efficiency, and gains from structure or auxiliary measurements. The present analysis gives a finite-alphabet minimax benchmark for the binary-treatment, binary-outcome chart-review design under independent random outcome labeling, occupied-cell overlap, and unrestricted cellwise propensities and outcome regressions.

The appendix records supporting details for the displayed mathematical layer. The paper proceeds as follows. \Cref{sec:related-work} positions the result within causal inference, semi-supervised estimation, and non-smooth discrete functional estimation. \Cref{sec:model} defines the finite-alphabet annotation experiment, the causal target, and minimax risk. \Cref{sec:main-results} states the frontier theorem, the known-marginal benchmark, and the common-marginal converse. \Cref{sec:hybrid-estimator} gives the algorithmic construction behind the upper bound. \Cref{sec:interpretation} discusses the resulting regimes, the inverse-count baseline, the binary-alphabet specialization, and future directions. \Cref{sec:appendix-upper,sec:appendix-lower,sec:appendix-known} collect the upper-bound ingredients, common-marginal lower bounds, known-marginal limit, verification note, and proofs.


\section{Related work}\label{sec:related-work}

This paper studies a \leanref{S-1}{two-sample semi-supervised discrete causal experiment}: a complete outcome-labeled sample of size \leanref{sym:n}{\ensuremath{n}} is augmented by an outcome-unlabeled treatment-covariate sample of size \leanref{sym:m}{\ensuremath{m}} on a finite covariate alphabet of size \leanref{sym:d}{\ensuremath{d}}. In that experiment, the target is the average treatment effect under the usual potential-outcomes interpretation, while the statistical difficulty is governed by how outcome labels and treatment-covariate cell information are split across the two samples. The contribution is therefore most closely connected to five literatures: identification in potential-outcomes models, semiparametric efficiency for average treatment effects with missing or observed outcomes, high-dimensional causal inference under structure, semi-supervised treatment-effect estimation, and minimax estimation of non-smooth discrete functionals.

The identification framework follows the potential-outcomes tradition of \citet{Rubin1974} and the propensity-score formulation of \citet{RosenbaumRubin1983}; see also \citet{ImbensRubin2015} for a systematic account. In that tradition, consistency and conditional exchangeability connect observed outcome regressions to the causal estimand. The present analysis specializes this framework to a finite covariate space and asks how accurately the resulting discrete average treatment effect can be estimated when additional observations reveal treatment and covariates but omit the outcome.

A large semiparametric literature characterizes regular estimation of average treatment effects under missingness, inverse weighting, augmentation, and doubly robust structure. Classical efficiency calculations include \citet{RobinsRotnitzkyZhao1994}, \citet{Hahn1998}, and \citet{HiranoImbensRidder2003}, with augmented and robust estimation developed further by \citet{BangRobins2005}. Modern high-dimensional versions combine orthogonal scores with flexible first-stage learning, as in \citet{Farrell2015}, \citet{AtheyImbensWager2018}, and \citet{ChernozhukovChetverikovDemirerDufloHansenNeweyRobins2018}; \citet{Kennedy2016} gives a broad review of semiparametric causal estimation. Those results identify regular-asymptotic regimes in which nuisance estimation error can be controlled through smoothness, sparsity, sample splitting, or orthogonality. Here the object is the finite-alphabet minimax risk in an unrestricted discrete model, where rare treatment-covariate cells create a non-smooth estimation problem.

Work on high-dimensional and structured semi-supervised inference provides a complementary perspective. \citet{ZhangBrownCai2019}, \citet{ChakraborttyCai2018}, and \citet{DengNingZhaoZhang2024} develop semi-supervised methods for means and regression targets in high-dimensional settings. Related robustness and prediction-assisted ideas appear in \citet{AngelopoulosBatesFannjiangJordanZrnic2023}. These papers show how auxiliary samples and predictive structure can improve estimation in rich covariate spaces. The present setting uses the finite alphabet itself as the primitive statistical object and characterizes the risk uniformly over the unrestricted cell-level causal model.

A related auxiliary-data literature studies how unlabeled samples, missing-outcome structure, transfer information, regression targets, quantile targets, and surrogate outcomes improve estimation. \citet{ChengAnanthakrishnanCai2021}, \citet[Sections 2--4]{HouMukherjeeCai2025}, \citet[Sections 3--5]{Kato2025}, \citet{ChakraborttyDai2024}, \citet{AnniwaerZhang2025}, and \citet{XuWittenShojaie2025} develop semi-supervised, transfer-style, or regular-asymptotic causal estimators, while \citet{ChakraborttyLuCaiLi2019}, \citet{ChakraborttyDaiCarroll2022}, and \citet{KallusMao2025} analyze missing-outcome, quantile, and surrogate-outcome settings. Hou, Mukherjee, and Cai establish root-\(n\) inference and efficiency for partially annotated treatment and response under structured nuisance estimation; Kato studies a two-sample unlabeled-covariate design through generalized Riesz regression and characterizes its efficiency bound. The present paper instead takes an unrestricted finite-cell model and asks for the global finite-sample minimax risk, including rare-cell regimes where regular asymptotics need not apply.

The discrete nature of the model also connects the analysis to minimax estimation of non-smooth functionals of distributions. For entropy, support-size, and related functionals, \citet{JiaoVenkatHanWeissman2015} and \citet{WuYang2016} show that polynomial approximation helps determine sharp rates, while \citet{ValiantValiant2017} and \citet{OrlitskySureshWu2016} develop sample-amplification and unseen-species perspectives on rare cells. The average treatment effect in the annotation experiment has an analogous cell-wise difficulty: estimating outcome means requires labeled observations, while estimating the treatment-covariate weights benefits from the larger treatment-covariate sample. The resulting minimax rate reflects that interaction between labeled outcome information and auxiliary marginal information.

The most direct benchmark is \citet[Theorems 1--2 and the discussion following Theorem 2]{ZengBalakrishnanHanKennedy2024}, who study the same unrestricted binary-outcome, discrete-covariate overlap class with only complete records. Their plug-in upper bound holds for all \(d\), whereas their minimax lower bound is stated for \(d\lesssim n\log n\); the two differ by a logarithmic factor in the alphabet-dependent term. The present paper closes that gap at the supervised endpoint and extends the problem to separate outcome-labeled and outcome-unlabeled samples. \Cref{tab:closest-comparisons} summarizes the sampling design, structural regime, target, and guarantee for the nearest comparisons.

\section{Model and experiment}\label{sec:model}

Throughout the paper, \(x\) indexes a finite covariate cell, \(a\in\{0,1\}\) indexes a treatment arm, and sums over cells run over the known alphabet \([d]=\{1,\ldots,d\}\). Generic constants may depend on the overlap level \(\epsilon\) when this dependence is indicated, while sample-size dependence is displayed explicitly. Norms and asymptotic notation are used in their standard finite-dimensional sense, and all probability laws below live on finite measurable spaces unless stated otherwise.

The statistical object is an observed record with a finite covariate, a binary treatment, and a binary outcome. Writing \leanref{sym:X}{\ensuremath{X}} for the covariate, \leanref{sym:A}{\ensuremath{A}} for treatment, and \leanref{sym:Y}{\ensuremath{Y}} for the observed outcome, the complete record takes values in the following finite space.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_11}[Complete observed-record space]
For each natural number \(d\), define the complete observed-record space by
\[
\operatorname{Obs}(d)\coloneqq [d]\times\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}.
\]
An element of \(\operatorname{Obs}(d)\) is a triple \((X,A,Y)\), where \(X\in[d]\) is the finite-alphabet covariate, \(A\in\{0,1\}\) is the binary treatment indicator, and \(Y\in\{0,1\}\) is the binary observed outcome.
\end{definitionv}

Let \leanref{sym:P}{\ensuremath{P}} denote a probability law on this observed record space. For a cell \(x\), the covariate mass \leanref{sym:p_x}{\ensuremath{p_x}} records how much population probability lies in that cell.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_16}[Covariate cell mass]
For an observed-data law \(P\) on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\) and a covariate value \(x\in[d]\), define the covariate cell mass
\[
p_x
=
\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}}
P(X=x,A=a,Y=y).
\]
\end{definitionv}

We also write \leanref{sym:s_{ax}}{\ensuremath{s_{ax}}} for the treatment-covariate mass in arm \(a\). The treatment propensity \leanref{sym:e_x}{\ensuremath{e_x}} is the conditional treatment probability in the cell, with the finite-space convention made explicit next.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_10}[Cell treatment propensity]
For an observed-data law \(P\) on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\) and a covariate cell \(x\in[d]\), define the treatment propensity \(e_x\) by
\[
e_x
:=
\frac{\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}} P(X=x,A=1,Y=y)}
{\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}} P(X=x,A=a,Y=y)}.
\]
The ratio is interpreted with the usual totalized real division convention, so it is \(0\) when the denominator is \(0\).
\end{definitionv}

\begin{assumptionv}{ass:overlap}[Occupied-cell overlap]
For every covariate cell \(x\in[d]\) with cell mass \(p_x>0\), the propensity score satisfies
\[
\epsilon \le e_x \le 1-\epsilon .
\]
\end{assumptionv}

The occupied-cell overlap condition in \cref{obj:ass:overlap} is the standard strong-overlap restriction on cells that occur with positive probability \citep{ZengBalakrishnanHanKennedy2024}. It keeps both treatment arms represented wherever the covariate distribution places mass and therefore gives the outcome regressions in the ATE functional a common observed-data interpretation.

The experiment combines complete outcome-labeled records with additional treatment-covariate records. We first record the sampling law for the labeled observations and then define the marginal law governing the auxiliary observations.

\begin{assumptionv}{ass:labeled-iid}[Labeled sampling]
The labeled sample is distributed as
\[
\mathcal L_n \sim P^{\otimes n}.
\]
\end{assumptionv}

The labeled sampling condition in \cref{obj:ass:labeled-iid} is the standard independent and identically distributed sampling condition for complete records \citep{ZengBalakrishnanHanKennedy2024}. These observations supply the outcome information used to learn the arm-specific regressions.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_14}[Treatment-covariate marginal]
For any \(d\) and any observed-data law \(P\) on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\), the treatment-covariate marginal \(P_{XA}\) is the probability mass function on \([d]\times\{0,1\}\) obtained by pushing \(P\) forward under the projection \((x,a,y)\mapsto (x,a)\):
\[
P_{XA}
=
P\circ\bigl((x,a,y)\mapsto (x,a)\bigr)^{-1}.
\]
\end{definitionv}

The marginal \leanref{sym:P_{XA}}{\ensuremath{P_{XA}}} records the \(2d\) treatment-covariate cell probabilities while integrating out the outcome. The auxiliary sample \leanref{sym:\mathcal U_m}{\ensuremath{\mathcal U_m}} is drawn from this same population marginal.

\begin{assumptionv}{ass:auxiliary-iid}[Auxiliary sampling]
The auxiliary sample is distributed as
\[
\mathcal U_m \sim P_{XA}^{\otimes m}.
\]
\end{assumptionv}

\Cref{obj:ass:auxiliary-iid} is the standard same-population outcome-unlabeled sampling condition \citep{ChakraborttyDai2024}. It lets the auxiliary records refine information about the treatment-covariate distribution relevant for weighting cell-level outcome contrasts.

\begin{assumptionv}{ass:sample-independence}[Sample independence]
The labeled and auxiliary samples are independent:
\[
\mathcal L_n \perp\!\!\!\perp \mathcal U_m .
\]
\end{assumptionv}

The independent two-sample design in \cref{obj:ass:sample-independence} is standard in semisupervised treatment-effect settings with separate labeled and outcome-unlabeled samples \citep{ChakraborttyDai2024}. Under this design, the product experiment separates outcome information from additional marginal information.

The causal interpretation uses a finite potential-outcome overlay on the same observed-data law. Let \leanref{sym:Y(a)}{\ensuremath{Y(a)}} denote the binary potential outcome under arm \(a\); the following assumptions connect the observed-data functional to the causal average treatment effect in the usual potential-outcome framework \citep{Rubin1974,ImbensRubin2015}.

\begin{assumptionv}{ass:consistency}[Potential-outcome consistency]
The observed binary outcome equals the potential outcome under the realized treatment:
\[
Y = Y(A)
\]
almost surely.
\end{assumptionv}

\Cref{obj:ass:consistency} is the standard consistency condition \citep{ZengBalakrishnanHanKennedy2024}. It identifies the observed outcome within each treatment arm with the corresponding potential outcome.

\begin{assumptionv}{ass:conditional-exchangeability}[Conditional exchangeability]
The potential outcomes are conditionally independent of treatment given the finite covariate:
\[
(Y(0),Y(1)) \perp\!\!\!\perp A \mid X .
\]
\end{assumptionv}

\Cref{obj:ass:conditional-exchangeability} is the standard conditional exchangeability condition \citep{ZengBalakrishnanHanKennedy2024}. Together with \cref{obj:ass:consistency,obj:ass:overlap}, it gives the observed conditional means their usual causal meaning within each finite covariate cell \citep{RosenbaumRubin1983,ImbensRubin2015}.

More explicitly, let \(Q\) be a full-data law for
\((X,A,Y(0),Y(1))\), and let \(P\) be its observed-data pushforward under
\[
(x,a,y_0,y_1)\longmapsto (x,a,y_a).
\]
Consistency and conditional exchangeability imply, for every occupied cell \(x\) and arm \(a\),
\[
\mathbb E_Q\{Y(a)\mid X=x\}
=
\mathbb E_Q\{Y(a)\mid X=x,A=a\}
=
\mathbb E_P\{Y\mid X=x,A=a\}.
\]
Overlap makes the last conditional mean identified in every occupied cell. Therefore
\[
\mathbb E_Q\{Y(1)-Y(0)\}
=
\sum_{x=1}^d p_x
\left[
\mathbb E_P\{Y\mid X=x,A=1\}
-
\mathbb E_P\{Y\mid X=x,A=0\}
\right],
\]
which is exactly the observed-data functional defined below. Thus the minimax analysis is over observed laws \(P\), while the causal interpretation is supplied by any compatible full-data law \(Q\) satisfying these assumptions.

We now collect the finite-space probability notation, the model class, and the target functional used in the risk analysis.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_13}[Probability laws on a measurable space]
For a measurable space \(\mathcal Z\), let \(\mathsf{Prob}(\mathcal Z)\) denote the set of all probability measures on \(\mathcal Z\). For the finite spaces used below, including \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\) and \([d]\times\{0,1\}\), the measurable structure is the full power set.
\end{definitionv}

\begin{definitionv}{def:model-class}[Model class \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\)]
For an overlap constant \(\epsilon\) and alphabet size \(d\), define
\[
\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}
:=
\Bigl\{
P\in\mathsf{Prob}\bigl([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\bigr):
d\ge2,\ 0<\epsilon<1/2,\ \text{\(P\) satisfies \cref{obj:ass:overlap}}
\Bigr\}.
\]
\end{definitionv}

Thus \leanref{sym:\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}}{\ensuremath{\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}}} is the class of observed-data laws on the finite alphabet satisfying occupied-cell overlap at level \(\epsilon\). All suprema in the main risk statements range over this unrestricted cell-level model.

For each arm and cell, the outcome regression \leanref{sym:mu_{ax}}{\ensuremath{\mu_{ax}}} is the conditional success probability computed from the observed law.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_17}[Outcome regression]
For a law \(P\) on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\), a treatment arm \(a\in\{0,1\}\), and a covariate cell \(x\in[d]\), define
\[
\mu_{ax}(P)
:=
\frac{P(X=x,A=a,Y=1)}
{\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}} P(X=x,A=a,Y=y)} .
\]
The quotient is interpreted as total real division, so \(\mu_{ax}(P)=0\) when the treatment--covariate cell mass \(\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}}P(X=x,A=a,Y=y)\) is zero. In particular, \(\mu_{1x}(P)\) and \(\mu_{0x}(P)\) are obtained by taking \(a=1\) and \(a=0\), respectively.
\end{definitionv}

\begin{definitionv}{def:ate-functional}[Average treatment effect functional]
For a finite alphabet size \(d\) and an observed-data law \(P\) on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\), define the average treatment effect functional by
\[
\tau(P):=\sum_{x=1}^{d}p_x(P)\bigl(\mu_{1x}(P)-\mu_{0x}(P)\bigr).
\]
Here \(p_x(P)\) is the covariate cell mass at \(x\), and \(\mu_{ax}(P)\) is the binary outcome regression in treatment arm \(a\) and covariate cell \(x\), totalized by division in the arm cell.
\end{definitionv}

The functional \leanref{sym:\tau(P)}{\ensuremath{\tau(P)}} averages the within-cell treated-control contrast using the population covariate distribution. Under the potential-outcome conditions above, it is the finite-alphabet causal average treatment effect.

Finally, the annotation experiment and minimax risk formalize the statistical problem studied in the rest of the paper.

\begin{definitionv}{def:annotation-experiment}[Annotation experiment \(\mathcal E_{n,m,d,\epsilon}\)]
Define the annotation experiment by
\[
\mathcal E_{n,m,d,\epsilon}
:=
\Bigl\{
P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}:
P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon},\ n\ge1,\ m\ge0,\ d\ge2
\Bigr\}.
\]
The product law corresponds to \(n\) independent complete outcome-labeled records and \(m\) independent additional outcome-unlabeled treatment-covariate records, with independence between the two samples.
\end{definitionv}

\Cref{obj:def:annotation-experiment} defines \leanref{sym:\mathcal E_{n,m,d,\epsilon}}{\ensuremath{\mathcal E_{n,m,d,\epsilon}}}, the experiment in which outcome labels and treatment-covariate information enter through separate independent samples. This formulation makes the amount of outcome information and the amount of marginal information explicit.

The realized data live on the corresponding product sample space.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_18}[Joint sample space]
For natural numbers \(n,m,d\), the joint sample space is
\[
\mathrm{Sample}(n,m,d)
=
\bigl([n]\to [d]\times\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}\bigr)
\times
\bigl([m]\to [d]\times\{0,1\}\bigr).
\]
Equivalently, an element consists of \(n\) complete records \((X,A,Y)\) and \(m\) outcome-unlabeled records \((X,A)\).
\end{definitionv}

\begin{definitionv}{def:minimax-risk}[Minimax risk \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\)]
Define the minimax mean-squared risk by
\[
R_\epsilon(n,m,d)
:=
\inf_{T\in\mathcal T_{n,m,d}}
\sup_{P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}}
E_{P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}}
\!\left[\bigl(T-\tau(P)\bigr)^2\right],
\]
where \(\mathcal T_{n,m,d}\) is the class of estimators measurable with respect to the labeled sample and auxiliary sample.
\end{definitionv}

The risk \leanref{sym:R_\epsilon(n,m,d)}{\ensuremath{R_\epsilon(n,m,d)}} measures the best possible uniform mean-squared accuracy for estimating the ATE functional in \cref{obj:def:ate-functional} under the experiment in \cref{obj:def:annotation-experiment}. The estimator class \leanref{sym:\mathcal T_{n,m,d}}{\ensuremath{\mathcal T_{n,m,d}}} contains all measurable procedures based on the two samples, so the minimax risk isolates the statistical difficulty created by finite cells, overlap, and the split between outcome-labeled and outcome-unlabeled information.


\section{Main minimax results}\label{sec:main-results}

The minimax analysis separates two sources of information in the annotation experiment. Outcome marks contribute through the \(n\) complete records, while treatment--covariate frequencies contribute through the combined table information carried by the \(n\) labeled records and the \(m\) auxiliary records. The resulting rate uses the total marginal-information size \leanref{sym:N}{\ensuremath{N}} and the labeled-sample logarithmic scale.

\begin{definitionv}{def:frontier-rate-handle}[Frontier rate \(r_\epsilon(n,m,d)\)]
Put
\[
N=n+m,
\qquad
\ell_n=\log(en).
\]
The unequal-information annotation-frontier rate is
\[
r_\epsilon(n,m,d)
:=
\min\!\left\{
1,\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{N^2\ell_n^2}
\right\}.
\]
\end{definitionv}

The rate \leanref{sym:r_\epsilon(n,m,d)}{\ensuremath{r_\epsilon(n,m,d)}} has two components. The term \(1/n\) is the labeled-outcome contribution. The term \(d^2/(N^2\ell_n^2)\) is the discrete marginal-estimation contribution, where the alphabet dimension is compared with the amount of treatment--covariate information available from both samples.

The upper construction and the converse both use finite calibration devices. For the estimator, the calibration fixes dyadic overlap, polynomial degree, light-cell scale, and the data splits used below.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_7}[Finite calibration scales for the mixed estimator]
Set
\[
A_\star=7056,\qquad c^\circ=\frac1{256}.
\]
For \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), let \(j_\epsilon\) be the least integer \(j\ge1\) such that \(2^{-j}\le\epsilon\), and define the dyadic overlap certificate
\[
\bar\epsilon(\epsilon)=2^{-j_\epsilon}.
\]
Then \(0<\bar\epsilon(\epsilon)\le\epsilon<2\bar\epsilon(\epsilon)\). Let \(H_\epsilon\) be the least positive integer \(H\) satisfying
\[
H\ge4,\qquad \frac H4\ge15+\frac{24}{c^\circ},\qquad
Hc^\circ\bar\epsilon(\epsilon)\ge672,\qquad
Hc^\circ\bar\epsilon(\epsilon)\ge24.
\]
For \(n\ge1\), put
\[
h_n=1+\lceil\log_2 n\rceil,\qquad
L(n)=\max\{2,\lfloor c^\circ h_n\rfloor\}.
\]
For \(n\ge24\) and \(m\ge0\), define
\[
M_0=\lfloor n/3\rfloor,\qquad
n_p=\left\lfloor\frac{n-M_0}{2}\right\rfloor,\qquad
n_f=n-M_0-n_p,
\]
\[
m_p=\lfloor m/2\rfloor,\qquad m_f=m-m_p,\qquad
M_p=n_p+m_p,\qquad M_f=n_f+m_f,
\]
and
\[
t_p=\frac{M_p}{8},\qquad t=\frac{M_f}{8}.
\]
The light-cell scale and pilot-count threshold are
\[
B(n,m,\epsilon)=\frac{H_\epsilon L(n)}{\min(t_p,t)},\qquad
k_0(n,m,\epsilon)=\left\lfloor\frac{t_p B(n,m,\epsilon)}4\right\rfloor .
\]
For \(n<24\), the calibration predicate using these quantities is declared false.
\end{definitionv}

The lower-bound argument compares two randomized laws through their induced distribution over the treatment--covariate table. The following notation records that marginal distribution for a branch prior.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_12}[Induced law of the treatment--covariate marginal]
Let \(b\in\{0,1\}\) and let \(\Pi_b\) be a probability measure on \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\). If \(P\sim\Pi_b\), define
\[
\mathcal L_{\Pi_b}(P_{XA})
:=
(P\mapsto P_{XA})_{\#}\Pi_b .
\]
Thus, for every measurable set \(B\subseteq\mathsf{Prob}([d]\times\{0,1\})\),
\[
\mathcal L_{\Pi_b}(P_{XA})(B)
=
\Pi_b\{P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}:P_{XA}\in B\}.
\]
Equivalently, \(\mathcal L_{\Pi_b}(P_{XA})\) is the distribution of the full \(2d\)-cell treatment--covariate marginal table induced when the complete-data law \(P\) is drawn from \(\Pi_b\).
\end{definitionv}

A useful benchmark gives the target risk when the treatment--covariate marginal is observed exactly. In that experiment, procedures may use the labeled sample together with the exact \(P_{XA}\) table.

\begin{definitionv}{def:known-marginal-risk}[Known-marginal minimax risk]
For nonnegative integers \(n\) and \(d\) and a real overlap parameter \(\epsilon\), the known-marginal minimax risk is
\[
R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d)
:=
\inf_{T\in\mathcal T^{\mathrm{known}}_{n,d}}
\sup_{P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}}
\int
\bigl(T(\mathcal L_n,P_{XA})-\tau(P)\bigr)^2
\,dP^{\otimes n}.
\]
Here \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) is the model class in \cref{obj:def:model-class}, \(\tau(P)\) is the average treatment effect functional in \cref{obj:def:ate-functional}, and \(\mathcal T^{\mathrm{known}}_{n,d}\) is the class of measurable estimators \(T\) based on the labeled sample together with the exact treatment-covariate table \(P_{XA}\), with \(T(\mathcal L_n,P_{XA})\in[-1,1]\) for every input.
\end{definitionv}

Here \leanref{sym:\mathcal T^{\mathrm{known}}_{n,d}}{\ensuremath{\mathcal T^{\mathrm{known}}_{n,d}}} is the estimator class for the known-marginal problem, and \leanref{sym:R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d)}{\ensuremath{R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d)}} records the corresponding minimax mean-squared error.

\begin{theoremv}{thm:known-marginal-boundary}[Known marginal boundary]
Suppose the overlap constant \(\epsilon\) satisfies \(0<\epsilon<1/2\). For the known-marginal minimax risk \(R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d)\) defined in \cref{obj:def:known-marginal-risk}, there exist real constants \(c_\epsilon\) and \(C_\epsilon\) with \(0<c_\epsilon\le C_\epsilon\) such that, for every \(n,d\in\mathbb N\) with \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge2\),
\[
\frac{c_\epsilon}{n}
\le
R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d)
\le
\frac{C_\epsilon}{n}.
\]
\end{theoremv}

The benchmark establishes the labeled-sample boundary when the full marginal table is available. Once \(P_{XA}\) is known, the remaining uniform risk is of order \(1/n\), so additional treatment--covariate observations move the original annotation problem toward this labeled-outcome scale.

The upper bound uses an explicit hybrid estimator. Its calibration first fixes a binary-length scale and a clipping map for the final statistic. The long \emph{Context} item in the next generated definition records formal dependencies and may be skipped on a first reading. Also, the type \(\mathcal T_{n,m,d}\) in the estimator display denotes precisely the map from the two sample spaces to \(\mathbb R\) displayed immediately below it.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_8}[Binary length \(b_n\)]
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Context.)} Work under \cref{obj:synth_1,obj:synth_2,obj:synth_3,obj:synth_4,obj:synth_5,obj:synth_6,obj:synth_7,obj:synth_9,obj:synth_10,obj:synth_11,obj:synth_12,obj:synth_13,obj:synth_14,obj:synth_16,obj:synth_17,obj:synth_18,obj:synth_19,obj:synth_20,obj:synth_21,obj:synth_22,obj:ass:overlap,obj:ass:labeled-iid,obj:ass:auxiliary-iid,obj:ass:sample-independence,obj:ass:consistency,obj:ass:conditional-exchangeability,obj:def:model-class,obj:def:ate-functional,obj:def:annotation-experiment,obj:def:minimax-risk,obj:def:frontier-rate-handle,obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle,obj:def:common-marginal-prior-handle,obj:def:finite-small-instance,obj:def:known-marginal-experiment,obj:def:known-marginal-risk,obj:thm:known-marginal-boundary,obj:thm:uniform-mixed-upper,obj:prop:binary-alphabet-parametric-rate,obj:lem:chebyshev-factorial-certificate,obj:lem:fixed-sample-hybrid-transfer,obj:lem:finite-alternation-duality,obj:lem:parametric-label-floor,obj:lem:poisson-inverse-marginal-energy-scalar,obj:lem:poisson-prefix-count-law,obj:lem:inverse-count-poisson-prefix-transfer,obj:lem:shifted-chebyshev-quotient-coefficient-bound,obj:lem:annotation-frontier-strict-improvement,obj:lem:common-marginal-recipe-target-concentration,obj:lem:common-marginal-recipe-scale-tail,obj:lem:common-marginal-canonical-certificate-family,obj:lem:calibrated-envelope-absorption,obj:lem:finite-side-measurable-comparison,obj:lem:hybrid-light-branch-mean-bound,obj:lem:poisson-inverse-arm-mean-formula,obj:lem:heavy-light-second-moment-sum,obj:lem:hybrid-heavy-branch-mean-bound,obj:lem:calibrated-hybrid-variance-bound,obj:lem:calibrated-hybrid-mean-bias,obj:lem:finite-product-padding-identity,obj:lem:finite-side-cover-approximate-decision,obj:thm:common-marginal-converse,obj:thm:sharp-annotation-frontier,obj:lem:poisson-inverse-count-risk,obj:thm:inverse-count-baseline,obj:lem:poisson-hybrid-risk,obj:lem:explicit-chebyshev-calibration,obj:lem:common-marginal-uniform-intensity,obj:lem:finite-side-information-convergence,obj:lem:common-marginal-recipe-transfer,obj:lem:fixed-sample-common-marginal-transfer,obj:thm:known-marginal-limit}.
\item \textbf{(Integer.)} Let \(n\ge1\) be an integer.
\end{itemize}
Define
\[
b_n:=1+\lceil\log_2 n\rceil .
\]
Equivalently, in the notation of \cref{obj:synth_7}, \(b_n=h_n\).
\end{definitionv}

\begin{definitionv}{synth_3}[Clipping to the unit interval]
For \(z\in\mathbb R\), define
\[
\operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]}(z)
=
\max\{-1,\min\{1,z\}\}.
\]
Equivalently, \(\operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]}(z)\) is the Euclidean projection of \(z\) onto the interval \([-1,1]\).
\end{definitionv}

The estimator itself combines a light-cell polynomial correction with a heavy-cell inverse-count correction. The construction is total: outside its finite calibration region it returns zero, and inside that region it averages a clipped Poisson-prefix statistic. For orientation before the full display, if
\[
W_{a,L}(s_0,s_1)=\sum_{r_0,r_1}c_{a,r_0,r_1}s_0^{r_0}s_1^{r_1},
\]
then its factorial lift at intensity \(t\) is
\[
\widehat W_{a,L}(K_0,K_1)
=
\sum_{r_0,r_1}c_{a,r_0,r_1}
\frac{(K_0)_{r_0}(K_1)_{r_1}}{t^{r_0+r_1}}.
\]
The complete definition and its moment identity appear in \cref{obj:synth_4}.

\begin{algorithmv}{def:mixed-estimator-handle}[Mixed estimator definition]
For \(n,m,d\in\mathbb N\) and a real overlap constant \(\epsilon\), define
\[
\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}: \mathcal T_{n,m,d}
\]
as the following map from
\[
\bigl([d]\times\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}\bigr)^n
\times
\bigl([d]\times\{0,1\}\bigr)^m
\]
to \(\mathbb R\). Put
\[
M_0=\lfloor n/3\rfloor,\qquad
n_p=\left\lfloor\frac{n-M_0}{2}\right\rfloor,\qquad
n_f=n-M_0-n_p,
\]
\[
m_p=\lfloor m/2\rfloor,\qquad
m_f=m-m_p,\qquad
M_p=n_p+m_p,\qquad
M_f=n_f+m_f,
\]
and
\[
u=\frac{M_0}{8},\qquad
t_p=\frac{M_p}{8},\qquad
t=\frac{M_f}{8}.
\]
Let \(b_n\) denote the binary-length calibration quantity used here, and set
\[
L=L(n),\qquad B=B(n,m,\epsilon),\qquad k_0=k_0(n,m,\epsilon),
\]
with \(\bar\epsilon=\bar\epsilon(\epsilon)\), \(A_\star=7056\), and \(c^\circ=1/256\). The calibration predicate \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) holds exactly when all of the following finite inequalities hold:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Sample size.)} \(24\le n\).
\item \textbf{(Block proportions.)}
\[
\frac1{32}\le\frac un\le\frac1{24},\qquad
\frac1{32}\le\frac{t_p}{n+m}\le\frac3{32},\qquad
\frac1{32}\le\frac t{n+m}\le\frac3{32},\qquad
\frac13\le\frac t{t_p}\le3.
\]
\item \textbf{(Degree and scale.)}
\[
\frac{c^\circ b_n}{2}\le L,\qquad
8\le t_pB,\qquad
L\le tB.
\]
\item \textbf{(Polynomial size.)}
\[
L^4A_\star^{2L}\le n,\qquad
L^6A_\star^{2L}\le n.
\]
\item \textbf{(Tail calibration.)}
\[
15L+12b_n\le\frac{t_pB}{4},\qquad
n^{12}\le\left(1+\bar\epsilon\frac t{t_p}\right)^{k_0},\qquad
12b_n\le\bar\epsilon tB.
\]
\end{itemize}
If \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) fails, set
\[
\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}(\mathcal L_n,\mathcal U_m)=0.
\]
If \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) holds, set
\[
\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}(\mathcal L_n,\mathcal U_m)
=
\sum_{r_0=0}^{M_0}
\sum_{r_p=0}^{M_p}
\sum_{r_f=0}^{M_f}
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(u)=r_0\}
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t_p)=r_p\}
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t)=r_f\}
\,\Phi_\epsilon(\mathcal L_n,\mathcal U_m;r_0,r_p,r_f),
\]
where
\[
\Phi_\epsilon(\mathcal L_n,\mathcal U_m;r_0,r_p,r_f)
=
\operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]}\left(\sum_{x\in[d]} Z_x\right).
\]
For each \(x\in[d]\), let \(J_x\) be the pilot-prefix count in cell \(x\), let \(S_{ax}\) be the outcome-block prefix count with covariate \(x\), treatment \(a\), and outcome \(1\), and let \(K_{ax}\) be the factorial-pool prefix count with covariate \(x\) and treatment \(a\). Then
\[
Z_x
=
\begin{cases}
\dfrac{S_{1x}}u\,\widehat W_{1,L,x}
-
\dfrac{S_{0x}}u\,\widehat W_{0,L,x},
& J_x\le k_0,\\[1.2em]
\dfrac{S_{1x}}u\,\dfrac{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1}{K_{1x}+1}
-
\dfrac{S_{0x}}u\,\dfrac{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1}{K_{0x}+1},
& k_0<J_x.
\end{cases}
\]
Here \(\widehat W_{a,L,x}\) is the factorial lift, at degree \(L\), scale \(B\), and intensity \(t\), of the light-cell polynomial weight
\[
W_{a,L}(s_0,s_1)=\frac{s_0+s_1}{B}G_L(s_a/B),
\]
where
\[
H_L(z)=\frac{1-T_L(1-2z)}{2L^2},\qquad
E_L(z)=\frac{H_L(z)}z,\qquad
G_L(z)=\frac{1-E_L(z)}z,
\]
with polynomial continuation at zero.
\end{algorithmv}

The statistic \leanref{sym:\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}}{\ensuremath{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}}} uses labeled outcomes through the \(S_{ax}\) counts and uses the larger treatment--covariate pool through the pilot and factorial counts. Light cells receive the polynomial correction familiar from discrete functional estimation \citep{JiaoVenkatHanWeissman2015,WuYang2016}, while heavy cells use direct inverse-count weighting.

\begin{theoremv}{thm:uniform-mixed-upper}[Uniform mixed upper bound]
Fix an overlap constant \(\epsilon\) with \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).  There exists a constant \(C_\epsilon>0\) such that, for every \(n\ge 1\), \(m\ge 0\), and \(d\ge 2\), the total computable hybrid estimator \(\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}\) of \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} satisfies
\[
\sup_{P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}}
E_{P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}}
\!\left[
\left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2
\right]
\le
C_\epsilon\, r_\epsilon(n,m,d),
\]
where \(\tau(P)\) is the average treatment effect functional in \cref{obj:def:ate-functional}, and \(r_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) is the annotation-frontier rate in \cref{obj:def:frontier-rate-handle}.
\end{theoremv}

The upper bound establishes that the displayed rate is attainable uniformly over the model class. Its form reflects the experiment: increasing \(m\) improves the marginal-frequency term through \(N=n+m\), while the labeled-outcome term remains governed by \(n\). Here \emph{total computable} means that the displayed finite sums define an everywhere-defined statistic, including at calibration failure; implementation-oriented scope is discussed in \cref{sec:interpretation}.

The matching converse is organized through priors with a common induced marginal. In the rare-cell regime, the construction below generates a shared random treatment--covariate table and perturbs the treated outcome regression across two branches.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_22}[Common-marginal finite-atom construction]
Fix \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), an integer \(L\ge2\), scales \(B>0\) and \(a>0\), and write
\[
\kappa=\frac{1-2\epsilon}{\epsilon},\qquad I=[a/\kappa,B].
\]
Let \(\sigma\) be a finite signed measure on \(I\) with total variation one, let
\(h=d\sigma/d|\sigma|\), and define a probability law \(\nu\) on \(\{0\}\cup I\) by
\[
\nu(dp)=\frac{a}{p+a}\,|\sigma|(dp)\quad(p\in I),
\qquad
\nu(\{0\})=1-\int_I\frac{a}{p+a}\,|\sigma|(dp).
\]
For an integer \(k\le d-1\), the finite-atom construction has atom-generator law
\[
\Gamma=\nu^{\otimes k}
\]
on \(w=(p_1,\ldots,p_k)\).  Given \(w\), form \(k\) rare cells and one reservoir cell.  For each rare cell with \(p_i>0\), set
\[
s_i=\epsilon(p_i+a),\qquad e_i=\frac{s_i}{p_i},
\]
put \(\mu_{0i}=0\), and set the treated outcome regression in branch \(b\in\{0,1\}\) to
\[
\mu_{1i}^{(b)}=\frac{1+(2b-1)h(p_i)}{2}.
\]
At atoms with \(p_i=0\), assign the same legal propensity and outcome values in both branches.  The reservoir has raw mass
\[
p_*(w)=1-kE_\nu[p],
\]
propensity \(1/2\), and outcome regressions equal to zero.  Alphabet cells not used by the \(k\) rare cells or the reservoir have mass zero.

The raw total mass is
\[
S(w)=p_*(w)+\sum_{i=1}^k p_i.
\]
The branch law \(P_b(w)\) is obtained by dividing every raw cell mass by \(S(w)\) and keeping the propensities and outcome regressions just specified.  The corresponding raw target is
\[
r_b(w)=\sum_{i=1}^k p_i\,\mu_{1i}^{(b)},
\]
so the normalized target value of the generated law is \(r_b(w)/S(w)\).  The branch priors are the pushforwards
\[
\Pi_b=\Gamma\circ P_b^{-1},\qquad b\in\{0,1\}.
\]
Thus the two branches use the same generated rare-cell masses, reservoir mass, propensities, normalization factor, and treatment--covariate table, while differing only in the branch sign of the treated rare-cell outcome regressions.
\end{definitionv}

The finite-atom construction makes the shared marginal table explicit: the branches draw the same rare-cell masses and propensities, then separate the targets through outcome regressions. The common-marginal handle chooses between this rare-cell recipe and a parametric point-mass regime according to the comparison in the following definition.

\begin{definitionv}{def:common-marginal-prior-handle}[Common-marginal prior handle]
Fix \(n,m,d\in\mathbb N\), \(\epsilon\in\mathbb R\), and a calibration consisting of constants
\[
C_\epsilon,\quad C_\epsilon^\circ,\quad b_0,\quad b_\epsilon,\quad
\gamma_\epsilon,\quad c_\epsilon^\circ,\quad c_\epsilon,\quad C_d
\]
with all eight constants positive and \(c_\epsilon\le 1/4\).  A common-marginal prior handle at \((n,m,d)\) consists of integers \(L,k\), real numbers \(B,a\), and a calibrated recipe supplying two induced priors, denoted \(\Pi_0(H)\) and \(\Pi_1(H)\), on laws on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\), subject to
\[
L=\left\lceil C_\epsilon^\circ \log(en)\right\rceil,\qquad
B=\frac{b_0L}{n+m},\qquad
a=\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{L^2},
\]
and
\[
k=\min\left\{d-1,\left\lfloor c_\epsilon^\circ(n+m)L\right\rfloor\right\}.
\]
The recipe uses the finite-atom construction exactly when
\[
\min\left\{1,\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2\log(en)^2}\right\}
>
\frac{C_\epsilon}{n}.
\]
For a handle \(H\) and a branch \(b\in\{0,1\}\), the common-marginal prior is the branch-indexed measure
\[
\Pi_b(H)=
\begin{cases}
\Pi_1(H), & b=1,\\
\Pi_0(H), & b=0.
\end{cases}
\]
\end{definitionv}

The two branches \leanref{sym:\Pi_a}{\ensuremath{\Pi_0,\Pi_1}} are calibrated so that the auxiliary sample sees the same random marginal table under both hypotheses. Hence the distinguishing information in the lower-bound experiment must pass through the labeled outcomes.

\begin{theoremv}{thm:common-marginal-converse}[Common marginal converse]
Let \(\epsilon\), the overlap parameter, satisfy \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).  Then there exist a constant \(c_\epsilon>0\) and a collection of \(\epsilon\)-dependent calibration constants as in \cref{obj:def:common-marginal-prior-handle} such that, for every \(n,m,d\in\mathbb N\) with \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), and \(d\ge2\), the regime-dependent handle in \cref{obj:def:common-marginal-prior-handle} supplies priors \(\Pi_0\) and \(\Pi_1\) satisfying
\[
\mathcal L_{\Pi_0}(P_{XA})=\mathcal L_{\Pi_1}(P_{XA})
\]
and
\[
c_\epsilon\, r_\epsilon(n,m,d)\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d),
\]
where \(r_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) is the frontier rate in \cref{obj:def:frontier-rate-handle} and \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) is the minimax risk in \cref{obj:def:minimax-risk}.
\end{theoremv}

The converse gives the complementary half of the sample-size separation. Matching the induced \(P_{XA}\) law across the two priors makes the auxiliary records contribute through the marginal-frequency side of the construction; outcome-marked observations remain the resource that controls the \(1/n\) component.

Combining the attainable estimator with the common-marginal converse yields the sharp risk statement and its sequence consequences.

\begin{theoremv}{thm:sharp-annotation-frontier}[Sharp annotation frontier]
For every overlap parameter \(\epsilon\) satisfying \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), there exist constants \(c_\epsilon\) and \(C_\epsilon\), depending only on \(\epsilon\), with
\[
0<c_\epsilon\le C_\epsilon,
\]
such that the following assertions hold.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Finite-sample bracket.)} For every \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), and \(d\ge2\),
\[
c_\epsilon r_\epsilon(n,m,d)
\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d)
\le C_\epsilon r_\epsilon(n,m,d),
\]
where, with \(N=n+m\) and \(\ell_n=\log(en)\),
\[
r_\epsilon(n,m,d)
=
\min\!\left\{1,\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{N^2\ell_n^2}\right\}.
\]

\item \textbf{(Unannotated endpoint.)} For every \(n\ge1\) and every \(d\ge0\),
\[
r_\epsilon(n,0,d)
=
\min\!\left\{1,\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{n^2\log^2(en)}\right\}.
\]

\item \textbf{(Known-marginal limit.)} For every fixed \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge2\),
\[
\lim_{m\to\infty} R_\epsilon(n,m,d)
=
R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d).
\]

\item \textbf{(Sequence regimes.)} For any sequences \((m_n)_{n\ge1}\) and \((d_n)_{n\ge1}\) of nonnegative integers satisfying \(d_n\ge2\) for every \(n\ge1\), set \(N_n=n+m_n\) and \(\ell_n=\log(en)\). Then
\[
R_\epsilon(n,m_n,d_n)\to0
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\frac{d_n}{N_n\ell_n}\to0,
\]
\[
R_\epsilon(n,m_n,d_n)=O(n^{-1})
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
d_n=O\!\left(\frac{N_n\ell_n}{\sqrt n}\right),
\]
and
\[
R_\epsilon(n,m_n,d_n)
=
o\!\left(R_\epsilon(n,0,d_n)\right)
\]
if and only if both
\[
\frac{d_n}{\sqrt n\,\ell_n}\to\infty
\]
and
\[
\frac{d_n/(N_n\ell_n)}
{\min\{1,d_n/(n\ell_n)\}}
\to0.
\]
\end{itemize}
\end{theoremv}

\Cref{obj:thm:sharp-annotation-frontier} characterizes the minimax risk up to constants depending only on the overlap parameter \(\epsilon\). In its generated statement, the phrase \emph{unannotated endpoint} means \(m=0\), namely the labeled-only endpoint with no auxiliary outcome-unlabeled records. The consistency threshold compares the alphabet size with \(N_n\ell_n\); the parametric-rate threshold allows \(d_n\) to grow as \(N_n\ell_n/\sqrt n\); and the strict-improvement criterion identifies when auxiliary treatment--covariate records improve on that labeled-only endpoint. The theorem therefore gives a theoretical sample-size frontier for the two sources of information; its conservative calibrated witness is not an operational allocation rule.

The known-marginal bullet in \cref{obj:thm:sharp-annotation-frontier} is a summary pointer. The following formally primary statement records that limiting link with the \leanref{S-3}{known-treatment--covariate-marginal benchmark experiment} and gives the exact large-\(m\) endpoint for fixed labeled sample size and alphabet size.

\begin{theoremv}{thm:known-marginal-limit}[Known marginal limit]
Fix an overlap parameter \(\epsilon\), a labeled sample size \(n\), and a finite covariate alphabet size \(d\). Suppose
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Overlap.)} \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).
\item \textbf{(Labeled size.)} \(n\ge 1\).
\item \textbf{(Alphabet size.)} \(d\ge 2\).
\end{itemize}
Then the minimax mean-squared risk \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) from \cref{obj:def:minimax-risk} converges, as the auxiliary sample size \(m\to\infty\), to the known-marginal minimax risk \(R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d)\) from \cref{obj:def:known-marginal-risk}:
\[
\lim_{m\to\infty} R_\epsilon(n,m,d)
=
R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d).
\]
\end{theoremv}

The limit is the formal version of the marginal-information interpretation. As the auxiliary sample grows with \(n\) and \(d\) fixed, the treatment--covariate table is learned to the known-marginal benchmark, and the risk converges to the \(n^{-1}\) boundary in \cref{obj:thm:known-marginal-boundary}.


\section{The hybrid estimator}\label{sec:hybrid-estimator}

This section gives an algorithmic reading of the estimator used in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}. The construction separates three statistical roles that are intertwined in the target functional: the labeled records supply outcome marks, the combined treatment--covariate records estimate marginal cell frequencies, and the calibration chooses when a cell is treated by polynomial extrapolation rather than by direct inverse-count weighting.

The first step is deterministic sample splitting. The labeled sample is divided into an outcome block of size \(M_0\), a pilot contribution of size \(n_p\), and a factorial contribution of size \(n_f\). The auxiliary sample is divided into pilot and factorial contributions of sizes \(m_p\) and \(m_f\). Pooling the corresponding labeled and auxiliary treatment--covariate records gives pilot and factorial pools of sizes \(M_p\) and \(M_f\). This split makes the outcome counts independent of the treatment--covariate counts used to choose and evaluate the weights, matching the independence structure required by the risk analysis.

The finite calibration in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} then fixes the polynomial degree \(L\), the light-cell scale \(B\), and the pilot threshold \(k_0\). The predicate \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) is a computable list of inequalities involving these quantities, the dyadic overlap certificate \(\bar\epsilon\), and the prefix intensities \(u,t_p,t\). When the predicate holds, the selected scales keep the polynomial coefficients and Poisson tails within the finite-sample ranges used by the upper-bound argument. When it fails, the estimator is defined by the zero rule, which keeps the procedure total for every admissible \(n,m,d\).

The estimator uses Poisson-prefix derandomization to express an independent-Poisson statistic as a deterministic function of the fixed samples. For each triple of prefix lengths \((r_0,r_p,r_f)\), the rule keeps the first \(r_0\) observations in the outcome block, the first \(r_p\) observations in the pilot pool, and the first \(r_f\) observations in the factorial pool, then averages the resulting clipped statistic against the three Poisson probabilities. This finite sum is the conditional expectation of the randomized prefix statistic, so the implemented estimator remains an ordinary measurable statistic of the observed data.

For each covariate cell \(x\), the statistic forms three counts. The pilot count \(J_x\) decides the regime. The outcome counts \(S_{1x}\) and \(S_{0x}\) count labeled successes in the two treatment arms within the outcome prefix. The factorial counts \(K_{1x}\) and \(K_{0x}\) count treatment--covariate observations in the factorial prefix. Thus outcome information enters only through the success counts, while the larger annotated and unannotated treatment--covariate pool enters through \(J_x\) and \(K_{ax}\).

Cells with \(J_x\le k_0\) use the light-cell polynomial weight. The Chebyshev component \(T_L\) defines \(H_L\), its continuation \(E_L\), and the correction polynomial \(G_L\), giving the weight polynomial \(W_{a,L}\) in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}. Its factorial lift \(\widehat W_{a,L,x}\) replaces monomials in the unknown cell probabilities by falling-factorial count statistics. This applies the polynomial-approximation strategy used in sharp discrete functional estimation \citep{JiaoVenkatHanWeissman2015,WuYang2016} to the inverse propensity-type weights needed for the average treatment effect, with the rare-cell organization motivated by sample-amplification and unseen-species perspectives \citep{ValiantValiant2017,OrlitskySureshWu2016}.

Cells with \(J_x>k_0\) use the heavy-cell inverse-count rule. In those cells, the factorial prefix supplies enough treatment--covariate mass for the ratios \((K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1)/(K_{ax}+1)\) to act as stabilized inverse-count weights. The added ones keep the weights finite at every realized count configuration, while the pilot threshold routes cells with limited marginal information to the polynomial branch.

Finally, \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} sums the cell contributions and applies \(\operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]}\). The clipping matches the natural range of the average treatment effect functional and controls exceptional prefix realizations without changing the structure of the light- and heavy-cell rules. The resulting statistic, \leanref{sym:\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}}{\ensuremath{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}}}, is therefore a fully specified finite-sample estimator built from deterministic splitting, calibrated polynomial extrapolation, inverse-count stabilization, and bounded aggregation.

\Cref{obj:thm:uniform-mixed-upper} is the performance guarantee attached to this construction. Under the overlap model and product-sample experiment stated earlier, the calibrated estimator attains the rate in \cref{obj:def:frontier-rate-handle} uniformly over the model class. The two pieces of the rate correspond to the two resources used by the algorithm: labeled outcomes determine the \(1/n\) component, while treatment--covariate counts from both samples determine the marginal-frequency component through \(n+m\).

\paragraph{Proof roadmap.}
Poissonization first makes the outcome, pilot, and factorial counts independent. The light-cell argument uses Chebyshev approximation and unbiased factorial moments; the heavy-cell argument uses the exact stabilized inverse-count mean and moment bounds. Pilot tails control routing errors, and summing the cellwise bias and variance bounds yields the Poissonized risk. Prefix conditioning then transfers that bound to the fixed samples. For the converse, moment-matched common-marginal priors keep the auxiliary table indistinguishable while separating the ATE, and a separate binary submodel supplies the \(1/n\) label floor. The dependency map at the start of \cref{sec:appendix-upper} links these ideas to the detailed certificates.


\section{Interpretation and extensions}\label{sec:interpretation}

The main minimax theorem yields three interpretable sequence consequences. In the notation of \cref{obj:def:frontier-rate-handle}, the risk is consistent precisely along sequences for which the labeled sample grows and the treatment--covariate information is sufficiently rich relative to the alphabet size. The first component, \(1/n\), is the irreducible cost of observing binary outcomes; the second component, \(d^2/\{(n+m)^2\ell_n^2\}\), is the cost of learning enough about the treatment--covariate distribution to stabilize the inverse-probability structure of \cref{obj:def:ate-functional}. Thus the auxiliary sample improves risk through the marginal table rather than through direct outcome information.

The parametric regime is the portion of the experiment in which the marginal-frequency component is of order \(1/n\) or smaller. There the sharp rate in \cref{obj:thm:sharp-annotation-frontier} matches the labeled-outcome benchmark up to constants depending only on the overlap constant. In that range, the hybrid estimator of \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} uses the auxiliary treatment--covariate records to make the nuisance marginal problem small enough that the remaining mean-squared error is governed by outcome sampling.

A complementary regime is where annotation yields strict improvement over the supervised endpoint. With \(m=0\), the marginal table is learned only from the labeled sample, and the second term in \cref{obj:def:frontier-rate-handle} is \(d^2/(n^2\ell_n^2)\). Adding outcome-unlabeled treatment--covariate records replaces \(n\) by \(n+m\) in that component. Consequently, when the alphabet is large enough for the marginal-frequency term to matter and the auxiliary sample makes \(n+m\) substantially larger than \(n\), \cref{obj:thm:sharp-annotation-frontier} gives a quantitative risk reduction inside the same overlap model and target functional. This is the sense in which outcome annotation has value here: it converts unlabeled treatment--covariate information into a sharper stabilized estimate of the average treatment effect.

The hybrid rule in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} is calibrated to attain the logarithmic rate in \cref{obj:def:frontier-rate-handle}. A simpler comparison is obtained by using inverse-count stabilization everywhere, without the light-cell polynomial correction. The next definition records this baseline in the same sample-splitting and Poisson-prefix format, so that the comparison isolates the role of the polynomial branch. Every quotient in this definition uses totalized real division: when \(u=0\), division by \(u\) evaluates to zero. Consequently the clipped prefix statistic remains defined for \(n=1,2\), where \(M_0=u=0\).

\begin{definitionv}{synth_5}[Poisson-prefix inverse-count estimator]
For \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), and \(d\ge2\), put
\[
M_0=\lfloor n/3\rfloor,\qquad
n_p=\left\lfloor\frac{n-M_0}{2}\right\rfloor,\qquad
n_f=n-M_0-n_p,\qquad
m_p=\lfloor m/2\rfloor,\qquad
m_f=m-m_p,
\]
and \(u=M_0/8\). Given a labeled sample \(\mathcal L_n\) and an auxiliary sample \(\mathcal U_m\), define \(\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC,prefix}}_{n,m,d}(r_0,r_f)=0\) when \(r_0>M_0\) or \(r_f>n_f+m_f\). For \(0\le r_0\le M_0\) and \(0\le r_f\le n_f+m_f\), let \(S_{ax}(r_0)\) be the number of records among the first \(r_0\) labeled observations with \((X,A,Y)=(x,a,1)\). Let \(K_{ax}(r_f)\) be the number of records with \((X,A)=(x,a)\) in the factorial prefix formed from the last \(n_f\) labeled records and the last \(m_f\) auxiliary records, taking the first \(r_f\) records of that pooled factorial block in its fixed order. Define
\[
\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC,prefix}}_{n,m,d}(r_0,r_f)
=
\operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]}
\left[
\sum_{x=1}^d
\left\{
\frac{S_{1x}(r_0)}{u}
\frac{K_{0x}(r_f)+K_{1x}(r_f)+1}{K_{1x}(r_f)+1}
-
\frac{S_{0x}(r_0)}{u}
\frac{K_{0x}(r_f)+K_{1x}(r_f)+1}{K_{0x}(r_f)+1}
\right\}
\right].
\]
\end{definitionv}

The statistic in \cref{obj:synth_5} uses the outcome prefix to estimate treated and control success masses and the factorial prefix to form stabilized inverse-count weights. The added ones make the expression finite in every cell and for every realized prefix length. Averaging over the Poisson prefix lengths derandomizes the construction while preserving the same count structure.

\begin{theoremv}{thm:inverse-count-baseline}[Inverse-count baseline bound]
Fix an overlap constant \(\epsilon\) with \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).  Then there is a constant \(C_\epsilon>0\) such that, for every \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), and \(d\ge2\), the clipped derandomized inverse-count estimator defined for each sample \(Z=(\mathcal L_n,\mathcal U_m)\) by
\[
\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC}}_{n,m,d}(Z)
=
\sum_{r_0=0}^{M_0}
\sum_{r_f=0}^{n_f+m_f}
\mathbb P\{\operatorname{Poi}(M_0/8)=r_0\}
\mathbb P\{\operatorname{Poi}((n_f+m_f)/8)=r_f\}
\,\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC,prefix}}_{n,m,d}(Z;r_0,r_f)
\]
is measurable.  Here \(M_0=\lfloor n/3\rfloor\), \(n_p=\lfloor(n-M_0)/2\rfloor\), \(n_f=n-M_0-n_p\), \(m_p=\lfloor m/2\rfloor\), and \(m_f=m-m_p\) are the block sizes from \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}, and \(\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC,prefix}}_{n,m,d}(Z;r_0,r_f)\) is the sample-and-prefix-count statistic of \cref{obj:synth_5}.

Moreover,
\[
\sup_{P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}}
\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}}
\left[
\left\{
\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)
\right\}^2
\right]
\le
C_\epsilon
\min\left\{
1,\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2}
\right\}.
\]
Here \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) is the overlap class in \cref{obj:def:model-class}, \(\tau(P)\) is the average treatment effect functional in \cref{obj:def:ate-functional}, and the expectation is under the annotation experiment of \cref{obj:def:annotation-experiment}.  Consequently, for the minimax risk in \cref{obj:def:minimax-risk},
\[
R_\epsilon(n,m,d)
\le
C_\epsilon
\min\left\{
1,\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2}
\right\}.
\]
\end{theoremv}

\Cref{obj:thm:inverse-count-baseline} shows that direct inverse-count stabilization already converts auxiliary treatment--covariate observations into a risk bound with the same two-resource form as the sharp theorem: a labeled-outcome term and a marginal-frequency term. Its marginal term is \(d^2/(n+m)^2\), whereas \cref{obj:thm:sharp-annotation-frontier} attains the logarithmically improved \(d^2/\{(n+m)^2\ell_n^2\}\) rate through the calibrated light-cell polynomial component. The comparison identifies the gain supplied by the hybrid construction in sparse cells while keeping the same model, target, and product-sample experiment.

The general rate also has a simple specialization at the smallest nontrivial alphabet size. When the covariate alphabet is binary, the marginal-frequency component is uniformly absorbed into the labeled-outcome scale.

\begin{theoremv}{prop:binary-alphabet-parametric-rate}[Binary parametric rate]
Let \(0<\epsilon<1/2\). For the minimax risk \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) defined in \cref{obj:def:minimax-risk}, at binary alphabet size \(d=2\) there exist constants \(c_\epsilon,C_\epsilon\in\mathbb R\) with \(0<c_\epsilon\le C_\epsilon\) such that, for every integer \(n\ge1\) and every integer \(m\ge0\),
\[
\frac{c_\epsilon}{n}\le R_\epsilon(n,m,2)\le \frac{C_\epsilon}{n}.
\]
\end{theoremv}

\Cref{obj:prop:binary-alphabet-parametric-rate} gives the exact order of the minimax risk for \(d=2\): the experiment is parametric for every auxiliary sample size. The upper bound follows the same scale as the labeled-outcome term in the main theorem, while the lower bound records that binary outcomes still require order \(1/n\) mean-squared error uniformly over the overlap class. Thus, in the binary-alphabet case, auxiliary treatment--covariate records fit naturally into the constant factor rather than changing the rate.

The result complements semisupervised causal and surrogate-outcome analyses in which auxiliary observations improve estimation through nuisance-function, design-distribution, or surrogate-response learning \citep{ChengAnanthakrishnanCai2021,ChakraborttyDai2024,KallusMao2025}. Here the finite-alphabet structure makes that role explicit in minimax form: unlabeled treatment--covariate records sharpen the marginal component of the risk, and the sharp theorem characterizes exactly when that sharpening changes the rate.

\paragraph{Limitations and future work.}
The analysis treats independent random outcome labeling from a shared population, binary treatment and outcome, a known finite covariate alphabet, occupied-cell overlap, and unrestricted cellwise nuisance functions. These conditions define a transparent discrete benchmark in which the value of outcome annotation can be characterized exactly. The constants in the explicit calibration are deliberately proof-oriented rather than implementation-oriented: for example, already at degree \(L=2\), the stronger displayed polynomial-size condition \(L^6 7056^{2L}\le n\) requires \(n\) on the order of \(1.6\times10^{17}\). Thus the theorem establishes a finite-sample minimax rate and an explicit witness, but it does not recommend this conservative calibration as a practical estimator at ordinary sample sizes. Natural extensions include sharper implementable tuning, continuous or growing covariate representations, multi-valued treatments or outcomes, adaptive annotation designs, and structural restrictions on the cellwise outcome regressions or propensity scores.


\appendix

\section*{Appendices}

\section{Upper-bound ingredients}\label{sec:appendix-upper}

This appendix collects the analytic ingredients used by the upper bounds in \cref{obj:thm:uniform-mixed-upper} and by the inverse-count comparison in \cref{obj:thm:inverse-count-baseline}. The statements are organized around the two treatment--covariate masses that enter the estimators, the all-heavy inverse-count calculation, the polynomial certificate for light cells, and the fixed-sample transfer that connects the Poissonized construction to the annotation experiment.

The proof dependency map is as follows. The inverse-count moment lemmas feed the heavy-cell analysis; \cref{obj:synth_4,obj:lem:chebyshev-factorial-certificate,obj:lem:explicit-chebyshev-calibration} feed the light-cell analysis; the calibrated mean and second-moment bounds combine in \cref{obj:lem:poisson-hybrid-risk}; and \cref{obj:lem:fixed-sample-hybrid-transfer} converts that Poissonized bound into \cref{obj:thm:uniform-mixed-upper}. \Cref{sec:appendix-lower} independently supplies the common-marginal converse, after which the upper and lower bounds meet in \cref{obj:thm:sharp-annotation-frontier}. This map is the shortest reading path through the detailed certificates below.

The inverse-count analysis uses one scalar inequality to insert the marginal treatment--covariate intensity into the variance calculation. It is stated first because the later cellwise bounds apply it separately to each covariate cell and treatment arm.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:poisson-inverse-marginal-energy-scalar}[Marginal energy insertion]
Let \(u,p,w,\epsilon,\lambda_0,\lambda_1,q_0,q_1,r_{2,0},r_{2,1},r_{4,0},r_{4,1}\in\mathbb R\).  Suppose that
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Basic scales.)} \(u>0\), \(p\ge0\), \(0<\epsilon\le1\), and \(w\ge0\).
\item \textbf{(Outcome masses.)} \(0\le q_0\le p\) and \(0\le q_1\le p\).
\item \textbf{(Marginal intensities.)} For each \(a\in\{0,1\}\), \(\epsilon w\le\lambda_a\le w\).
\item \textbf{(Second-moment rates.)} \(r_{2,0},r_{2,1}\ge0\), \((1+\lambda_0)^2r_{2,0}\le8\), and \((1+\lambda_1)^2r_{2,1}\le8\).
\item \textbf{(Fourth-moment rates.)} \(r_{4,0},r_{4,1}\ge0\), \((1+\lambda_0)^4r_{4,0}\le96\), and \((1+\lambda_1)^4r_{4,1}\le96\).
\end{itemize}
Put
\[
c_{\mathrm{mar}}=8\epsilon^{-2}+96(\epsilon^{-2}+\epsilon^{-3}).
\]
Then
\[
\lambda_1\bigl\{(q_1u^{-1}+q_1^2)(\lambda_0+\lambda_0^2)r_{4,1}
 +(q_0u^{-1}+q_0^2)r_{2,0}\bigr\}
+\lambda_0\bigl\{(q_0u^{-1}+q_0^2)(\lambda_1+\lambda_1^2)r_{4,0}
 +(q_1u^{-1}+q_1^2)r_{2,1}\bigr\}
\le
(pu^{-1}+p^2)\frac{2c_{\mathrm{mar}}}{1+w}.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:poisson-inverse-marginal-energy-scalar}]
\begin{enumerate}
\item First derive the scalar bidirectional energy bound.  The lower and upper intensity bounds, together with \(0<\epsilon\le1\) and \(w\ge0\), imply
\[
0\le \lambda_0,\qquad 0\le \lambda_1,
\]
and, for the denominator attached to \(\lambda_1\),
\[
\epsilon(1+w)=\epsilon+\epsilon w\le 1+\lambda_1,
\qquad
1+w\le \epsilon^{-1}(1+\lambda_1),
\qquad
w\le \epsilon^{-1}(1+\lambda_1).
\]
Using also \(\lambda_0\le w\), we have
\[
(1+w)\lambda_0
   \le (1+w)w
   \le \epsilon^{-2}(1+\lambda_1)^2,
\]
and hence
\[
\lambda_0 r_{2,1}
   \le 8\,{\lambda_0\over (1+\lambda_1)^2}
   \le {8\epsilon^{-2}\over 1+w}.
\]
Similarly,
\[
(1+w)\lambda_1\lambda_0
 \le \epsilon^{-2}(1+\lambda_1)^4,
\qquad
(1+w)\lambda_1\lambda_0^2
 \le \epsilon^{-3}(1+\lambda_1)^4,
\]
so the fourth-moment rate gives
\[
\lambda_1(\lambda_0+\lambda_0^2)r_{4,1}
 \le
 {96(\epsilon^{-2}+\epsilon^{-3})\over 1+w}.
\]
Combining these two estimates yields
\[
\lambda_1(\lambda_0+\lambda_0^2)r_{4,1}
 +\lambda_0 r_{2,1}
 \le {c_{\mathrm{mar}}\over 1+w}.
\]
The same calculation with the roles of \(0\) and \(1\) interchanged gives
\[
\lambda_0(\lambda_1+\lambda_1^2)r_{4,0}
 +\lambda_1 r_{2,0}
 \le {c_{\mathrm{mar}}\over 1+w}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\bigl\{\lambda_1(\lambda_0+\lambda_0^2)r_{4,1}
 +\lambda_0 r_{2,1}\bigr\}
+
\bigl\{\lambda_0(\lambda_1+\lambda_1^2)r_{4,0}
 +\lambda_1 r_{2,0}\bigr\}
\le {2c_{\mathrm{mar}}\over 1+w}.
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.poisson_inverse_bidirectional_poincare_energy_scalar

\item Define the three real quantities
\[
\eta_0=q_0u^{-1}+q_0^2,\qquad
\eta_1=q_1u^{-1}+q_1^2,\qquad
\eta_p=pu^{-1}+p^2 .
\]
Since \(u>0\), we have \(u^{-1}\ge0\).  For each displayed outcome mass,
\[
\eta_p-\eta_0
  =(p-q_0)(u^{-1}+p+q_0)\ge0,
\qquad
\eta_p-\eta_1
  =(p-q_1)(u^{-1}+p+q_1)\ge0,
\]
because \(0\le q_0,q_1\le p\).  Thus
\[
\eta_0\le \eta_p,\qquad \eta_1\le \eta_p,
\qquad \eta_p\ge0.
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.poisson_inverse_marginal_energy_with_outcome_rates

\item All coefficients multiplying \(\eta_0\) and \(\eta_1\) below are nonnegative, by the nonnegativity of the intensities, the moment rates, and \(\lambda_a+\lambda_a^2\).  Hence the comparison from the previous step gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\lambda_1\bigl\{\eta_1(\lambda_0+\lambda_0^2)r_{4,1}
 +\eta_0 r_{2,0}\bigr\}
+\lambda_0\bigl\{\eta_0(\lambda_1+\lambda_1^2)r_{4,0}
 +\eta_1 r_{2,1}\bigr\} \\
&\qquad\le
\eta_p\Bigl[
\bigl\{\lambda_1(\lambda_0+\lambda_0^2)r_{4,1}
 +\lambda_0 r_{2,1}\bigr\}
+
\bigl\{\lambda_0(\lambda_1+\lambda_1^2)r_{4,0}
 +\lambda_1 r_{2,0}\bigr\}
\Bigr].
\end{aligned}
\]
Applying the scalar bidirectional bound from Step 1 and using \(\eta_p\ge0\), we obtain
\[
\lambda_1\bigl\{(q_1u^{-1}+q_1^2)(\lambda_0+\lambda_0^2)r_{4,1}
 +(q_0u^{-1}+q_0^2)r_{2,0}\bigr\}
+\lambda_0\bigl\{(q_0u^{-1}+q_0^2)(\lambda_1+\lambda_1^2)r_{4,0}
 +(q_1u^{-1}+q_1^2)r_{2,1}\bigr\}
\le
(pu^{-1}+p^2){2c_{\mathrm{mar}}\over 1+w}.
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.poisson_inverse_marginal_energy_with_outcome_rates
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:poisson-inverse-marginal-energy-scalar} converts arm-level inverse-count moment bounds into a covariate-cell contribution controlled by the total marginal scale \(w\). The overlap inequalities in the statement match the occupied-cell overlap structure of \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), while the final display has the form needed to sum over cells.

We next isolate the cell-level notation used throughout the appendix. The treatment--covariate mass records how often an arm appears in a covariate cell, while the outcome-marked mass records the same cell together with a successful binary outcome.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_2}[Cell arm mass]
For a law \(P\) on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\), a covariate cell \(x\in[d]\), and a treatment arm \(a\in\{0,1\}\), define the treatment-covariate cell mass
\[
s_{ax}
=
\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}} P(X=x,A=a,Y=y).
\]
\end{definitionv}

The mass \leanref{sym:s_{ax}}{\ensuremath{s_{ax}}} is the denominator scale for inverse treatment-cell weights. Under the overlap model in \cref{obj:def:model-class}, occupied covariate cells have both arm masses controlled by the same covariate mass, so inverse-count weights can be calibrated uniformly over the model class.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_1}[Outcome-marked cell mass]
For an observed-data law \(P\) on the finite record space \([d]\times\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}\), define the outcome-marked cell mass \(q_{ax}\) for \(x\in[d]\) and \(a\in\{0,1\}\) by
\[
q_{ax}
=
P(X=x,A=a,Y=1).
\]
Equivalently, \(q_{ax}\) is the probability mass assigned by \(P\) to the complete atom \((x,a,1)\).
\end{definitionv}

The outcome-marked mass \leanref{sym:q_{ax}}{\ensuremath{q_{ax}}} supplies the numerator scale. Combining \(q_{ax}\) with an inverse estimate of \(s_{ax}\) gives a cellwise contribution to the average treatment effect functional in \cref{obj:def:ate-functional}.

The simplest upper-bound component treats every cell as sufficiently observed and uses inverse counts throughout. This statistic is useful both as a baseline and as the heavy-cell part of the hybrid estimator.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:poisson-inverse-count-risk}[Poisson inverse-count risk]
Fix an overlap constant \(\epsilon\) with \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).  There is a constant \(C=C_\epsilon>0\) such that, for every \(d\), every \(u,t>0\), and every observed-data law \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) as in \cref{obj:def:model-class}, the following holds.

In the independent Poisson count experiment with independent counts
\[
S_{ax}\sim \operatorname{Poi}(u q_{ax}),
\qquad
K_{ax}\sim \operatorname{Poi}(t s_{ax}),
\qquad a\in\{0,1\},\ x\in[d],
\]
define
\[
Z^H
=
\sum_{x=1}^d
\left\{
\frac{S_{1x}}{u}\frac{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1}{K_{1x}+1}
-
\frac{S_{0x}}{u}\frac{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1}{K_{0x}+1}
\right\}.
\]
Writing \(\tau(P)\) for the average treatment effect functional in \cref{obj:def:ate-functional},
\[
\left|E_P Z^H-\tau(P)\right|
\le
C\min\{1,d/t\},
\qquad
\operatorname{Var}_P(Z^H)
\le
C\left(u^{-1}+t^{-1}\right).
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:poisson-inverse-count-risk}]
Fix \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).  Put
\[
c_{\mathrm{out}}:=1+10\epsilon^{-1}+8\epsilon^{-2},\qquad
c_{\mathrm{mar}}:=8\epsilon^{-2}+96(\epsilon^{-2}+\epsilon^{-3}),
\]
\[
c_{\mathrm{bias}}:=2\max\{1,(\epsilon e)^{-1}\},
\qquad
C_\epsilon:=c_{\mathrm{bias}}+c_{\mathrm{out}}+4c_{\mathrm{mar}}+1 .
\]
These constants are positive.  We prove the two asserted inequalities with
\(C=C_\epsilon\).  Fix \(d\), \(u,t>0\), and \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\).
For each cell write
\[
p_x:=\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}}P(x,a,y),
\qquad
s_{ax}:=\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}}P(x,a,y),
\qquad
q_{ax}:=P(x,a,1).
\]
Thus \(\sum_xp_x=1\), \(q_{ax}\le s_{ax}\le p_x\), and \(\sum_{x,a}q_{ax}\le1\).  By \cref{obj:def:model-class}, the overlap condition gives
\[
\epsilon p_x\le s_{ax}\le p_x,\qquad a\in\{0,1\}.
\]
% lean: poisson_inverse_count_risk

\begin{enumerate}
\item
For \(a\in\{0,1\}\), let \(\bar a\) denote the opposite arm and define the scalar arm mean
\[
m_{ax}:=
E\left[
{S_{ax}\over u}\,
{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1\over K_{ax}+1}
\right].
\]
Independence of the Poisson coordinates and linearity of expectation give
\[
E_P Z^H=\sum_x(m_{1x}-m_{0x}).
\]
Indeed, after expanding the summand defining \(Z^H\), each term is integrable by the polynomial growth bound
\[
\left|
{S_{ax}\over u}\,
{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1\over K_{ax}+1}
\right|
\le
|u^{-1}|(1+T)^2 ,
\]
where \(T\) is the total count over all displayed Poisson coordinates, and all polynomial moments of the finite product Poisson law are finite.  The product structure then reduces the expectation to the one-cell arm mean above. % lean: poisson_inverse_count_expectation_eq_arm_means

\item
For a Poisson variable \(J\) with mean \(\lambda\ge0\),
\[
E{1\over J+1}=
\begin{cases}
(1-e^{-\lambda})/\lambda,&\lambda>0,\\
1,&\lambda=0.
\end{cases}
\]
Define the arm outcome regression by
\[
\mu_{ax}:=
\begin{cases}
q_{ax}/s_{ax},& s_{ax}>0,\\
0,& s_{ax}=0.
\end{cases}
\]
Then \(q_{ax}=s_{ax}\mu_{ax}\) for all cells and arms, including zero-mass arms, and \(0\le \mu_{ax}\le1\).  Using \(E(S_{ax}/u)=q_{ax}\) and the preceding identity with
\(\lambda=ts_{ax}\), one obtains the continuous formula
\[
m_{ax}
=
p_x\mu_{ax}-(p_x-s_{ax})\mu_{ax}e^{-ts_{ax}}.
\]
Since \(0\le \mu_{ax}\le1\), \(p_x-s_{ax}\le p_x\), and \(s_{ax}\ge\epsilon p_x\),
\[
|m_{ax}-p_x\mu_{ax}|
\le p_x e^{-\epsilon t p_x}.
\]
Consequently, using \cref{obj:def:ate-functional},
\[
\left|E_P Z^H-\tau(P)\right|
\le
2\sum_x p_x e^{-\epsilon t p_x}.
\]
For every \(z\ge0\), \(z e^{-\epsilon tz}\le(\epsilon e t)^{-1}\), while
\(\sum_xp_x=1\).  Hence
\[
\left|E_P Z^H-\tau(P)\right|
\le
2\min\left\{1,{d\over \epsilon e t}\right\}.
\]
Writing \(\xi=d/t\), the elementary inequality
\[
2\min\{1,\xi/(\epsilon e)\}
\le
2\max\{1,(\epsilon e)^{-1}\}\min\{1,\xi\}
=
c_{\mathrm{bias}}\min\{1,d/t\}
\]
gives
\[
\left|E_P Z^H-\tau(P)\right|
\le C_\epsilon\min\{1,d/t\}.
\]
% lean: poisson_inverse_count_bias_of_expectation_eq

\item
We next bound the variance.  For a full count array \(\omega\), let
\(\Delta^S_{ax}F(\omega)\) be the change in \(F\) after adding one to \(S_{ax}\), and let
\(\Delta^K_{ax}F(\omega)\) be the change after adding one to \(K_{ax}\).  The finite-product Poisson add-one Poincare inequality gives, for \(F=Z^H\),
\[
\operatorname{Var}_P(Z^H)
\le
\sum_{x,a}
u q_{ax}E\{(\Delta^S_{ax}Z^H)^2\}
+
\sum_{x,a}
t s_{ax}E\{(\Delta^K_{ax}Z^H)^2\}.
\]
The square-integrability hypotheses required for this inequality follow from the same polynomial growth estimate used above, applied also to the add-one increments. % lean: nestedPairedPoisson_addOne_poincare

\item
For the outcome-count increments,
\[
\Delta^S_{ax}Z^H
=
\pm u^{-1}\left(1+{K_{\bar a x}\over K_{ax}+1}\right),
\]
where the sign is irrelevant after squaring.  If
\(\lambda=ts_{ax}\) and \(\nu=ts_{\bar a x}\), then \(\epsilon\nu\le\lambda\).  For independent
\(J\sim\operatorname{Poi}(\lambda)\) and \(V\sim\operatorname{Poi}(\nu)\), set
\[
r_1:=E{1\over J+1},
\qquad
r_2:=E{1\over (J+1)^2}.
\]
The Poisson reciprocal bounds give
\[
\lambda r_1\le1,
\qquad
(1+\lambda)^2r_2\le8,
\qquad
r_1\ge0,
\qquad
r_2\ge0.
\]
Moreover \(E V=\nu\) and \(E V^2\le \nu+\nu^2\).  Thus product-measure factorization gives
\[
E\left(1+{V\over J+1}\right)^2
\le
1+2\nu r_1+(\nu+\nu^2)r_2.
\]
The overlap comparison \(\epsilon\nu\le\lambda\), together with \(\epsilon>0\), implies \(\nu\le\epsilon^{-1}\lambda\).  Hence
\[
2\nu r_1\le 2\epsilon^{-1}\lambda r_1\le2\epsilon^{-1},
\]
and, using \(r_2\le 8(1+\lambda)^{-2}\),
\[
\nu r_2
\le
8\epsilon^{-1}{\lambda\over(1+\lambda)^2}
\le 8\epsilon^{-1},
\qquad
\nu^2r_2
\le
8\epsilon^{-2}{\lambda^2\over(1+\lambda)^2}
\le8\epsilon^{-2}.
\]
Consequently
\[
E\left(1+{V\over J+1}\right)^2
\le
1+10\epsilon^{-1}+8\epsilon^{-2}
=
c_{\mathrm{out}}.
\]
Multiplying by the rate \(uq_{ax}\) and summing yields
\[
\sum_{x,a}u q_{ax}E\{(\Delta^S_{ax}Z^H)^2\}
\le
c_{\mathrm{out}}u^{-1}\sum_{x,a}q_{ax}
\le
c_{\mathrm{out}}u^{-1}.
\]
% lean: poisson_inverse_outcome_coordinate_energy_bound

\item
It remains to control the marginal-count increments.  In one fixed cell, write
\[
\theta_{ax}:=ts_{ax},\qquad
\Theta_x:=tp_x,
\]
and, for \(\widetilde K_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(\theta_{ax})\), write
\[
\eta_{2,a}:=E{1\over (\widetilde K_{ax}+1)^2},\qquad
\eta_{4,a}:=E{1\over ((\widetilde K_{ax}+1)(\widetilde K_{ax}+2))^2}.
\]
The reciprocal estimates are
\[
\eta_{2,a}\ge0,
\qquad
(1+\theta_{ax})^2\eta_{2,a}\le8,
\qquad
\eta_{4,a}\ge0,
\qquad
(1+\theta_{ax})^4\eta_{4,a}\le96.
\]
For a full count array \(\omega\), define
\[
\operatorname{cr}_{ax}(\omega):=
{S_{ax}(\omega)\over u}{K_{\bar a x}(\omega)\over
(K_{ax}(\omega)+1)(K_{ax}(\omega)+2)},
\qquad
\operatorname{rc}_{ax}(\omega):=
{S_{ax}(\omega)\over u}{1\over K_{ax}(\omega)+1}.
\]
The add-one formulas for \(K_{ax}\) are
\[
\Delta^K_{1x}Z^H
=
-\{\operatorname{cr}_{1x}+\operatorname{rc}_{0x}\},
\qquad
\Delta^K_{0x}Z^H
=
