\operatorname{cr}_{0x}+\operatorname{rc}_{1x}.
\]
The two displayed components are nonnegative.  Hence
\[
\operatorname{cr}_{ax}\le |\Delta^K_{ax}Z^H|,
\qquad
\operatorname{rc}_{\bar a x}\le |\Delta^K_{ax}Z^H|,
\]
so the component squares are integrable because the add-one increment is square-integrable.  Applying \((r+s)^2\le2(r^2+s^2)\) to the preceding decomposition gives, for each \(a\),
\[
E\{(\Delta^K_{ax}Z^H)^2\}
\le
2\left(E\operatorname{cr}_{ax}^2+E\operatorname{rc}_{\bar a x}^2\right).
\]
Product-measure factorization gives
\[
E\operatorname{cr}_{ax}^2
=
E\left({S_{ax}\over u}\right)^2
\eta_{4,a}
E K_{\bar a x}^2,
\qquad
E\operatorname{rc}_{ax}^2
=
E\left({S_{ax}\over u}\right)^2\eta_{2,a}.
\]
The Poisson second-moment bounds are
\[
E\left({S_{ax}\over u}\right)^2
\le q_{ax}u^{-1}+q_{ax}^2,
\qquad
E K_{ax}^2\le \theta_{ax}+\theta_{ax}^2.
\]
Combining the last three displays yields
\[
E\{(\Delta^K_{ax}Z^H)^2\}
\le
2\left[
(q_{ax}u^{-1}+q_{ax}^2)(\theta_{\bar a x}+\theta_{\bar a x}^2)\eta_{4,a}
+
(q_{\bar a x}u^{-1}+q_{\bar a x}^2)\eta_{2,\bar a}
\right].
\]
Since \(q_{ax}\le p_x\) and \(u>0\),
\[
q_{ax}u^{-1}+q_{ax}^2\le p_xu^{-1}+p_x^2.
\]
The hypotheses of \cref{obj:lem:poisson-inverse-marginal-energy-scalar} hold after identifying
its generic cell mass and total intensity with
\[
p=p_x,
\qquad
w=\Theta_x,
\]
with its arm rates equal to \(\theta_{0x},\theta_{1x}\), its marked masses equal to
\(q_{0x},q_{1x}\), and its four reciprocal moments equal to
\((\eta_{2,0},\eta_{2,1},\eta_{4,0},\eta_{4,1})\).  These arm rates are simply
\(t s_{ax}\), which are nonnegative because \(t>0\) and \(s_{ax}\ge0\).  Also
\(p_x\ge0\), \(u>0\), \(0<\epsilon\le1\), and \(\Theta_x\ge0\); the outcome masses
satisfy \(0\le q_{ax}\le p_x\); overlap gives
\(\epsilon\Theta_x\le\theta_{ax}\), while \(s_{ax}\le p_x\) gives
\(\theta_{ax}\le\Theta_x\); and the displayed reciprocal estimates give the required
second- and fourth-moment rate conditions.  Therefore
\cref{obj:lem:poisson-inverse-marginal-energy-scalar} yields
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\theta_{1x}\bigl\{(q_{1x}u^{-1}+q_{1x}^2)(\theta_{0x}+\theta_{0x}^2)\eta_{4,1}
 +(q_{0x}u^{-1}+q_{0x}^2)\eta_{2,0}\bigr\} \\
&\quad+
\theta_{0x}\bigl\{(q_{0x}u^{-1}+q_{0x}^2)(\theta_{1x}+\theta_{1x}^2)\eta_{4,0}
 +(q_{1x}u^{-1}+q_{1x}^2)\eta_{2,1}\bigr\} \\
&\hspace{3cm}\le
(p_xu^{-1}+p_x^2){2c_{\mathrm{mar}}\over1+\Theta_x}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Multiplying the add-one-square bounds by their Poisson rates \(\theta_{ax}=ts_{ax}\), summing over \(a\), and using the preceding display gives the cell estimate
\[
\sum_a ts_{ax}E\{(\Delta^K_{ax}Z^H)^2\}
\le
4c_{\mathrm{mar}}\,
{p_xu^{-1}+p_x^2\over 1+tp_x}.
\]
% lean: poisson_inverse_marginal_cell_energy_bound

\item
Summing the preceding display over \(x\),
\[
\sum_{x,a}ts_{ax}E\{(\Delta^K_{ax}Z^H)^2\}
\le
4c_{\mathrm{mar}}
\sum_x{p_xu^{-1}+p_x^2\over 1+tp_x}.
\]
Since \(1+tp_x\ge1\) and \(p_xu^{-1}\ge0\),
\[
{p_xu^{-1}+p_x^2\over 1+tp_x}
\le
p_xu^{-1}+{p_x^2\over 1+tp_x}.
\]
Thus
\[
\sum_{x,a}ts_{ax}E\{(\Delta^K_{ax}Z^H)^2\}
\le
4c_{\mathrm{mar}}
\sum_x\left(p_xu^{-1}+{p_x^2\over 1+tp_x}\right).
\]
The first sum is \(u^{-1}\).  For the second, since \(t>0\) and \(p_x\ge0\),
\[
{p_x^2\over 1+tp_x}\le {p_x\over t},
\]
and hence
\[
\sum_x{p_x^2\over 1+tp_x}\le t^{-1}\sum_xp_x=t^{-1}.
\]
Thus
\[
\sum_{x,a}ts_{ax}E\{(\Delta^K_{ax}Z^H)^2\}
\le
4c_{\mathrm{mar}}(u^{-1}+t^{-1}).
\]
% lean: poisson_cell_variance_sum_bound

\item
Combining the Poisson Poincare bound with the outcome and marginal energy estimates,
\[
\operatorname{Var}_P(Z^H)
\le
c_{\mathrm{out}}u^{-1}+4c_{\mathrm{mar}}(u^{-1}+t^{-1}).
\]
Since \(u^{-1}\le u^{-1}+t^{-1}\) and
\(C_\epsilon=c_{\mathrm{bias}}+c_{\mathrm{out}}+4c_{\mathrm{mar}}+1\),
\[
\operatorname{Var}_P(Z^H)
\le
C_\epsilon(u^{-1}+t^{-1}).
\]
Together with the bias bound from Step 2, this proves the claim. % lean: poisson_inverse_count_risk
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:poisson-inverse-count-risk} separates the two costs of the all-heavy construction: the bias is governed by the number of cells relative to the treatment--covariate intensity, and the variance is governed by the outcome and count intensities. This is the parametric inverse-count behavior that becomes effective once the pilot stage classifies a cell as heavy.

To use this calculation with sampled prefixes, the Poisson record experiment is identified with the independent count experiment. The next two statements record the count law for two independent prefixes and then name the prefix distribution used by the inverse-count transfer.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:poisson-prefix-count-law}[Poisson prefix count law]
Let \(P\) be a probability law on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\), and let \(u,t\ge 0\).  Write
\[
q_{ax}=P(x,a,1),
\qquad
s_{ax}=P(x,a,0)+P(x,a,1).
\]
Under the prefix law that independently draws an outcome-marked prefix with \(\operatorname{Poi}(u)\) records from \(P\) and a treatment--covariate prefix with \(\operatorname{Poi}(t)\) records from the marginal \(P_{XA}\), define
\[
S_{ax}=\#\{\text{outcome-marked prefix records equal to }(x,a,1)\},
\qquad
K_{ax}=\#\{\text{treatment--covariate prefix records equal to }(x,a)\}.
\]
Then the pushforward of the prefix law under the map sending the two prefixes to the array \((S_{ax},K_{ax})_{x\in[d],\,a\in\{0,1\}}\) is the product measure
\[
\bigotimes_{x\in[d]}\bigotimes_{a\in\{0,1\}}
\left\{\operatorname{Poi}(u q_{ax})\otimes \operatorname{Poi}(t s_{ax})\right\}.
\]
Equivalently, the coordinates are mutually independent, with
\[
S_{ax}\sim \operatorname{Poi}(u q_{ax}),
\qquad
K_{ax}\sim \operatorname{Poi}(t s_{ax})
\]
for every \(x\in[d]\) and \(a\in\{0,1\}\).
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:poisson-prefix-count-law}]
Let \(\Omega_{\mathrm{obs}}=[d]\times\{0,1\}^2\) and
\(\Omega_{\mathrm{aux}}=[d]\times\{0,1\}\).  For a finite prefix
\(\xi=(z_1,\ldots,z_r)\) in a finite space \(\Omega\), write
\[
H_\xi(z)=\#\{i:z_i=z\},\qquad z\in\Omega .
\]
The elementary Poisson splitting identity says that if \(R\sim\operatorname{Poi}(\lambda)\)
and, conditionally on \(R\), \(z_1,\ldots,z_R\) are i.i.d. with law \(\nu\) on
\(\Omega\), then
\[
(H_\xi(z))_{z\in\Omega}\sim \bigotimes_{z\in\Omega}\operatorname{Poi}(\lambda\nu\{z\}).
\]
Indeed, for any nonnegative integers \((r_z)_{z\in\Omega}\), with
\(r_\bullet=\sum_z r_z\),
\[
\Pr\{H_\xi(z)=r_z\ \forall z\}
=e^{-\lambda}\frac{\lambda^{r_\bullet}}{r_\bullet!}
\frac{r_\bullet!}{\prod_z r_z!}\prod_z \nu\{z\}^{r_z}
=\prod_z e^{-\lambda\nu\{z\}}\frac{(\lambda\nu\{z\})^{r_z}}{r_z!}.
\]
Applied to the outcome-marked prefix with intensity \(u\) and the treatment--covariate
prefix with intensity \(t\), and using the independence of the two prefixes, this gives
\[
(H^{\mathrm{obs}}_z)_{z\in\Omega_{\mathrm{obs}}}
\sim
\bigotimes_{z\in\Omega_{\mathrm{obs}}}\operatorname{Poi}(uP\{z\}),
\qquad
(H^{\mathrm{aux}}_w)_{w\in\Omega_{\mathrm{aux}}}
\sim
\bigotimes_{w\in\Omega_{\mathrm{aux}}}\operatorname{Poi}(tP_{XA}\{w\}),
\]
with the two histogram arrays independent.  Mapping the first histogram to
\[
E(H^{\mathrm{obs}})(x,a)=H^{\mathrm{obs}}(x,a,1)
\]
selects one coordinate for each \((x,a)\).  Since the selected observed coordinates are
distinct, and the auxiliary histogram is independent of the observed histogram, the
array
\[
\bigl(E(H^{\mathrm{obs}})(x,a),H^{\mathrm{aux}}(x,a)\bigr)_{x\in[d],\,a\in\{0,1\}}
\]
has law
\[
\bigotimes_{x\in[d]}\bigotimes_{a\in\{0,1\}}
\left\{\operatorname{Poi}\bigl(uP\{(x,a,1)\}\bigr)
\otimes
\operatorname{Poi}\bigl(tP_{XA}\{(x,a)\}\bigr)\right\}.
\]
This array is exactly \((S_{ax},K_{ax})_{x,a}\).  In the degenerate case
\([d]=\varnothing\), the same display is the empty product measure on the one-point
array space. % lean: finitePrefixCounts_map_independentPoissonPrefixLaw

It remains to identify the two singleton masses in the displayed product.  By the
definition of \(q_{ax}\),
\[
P\{(x,a,1)\}=P(x,a,1)=q_{ax}.
\]
Hence the first Poisson intensity is \(u q_{ax}\). % lean: finitePrefixCounts_map_eq_poissonCountLaw

For the auxiliary marginal, \(P_{XA}\) is the pushforward of \(P\) under
\((x,a,y)\mapsto(x,a)\).  Therefore
\[
P_{XA}\{(x,a)\}
=\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}}P(x,a,y)
=P(x,a,0)+P(x,a,1)
=s_{ax}.
\]
Thus the second Poisson intensity is \(t s_{ax}\).  Since \(u,t\ge0\), these products
are the corresponding nonnegative Poisson parameters, and substituting the two
identities into the preceding product law yields
\[
\bigotimes_{x\in[d]}\bigotimes_{a\in\{0,1\}}
\left\{\operatorname{Poi}(u q_{ax})\otimes
\operatorname{Poi}(t s_{ax})\right\}.
\]
The product representation is equivalent to mutual independence of all coordinates
and to the stated marginal laws for every \(x\) and \(a\). % lean: finitePrefixCounts_map_eq_poissonCountLaw
\end{proof}

\begin{definitionv}{synth_19}[Poisson-prefix law]
For \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) and intensities \(u,t\ge0\), let \(\mathbb P^{\mathrm{pref}}_{P,u,t}\) denote the following probability law. Draw two prefix lengths independently,
\[
R_0\sim \operatorname{Poi}(u),\qquad R_f\sim \operatorname{Poi}(t).
\]
Conditional on \((R_0,R_f)=(r_0,r_f)\), draw an outcome prefix
\[
((X_i,A_i,Y_i))_{i=1}^{r_0}\sim P^{\otimes r_0}
\]
and, independently, a factorial treatment--covariate prefix
\[
((\widetilde X_j,\widetilde A_j))_{j=1}^{r_f}\sim P_{XA}^{\otimes r_f}.
\]
Thus the two prefixes are independent, the outcome prefix has complete-record sampling law \(P\), and the factorial prefix has treatment--covariate sampling law \(P_{XA}\). Under \(\mathbb P^{\mathrm{pref}}_{P,u,t}\), the associated counts
\[
S_{ax}=\sum_{i=1}^{R_0}\mathbf 1\{X_i=x,A_i=a,Y_i=1\},
\qquad
K_{ax}=\sum_{j=1}^{R_f}\mathbf 1\{\widetilde X_j=x,\widetilde A_j=a\}
\]
are independent across the two prefixes, with
\[
S_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(u q_{ax}),\qquad
K_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t s_{ax}).
\]
\end{definitionv}

\Cref{obj:lem:poisson-prefix-count-law} and \cref{obj:synth_19} justify applying the count-risk calculation directly to Poisson prefixes of the original records. The prefix representation is convenient for derandomization because every admissible prefix length is a finite statistic of the fixed sample pools.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:inverse-count-poisson-prefix-transfer}[Poisson prefix transfer]
Let \(n,m,d\) be nonnegative integers, let \(\epsilon\in\mathbb R\), and let \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) as in \cref{obj:def:model-class}.  Define
\[
M_0=\bigl\lfloor n/3\bigr\rfloor,\qquad
n_p=\bigl\lfloor (n-M_0)/2\bigr\rfloor,\qquad
n_f=n-M_0-n_p,\qquad
m_f=m-\bigl\lfloor m/2\bigr\rfloor,
\]
and set \(u=M_0/8\) and \(t=(n_f+m_f)/8\).  Let \(\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC}}_{n,m,d}\) be the clipped derandomized inverse-count estimator obtained by averaging, over \(0\le r_0\le M_0\) and \(0\le r_f\le n_f+m_f\), the finite-prefix inverse-count output with weights
\[
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(u)=r_0\}\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t)=r_f\}.
\]
Let \(\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC,pref}}_{u,t}\) be the clipped inverse-count statistic computed from two independent Poisson prefixes: an outcome-marked prefix with law induced by \(P\) and length \(\operatorname{Poi}(u)\), and a treatment--covariate prefix with law \(P_{XA}\) and length \(\operatorname{Poi}(t)\).  Then, for the annotation experiment in \cref{obj:def:annotation-experiment} and the ATE functional \(\tau(P)\) in \cref{obj:def:ate-functional},
\[
E_{P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}}
\!\left[
  \bigl(\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\bigr)^2
\right]
\le
E_{\mathbb P^{\mathrm{pref}}_{P,u,t}}
\!\left[
  \bigl(\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC,pref}}_{u,t}-\tau(P)\bigr)^2
\right]
+4\Bigl[
  \Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(u)>M_0\}
  +\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t)>n_f+m_f\}
\Bigr].
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:inverse-count-poisson-prefix-transfer}]
Put
\[
M_f:=n_f+m_f,\qquad m_p:=\lfloor m/2\rfloor .
\]
All lists below use the empty-list convention when one of the displayed lengths is zero.

\begin{enumerate}
\item
For finite prefixes \(\omega_0\) of outcome-marked observations and \(\omega_f\) of treatment--covariate observations, write
\[
\Phi_u(\omega_0,\omega_f)
:=
\text{the clipped finite-prefix inverse-count output at scale }u
\]
so that, by clipping,
\[
-1\le \Phi_u(\omega_0,\omega_f)\le 1
\qquad\text{for every }(\omega_0,\omega_f).
\]
Also,
\[
|\tau(P)|
=
\left|\sum_{x=1}^{d}p_x(\mu_{1x}-\mu_{0x})\right|
\le
\sum_{x=1}^{d}p_x|\mu_{1x}-\mu_{0x}|
\le
\sum_{x=1}^{d}p_x
=1,
\]
using \cref{obj:def:ate-functional}, the fact that \(P\) is a probability law in \cref{obj:def:model-class}, and \(\mu_{ax}\in[0,1]\), with the standard zero-mass-cell convention.

For fixed pools \(\mathbf o=(o_1,\ldots,o_{M_0})\) and \(\mathbf v=(v_1,\ldots,v_{M_f})\), define the capped prefix value
\[
\mathcal C(\mathbf o,\mathbf v,r_0,r_f)
:=
\begin{cases}
\Phi_u\bigl((o_1,\ldots,o_{r_0}),(v_1,\ldots,v_{r_f})\bigr),
& r_0\le M_0,\ r_f\le M_f,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\]
Let \(R_0\sim\operatorname{Poi}(u)\) and \(R_f\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t)\) be independent, and set
\[
\mathcal R_{u,t}(\mathbf o,\mathbf v)
:=
\mathbb E\!\left[\mathcal C(\mathbf o,\mathbf v,R_0,R_f)\right].
\]
If \((\mathbf O,\mathbf V)\sim P^{\otimes M_0}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes M_f}\), Jensen's inequality for the conditional expectation over \((R_0,R_f)\) gives
\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
  \bigl(\mathcal R_{u,t}(\mathbf O,\mathbf V)-\tau(P)\bigr)^2
\right]
\le
\mathbb E\!\left[
  \bigl(\mathcal C(\mathbf O,\mathbf V,R_0,R_f)-\tau(P)\bigr)^2
\right].
\]
On the event \(\Omega_{\mathrm{fit}}=\{R_0\le M_0,\ R_f\le M_f\}\), the selected prefixes have the same law as the independent Poisson prefixes restricted to their lengths fitting in the two pools. Hence
\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
  \bigl(\mathcal C-\tau(P)\bigr)^2\mathbf 1_{\Omega_{\mathrm{fit}}}
\right]
\le
\mathbb E_{\mathbb P^{\mathrm{pref}}_{P,u,t}}\!\left[
  \bigl(\Phi_u-\tau(P)\bigr)^2
\right].
\]
On \(\Omega_{\mathrm{fit}}^c\), the capped value is \(0\), and the interval bound above gives
\[
\bigl(0-\tau(P)\bigr)^2\le (1-(-1))^2=4.
\]
The union bound gives
\[
\Pr(\Omega_{\mathrm{fit}}^c)
\le
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(u)>M_0\}
+
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t)>M_f\}.
\]
Combining these displays,
\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
  \bigl(\mathcal R_{u,t}(\mathbf O,\mathbf V)-\tau(P)\bigr)^2
\right]
\le
\mathbb E_{\mathbb P^{\mathrm{pref}}_{P,u,t}}\!\left[
  \bigl(\Phi_u-\tau(P)\bigr)^2
\right]
+4\Bigl[
  \Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(u)>M_0\}
  +
  \Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t)>M_f\}
\Bigr].
\]
% lean: Causalean.Stat.FiniteRaoBlackwell.IndependentPoissonPrefix.finiteAlphabet_independentUnequalPrefix_risk_le

\item
For a full sample \(z=((O_1,\ldots,O_n),(V_1,\ldots,V_m))\), with \(O_i=(X_i,A_i,Y_i)\) and \(V_j\in[d]\times\{0,1\}\), define the fixed-pool projection
\[
\Psi(z)
:=
\Bigl(
  (O_1,\ldots,O_{M_0}),
  \bigl((X_{M_0+n_p+1},A_{M_0+n_p+1}),\ldots,(X_n,A_n),
        V_{m_p+1},\ldots,V_m\bigr)
\Bigr).
\]
For every \(z\),
\[
\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC}}_{n,m,d}(z)
=
\mathcal R_{u,t}(\Psi(z)).
\]
Indeed, expanding \(\mathcal R_{u,t}\) gives
\[
\sum_{r_0,r_f\ge0}
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(u)=r_0\}
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t)=r_f\}
\mathcal C(\Psi(z),r_0,r_f),
\]
and the terms with \(r_0>M_0\) or \(r_f>M_f\) are zero, leaving exactly the finite average over \(0\le r_0\le M_0\) and \(0\le r_f\le M_f\) in the derandomized inverse-count estimator.
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.independentPrefixInverseCountStatistic_fixedPools

\item
Under the annotation experiment in \cref{obj:def:annotation-experiment},
\[
\Psi_{\#}\bigl(P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}\bigr)
=
P^{\otimes M_0}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes M_f}.
\]
The first component selects the first \(M_0\) labeled records. The second component concatenates the treatment--covariate projections of the final \(n_f\) labeled records with the final \(m_f\) auxiliary records; independence of the original product samples and the definition of \(P_{XA}\) give the displayed product law. Therefore the change of variables formula yields
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}}
 \!\left[
  \bigl(\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\bigr)^2
 \right]  \\
&\qquad =
\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes M_0}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes M_f}}
 \!\left[
  \bigl(\mathcal R_{u,t}-\tau(P)\bigr)^2
 \right].
\end{aligned}
\]
Measurability is immediate because all sample spaces involved are finite products and the statistic is clipped.
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.inverseCountFixedPools_map_annotationLaw

\item
Applying the transfer bound from the first step to the fixed-pool risk in the last display gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}}
 \!\left[
  \bigl(\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\bigr)^2
 \right] \\
&\qquad\le
\mathbb E_{\mathbb P^{\mathrm{pref}}_{P,u,t}}
 \!\left[
  \bigl(\Phi_u-\tau(P)\bigr)^2
 \right]
+4\Bigl[
  \Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(u)>M_0\}
  +
  \Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t)>M_f\}
\Bigr].
\end{aligned}
\]
Under \(\mathbb P^{\mathrm{pref}}_{P,u,t}\), the random value \(\Phi_u\) is precisely
\(\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{IC,pref}}_{u,t}\), and \(M_f=n_f+m_f\). This is the claimed inequality.
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.inverseCountEstimator_risk_le_poissonPrefixes
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:inverse-count-poisson-prefix-transfer} converts the Poisson-prefix risk into a fixed-sample inverse-count estimator, with the excess risk controlled by the two prefix-overflow probabilities. This is the transfer step behind the inverse-count comparison in \cref{obj:thm:inverse-count-baseline}.

For light cells, the hybrid estimator replaces reciprocal weights by polynomial weights. The next definition records the factorial-moment lift used to turn the polynomial into an unbiased Poisson statistic, following the same polynomial-estimation principle used in discrete functional estimation \citep{JiaoVenkatHanWeissman2015,WuYang2016,ValiantValiant2017,OrlitskySureshWu2016}.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_4}[Factorial lift of the light-cell polynomial]
Fix \(a\in\{0,1\}\), an integer \(L\ge2\), and constants \(B,t>0\). Let
\[
W_{a,L}(s_0,s_1)
=
\frac{s_0+s_1}{B}G_L(s_a/B)
=
\sum_{r_0,r_1\ge0}c_{a,r_0,r_1}s_0^{r_0}s_1^{r_1},
\]
where \(G_L\) is the Chebyshev correction polynomial and only finitely many coefficients \(c_{a,r_0,r_1}\) are nonzero. For integer counts \(K_0,K_1\ge0\), define the factorial lift
\[
\widehat W_{a,L}(K_0,K_1)
=
\sum_{r_0,r_1\ge0}
c_{a,r_0,r_1}
\frac{(K_0)_{r_0}(K_1)_{r_1}}{t^{r_0+r_1}},
\]
with \((k)_r=k(k-1)\cdots(k-r+1)\) and \((k)_0=1\). Thus each monomial of \(W_{a,L}\) is replaced by the corresponding unbiased Poisson factorial-moment statistic.
\end{definitionv}

The lift in \cref{obj:synth_4} is tailored to independent Poisson counts: each falling factorial has expectation equal to the corresponding power of the intensity. The remaining task is to control the polynomial and the second moment of its lift uniformly over the light-cell range.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:chebyshev-factorial-certificate}[Chebyshev factorial certificate]
There is a universal constant \(A>1\) such that, for every integer \(L\ge2\), the polynomial continuations
\[
E_L(z)=H_L(z)/z,
\qquad
G_L(z)=\{1-E_L(z)\}/z
\]
satisfy the following properties:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Degree.)} The polynomial \(G_L\) has degree at most \(L-2\).
\item \textbf{(Chebyshev certificate.)} For every \(z\in[0,1]\),
\[
0\le E_L(z)\le 1,
\qquad
zG_L(z)+E_L(z)=1,
\]
and, whenever \(z>0\),
\[
E_L(z)\le (L^2z)^{-1}.
\]
\item \textbf{(Factorial-lift moment.)} For every \(a\in\{0,1\}\) and every \(t,B,s_0,s_1\in\mathbb R\) with
\[
t>0,\qquad B>0,\qquad s_0\ge0,\qquad s_1\ge0,\qquad L\le tB,
\]
if \(K_0\sim\operatorname{Poi}(ts_0)\) and \(K_1\sim\operatorname{Poi}(ts_1)\) are independent, then the factorial lift \(\widehat W_{a,L}\) satisfies
\[
\mathbb E\!\left[\widehat W_{a,L}(K_0,K_1)^2\right]
\le
A^L\left(1+\frac{s_0+s_1}{B}\right)^{2L}.
\]
Moreover, if \(s_0+s_1\le B\), then
\[
\mathbb E\!\left[\widehat W_{a,L}(K_0,K_1)^2\right]\le A^L.
\]
\end{itemize}
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:chebyshev-factorial-certificate}]
Choose \(A=7056\), so \(A>1\).

1. Fix an integer \(L\ge2\).  Write
\[
Q_L(z)=T_L(1-2z),
\qquad
H_L(z)=\frac{1-Q_L(z)}{2L^2},
\qquad
E_L(z)=H_L(z)/z,
\qquad
G_L(z)=\{1-E_L(z)\}/z .
\]
Since \(T_L\) has degree \(L\) and \(1-2z\) has degree at most \(1\),
\[
\deg Q_L\le L .
\]
Multiplication by the scalar \((2L^2)^{-1}\) does not increase degree, and subtraction from \(1\) gives
\[
\deg H_L
\le \deg(1-Q_L)
\le \max\{0,\deg Q_L\}
\le L .
\]
Because \(E_L\) is the quotient of \(H_L\) by \(z\), its degree is at most \(L-1\).  Hence
\[
\deg(1-E_L)\le \max\{0,\deg E_L\}\le L-1,
\]
using \(L\ge2\).  Dividing once more by \(z\) gives
\[
\deg G_L\le L-2 .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.chebyshev_factorial_certificate

2. By \cref{obj:lem:explicit-chebyshev-calibration}, for every \(z\in[0,1]\),
\[
0\le E_L(z)\le1,
\qquad
zG_L(z)+E_L(z)=1,
\]
and, for \(z>0\),
\[
E_L(z)\le \frac{1}{L^2z}.
\]
These are exactly the Chebyshev-certificate assertions. 
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.explicit_chebyshev_calibration

3. It remains to check the moment assertions with the same constant.  Let
\(a\in\{0,1\}\), \(t>0\), \(B>0\), \(s_0,s_1\ge0\), and \(L\le tB\), and let
\(K_0\sim\operatorname{Poi}(ts_0)\) and \(K_1\sim\operatorname{Poi}(ts_1)\) be independent.  Applying \cref{obj:lem:explicit-chebyshev-calibration} with \(p=s_0+s_1\) gives
\[
\mathbb E\!\left[\widehat W_{a,L}(K_0,K_1)^2\right]
\le
7056^L\left(1+\frac{s_0+s_1}{B}\right)^{2L}.
\]
If in addition \(s_0+s_1\le B\), the same calibration yields
\[
\mathbb E\!\left[\widehat W_{a,L}(K_0,K_1)^2\right]
\le
7056^L .
\]
Since \(A=7056\), these are the two required factorial-lift moment bounds. 
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.chebyshev_factorial_certificate
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:chebyshev-factorial-certificate} provides the approximation identity and moment growth bound needed for the light-cell part of the hybrid statistic. The identity \(zG_L(z)+E_L(z)=1\) expresses the reciprocal-weight correction, and the displayed decay of \(E_L\) quantifies the approximation error at positive mass. The factorial-lift moment bound then keeps the variance compatible with the polynomial degree used in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}.

The hybrid mean analysis separates the deterministic light-cell approximation error from the pilot-classification error on heavy cells. The next statements give the corresponding cell-summed bounds and the envelope calculation that puts them on the target rate scale.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:calibrated-envelope-absorption}[Calibrated envelope absorption]
Let \(\epsilon>0\) and \(C_0>0\). There is a constant \(C>0\), depending on \(\epsilon\) and \(C_0\), such that the following holds.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Sample sizes.)} \(n,m,d\) are integers with \(n\ge 1\), \(m\ge 0\), and \(d\ge 2\).
\item \textbf{(Calibration.)} The finite calibration predicate \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) from \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} holds.
\item \textbf{(Estimator scales.)} With the block sizes and scales from \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle},
\[
M_0=\lfloor n/3\rfloor,\qquad
n_p=\lfloor (n-M_0)/2\rfloor,\qquad
n_f=n-M_0-n_p,\qquad
m_f=m-\lfloor m/2\rfloor,
\]
and
\[
u=M_0/8,\qquad
t=(n_f+m_f)/8,\qquad
L=L(n),\qquad
B=B(n,m,\epsilon),\qquad
A_\star=7056.
\]
Let \(N=n+m\) and \(\ell_n=\log(en)\), as in \cref{obj:def:frontier-rate-handle}.
\end{itemize}

Then
\[
4A_\star^L
\left\{
\frac{\min\{1,dB\}}{u}+dB^2
\right\}
+C_0\left(u^{-1}+t^{-1}\right)
+\frac{8dB^2}{\epsilon^2L^4}
+\frac{4}{\epsilon t}
+414\,n^{-12}
+\left(
\frac{2dB}{\epsilon L^2}
+6n^{-12}
\right)^2
\le
C\left(
\frac{1}{n}
+\frac{d^2}{N^2\ell_n^2}
\right).
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:calibrated-envelope-absorption}]
\begin{enumerate}
\item Let \(H_\epsilon\) be the calibration constant appearing in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}, viewed as a real number, and set
\[
K_\epsilon(C_0)
=
4096H_\epsilon 512^2+4096H_\epsilon^2 512+64C_0
+\frac{8192H_\epsilon^2 512^2}{\epsilon^2}
+\frac{128}{\epsilon}+414
+2\left(\frac{64H_\epsilon 512}{\epsilon}\right)^2+72 .
\]
We take
\[
C=1+K_\epsilon(C_0).
\]
Since \(\epsilon>0\), \(C_0>0\), and \(H_\epsilon\ge0\), every displayed summand in \(K_\epsilon(C_0)\) is nonnegative except for none, and hence \(C>0\). % lean: calibrated_envelope_absorption_exists

\item Fix \(n,m,d\in\mathbb N\) with \(n\ge1\), \(d\ge2\), and \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\). Write
\[
N=n+m,\qquad \ell_n=\log(en),\qquad A=A_\star^L,\qquad \eta=n^{-12},
\]
where \(L=L(n)\), and define the comparison scale
\[
\Xi
=
\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{N^2\ell_n^2}.
\]
All quantities in the displayed inequalities below are understood as real numbers. The sample-size assumptions give
\[
1\le n,\qquad n\le N,\qquad 1\le d.
\]
Moreover,
\[
\ell_n=\log(en)=1+\log n\ge1,
\]
because \(n\ge1\). From \(L\ge2\) in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}, \(L>0\), and \(A=A_\star^L\ge0\). The calibration inequalities also give \(u>0\), \(t_p>0\), \(t>0\), and \(B>0\): indeed \(u/n\ge1/32\), \(t_p/N\ge1/32\), \(t/N\ge1/32\), and \(8\le t_pB\). % lean: calibrationPredicate_positive_parameters, Ldeg_ge_two

\item The block-proportion part of \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) supplies
\[
\frac{n}{32}\le u,
\qquad
\frac{N}{32}\le t .
\]
The scale definition \(B=H_\epsilon L/\min\{t_p,t\}\), together with
\[
\frac{N}{32}\le t_p,\qquad \frac{N}{32}\le t,
\]
gives
\[
B
=
\frac{H_\epsilon L}{\min\{t_p,t\}}
\le
\frac{32H_\epsilon L}{N}.
\]
The same calculation with the upper block bounds \(t_p\le3N/32\) and \(t\le3N/32\) yields the full auxiliary estimate
\[
\frac{32H_\epsilon L}{3N}\le B\le \frac{32H_\epsilon L}{N}.
\]
Only the upper bound is used below. % lean: Bscale_bounds

\item Let \(b_n\) be the binary-length calibration quantity from \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}. The logarithmic estimate gives
\[
\ell_n\le b_n.
\]
The degree calibration inequality is
\[
\frac{c^\circ b_n}{2}\le L,
\qquad c^\circ=\frac1{256},
\]
so \(b_n\le512L\). Therefore
\[
\ell_n\le512L.
\]
The polynomial-size condition \(L^6A_\star^{2L}\le n\) gives
\[
(L^3A_\star^L)^2=L^6A_\star^{2L}\le n.
\]
Finally, since \(n\ge1\),
\[
0\le \eta=n^{-12}\le \frac1n .
\]
% lean: logEN_le_binLen, moment_tests_square_forms, calibrated_envelope_absorption_exists

\item Put
\[
\Theta=L^3A=L^3A_\star^L.
\]
From \(\Theta^2\le n\), the elementary inequality \((x-y)^2\ge0\), applied with
\[
x=\frac{\Theta}{n},
\qquad
y=\frac{d}{N\ell_n},
\]
gives
\[
\frac{\Theta d}{Nn\ell_n}
\le
\frac12\left\{\left(\frac{\Theta}{n}\right)^2
+\left(\frac{d}{N\ell_n}\right)^2\right\}
\le
\frac12\left(\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{N^2\ell_n^2}\right)
=
\frac{\Xi}{2}.
\]
Also, from \(\ell_n\le512L\) and positivity of \(\ell_n,L\),
\[
\frac1L\le\frac{512}{\ell_n},
\qquad
\frac1{L^2}\le\frac{512^2}{\ell_n^2}.
\]
% lean: normalized_young, inverse_schedule, inverse_square_schedule

\item The reciprocal block bounds are
\[
u^{-1}\le\frac{32}{n},
\qquad
t^{-1}\le\frac{32}{n},
\]
because \(u\ge n/32\) and \(t\ge N/32\ge n/32\). Hence
\[
C_0(u^{-1}+t^{-1})
\le
64C_0\frac1n,
\]
\[
\frac4{\epsilon t}
=
\frac4\epsilon\,t^{-1}
\le
\frac{128}{\epsilon}\frac1n,
\]
and
\[
414\eta\le414\frac1n.
\]
Since \(1/n\le\Xi\), these estimates combine to
\[
C_0(u^{-1}+t^{-1})+\frac4{\epsilon t}+414\eta
\le
\left(64C_0+\frac{128}{\epsilon}+414\right)\Xi .
\]
% lean: hybrid_deterministic_envelope_le

\item The two core normalized products obey
\[
\frac{\Theta d}{NnL^2}
\le
\frac{\Theta d}{Nn}\frac{512^2}{\ell_n^2}
\le
512^2\frac{\Theta d}{Nn\ell_n}
\le
512^2\frac{\Xi}{2},
\]
where the middle step uses \(\ell_n\ge1\), and
\[
\frac{\Theta d}{N^2L}
\le
\frac{\Theta d}{N^2}\frac{512}{\ell_n}
\le
512\frac{\Theta d}{Nn\ell_n}
\le
512\frac{\Xi}{2},
\]
where \(N\ge n\) is used in the second inequality. % lean: hybrid_deterministic_envelope_le

\item The first light-envelope term satisfies
\[
4A\frac{\min\{1,dB\}}{u}
\le
4A\frac{dB}{u}
\le
4A\frac{d(32H_\epsilon L/N)}{n/32}
=
4096H_\epsilon\frac{\Theta d}{NnL^2}
\le
(4096H_\epsilon512^2)\frac{\Xi}{2}.
\]
The second light-envelope term satisfies
\[
4AdB^2
\le
4Ad\left(\frac{32H_\epsilon L}{N}\right)^2
=
4096H_\epsilon^2\frac{\Theta d}{N^2L}
\le
(4096H_\epsilon^2 512)\frac{\Xi}{2}.
\]
Adding the two bounds and using \(\Xi\ge0\) gives
\[
4A\left\{\frac{\min\{1,dB\}}u+dB^2\right\}
\le
(4096H_\epsilon512^2+4096H_\epsilon^2 512)\Xi .
\]
% lean: hybrid_deterministic_envelope_le

\item Since \(d\ge1\),
\[
d\le d^2.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\frac{8dB^2}{\epsilon^2L^4}
\le
\frac{8d}{\epsilon^2L^4}
\left(\frac{32H_\epsilon L}{N}\right)^2
=
\frac{8192H_\epsilon^2}{\epsilon^2}
\frac{d}{N^2L^2}.
\]
Using \(1/L^2\le512^2/\ell_n^2\) and \(d\le d^2\),
\[
\frac{d}{N^2L^2}
\le
\frac{512^2d^2}{N^2\ell_n^2}.
\]
Because \(d^2/(N^2\ell_n^2)\le\Xi\), we obtain
\[
\frac{8dB^2}{\epsilon^2L^4}
\le
\frac{8192H_\epsilon^2 512^2}{\epsilon^2}\,\Xi .
\]
% lean: hybrid_deterministic_envelope_le

\item For the squared bias term,
\[
\frac{2dB}{\epsilon L^2}
\le
\frac{2d}{\epsilon L^2}\frac{32H_\epsilon L}{N}
=
\frac{64H_\epsilon}{\epsilon}\frac{d}{NL}
\le
\frac{64H_\epsilon512}{\epsilon}\frac{d}{N\ell_n}.
\]
Both sides are nonnegative, so squaring gives
\[
\left(\frac{2dB}{\epsilon L^2}\right)^2
\le
\left(\frac{64H_\epsilon512}{\epsilon}\right)^2
\frac{d^2}{N^2\ell_n^2}
\le
\left(\frac{64H_\epsilon512}{\epsilon}\right)^2\Xi .
\]
Also \(0\le\eta\le1/n\le1\), hence
\[
(6\eta)^2=36\eta^2\le36\eta\le\frac{36}{n}.
\]
The inequality \((x+y)^2\le2x^2+2y^2\), applied to
\[
x=\frac{2dB}{\epsilon L^2},
\qquad
y=6\eta,
\]
therefore yields
\[
\left(\frac{2dB}{\epsilon L^2}+6\eta\right)^2
\le
\left\{
2\left(\frac{64H_\epsilon512}{\epsilon}\right)^2+72
\right\}\Xi .
\]
% lean: sq_add_le_two, hybrid_deterministic_envelope_le

\item Combining the light-envelope, reciprocal, variance-polynomial, and squared-bias estimates gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
&4A\left\{\frac{\min\{1,dB\}}u+dB^2\right\}
+C_0(u^{-1}+t^{-1})
+\frac{8dB^2}{\epsilon^2L^4}
+\frac4{\epsilon t}
+414\eta
+\left(\frac{2dB}{\epsilon L^2}+6\eta\right)^2  \\
&\qquad\le
K_\epsilon(C_0)\,\Xi .
\end{aligned}
\]
Since \(0\le\Xi\) and \(K_\epsilon(C_0)\le C\), the right-hand side is at most \(C\Xi\). Substituting back
\[
A=A_\star^L,\qquad \eta=n^{-12},\qquad
\Xi=\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{N^2\ell_n^2},
\]
with \(N=n+m\) and \(\ell_n=\log(en)\) from \cref{obj:def:frontier-rate-handle}, is precisely the asserted inequality. % lean: hybrid_deterministic_envelope_le, calibrated_envelope_absorption_exists
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:hybrid-light-branch-mean-bound} compares the light polynomial contribution with the inverse-count contribution on cells whose covariate mass is at most \(B\). The bound has a Chebyshev approximation term and a factorial-pool intensity term, matching the light-branch mean terms that later enter the calibrated envelope.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:hybrid-light-branch-mean-bound}[Light branch mean bound]
Let \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) be a law in the finite model class of \cref{obj:def:model-class}.  Let \(u,t,B>0\) and let \(L\ge 2\) be an integer.  For each \(x\in[d]\), write
\[
p_x=P(X=x),\qquad
q_{ax}=P(X=x,A=a,Y=1),\qquad
s_{ax}=P(X=x,A=a).
\]
For each \(x\in[d]\), let \(S_{0x},S_{1x},K_{0x},K_{1x}\) be independent Poisson counts with
\[
S_{ax}\sim \operatorname{Poi}(u q_{ax}),
\qquad
K_{ax}\sim \operatorname{Poi}(t s_{ax}),
\qquad a\in\{0,1\}.
\]
Using the light factorial-lift weights and heavy inverse-count weights from \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}, set
\[
P_x
=
\frac{S_{1x}}{u}\widehat W_{1,L,x}
-
\frac{S_{0x}}{u}\widehat W_{0,L,x},
\qquad
H_x
=
\frac{S_{1x}}{u}\widehat W^H_{1,x}
-
\frac{S_{0x}}{u}\widehat W^H_{0,x},
\]
where the light weights use degree \(L\), scale \(B\), and intensity \(t\).  Then
\[
\sum_{x:\,p_x\le B}
\left(\mathbb E P_x-\mathbb E H_x\right)^2
\le
\frac{8dB^2}{\epsilon^2 L^4}
+
\frac{4}{\epsilon t}.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:hybrid-light-branch-mean-bound}]
Let
\[
\theta_x:=p_x(\mu_{1x}-\mu_{0x}),\qquad
\ell_x:=\mathbb E P_x,\qquad
h_x:=\mathbb E H_x,
\]
where \(\mu_{ax}=P(Y=1\mid X=x,A=a)\), with the usual harmless value when
\(s_{ax}=0\). Let \(S_B=\{x\in[d]:p_x\le B\}\). The cell masses are nonnegative and
\(\sum_{x\in[d]}p_x=1\), since \(P\) is a probability law. We prove the bound by first controlling
each summand and then summing over \(S_B\). % lean: sum_light_branchMean_sq_le

\emph{Step 1.} Fix \(x\in S_B\). The light-branch expectation equals
\[
\ell_x
=
q_{1x}\frac{p_x}{B}G_L(s_{1x}/B)
-
q_{0x}\frac{p_x}{B}G_L(s_{0x}/B).
\]
To verify the first equality, write the light polynomial as
\[
W_{a,L}(z_0,z_1)=\sum_{r_0,r_1}c_{a,r_0,r_1}z_0^{r_0}z_1^{r_1}.
\]
The factorial lift in \cref{obj:synth_4} is
\[
\widehat W_{a,L,x}
=\sum_{r_0,r_1}c_{a,r_0,r_1}
\frac{(K_{0x})_{r_0}(K_{1x})_{r_1}}{t^{r_0+r_1}}.
\]
Independence and the Poisson factorial-moment identity
\(\mathbb E[(K_{ax})_r]=(ts_{ax})^r\) therefore give
\(\mathbb E\widehat W_{a,L,x}=W_{a,L}(s_{0x},s_{1x})\).  Independently,
\(\mathbb E(S_{ax}/u)=q_{ax}\), which proves the displayed formula for \(\ell_x\).
The light Chebyshev estimate then gives
\[
|\ell_x-\theta_x|
\le
\frac{2B}{\epsilon L^2}.
\]
Indeed \(p_x\le B\), \(s_{0x}+s_{1x}=p_x\), and \cref{obj:ass:overlap} gives
\(s_{ax}\ge \epsilon p_x\) for each arm \(a\) whenever \(p_x>0\). If \(p_x=0\),
then \(s_{0x}=s_{1x}=q_{0x}=q_{1x}=0\), so the displayed bound is immediate. If \(p_x>0\), the
shifted Chebyshev certificate in \cref{obj:lem:chebyshev-factorial-certificate}, together with the
definitions of \(E_L\) and \(G_L\) in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}, gives, for
\(z_a=s_{ax}/B\in[0,1]\),
\[
0\le E_L(z_a)\le \frac{1}{L^2z_a},
\qquad
1-z_aG_L(z_a)=E_L(z_a).
\]
Writing \(q_{ax}=s_{ax}\mu_{ax}\), the light-branch error becomes a linear combination of the two
terms \(p_x\mu_{ax}E_L(s_{ax}/B)\). Since \(0\le\mu_{ax}\le1\) and
\(s_{ax}\ge\epsilon p_x\), each arm contributes at most \(B/(\epsilon L^2)\), giving the stated
two-arm bound. % lean: light_exact_mean_bias_le

\emph{Step 2.} The heavy inverse-count branch satisfies
\[
|h_x-\theta_x|
\le
2p_x\exp(-\epsilon t p_x).
\]
Write \(u_{\rm out}:=u\) for the outcome-block intensity.  For each arm
\(a\in\{0,1\}\), set
\[
\eta_{ax}:=
\mathbb E\!\left[
\frac{S_{ax}}{u_{\rm out}}\frac{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1}{K_{ax}+1}
\right].
\]
Since the outcome pool is independent of the factorial pool and
\(\mathbb E S_{ax}=u_{\rm out}q_{ax}\),
\[
\eta_{ax}
=
q_{ax}\,
\mathbb E\!\left[\frac{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1}{K_{ax}+1}\right].
\]
The armwise inverse-count expectation formula in
\cref{obj:lem:poisson-inverse-arm-mean-formula}, applied to the independent factorial counts,
gives
\[
\eta_{ax}
=
p_x\mu_{ax}-(p_x-s_{ax})\mu_{ax}\exp(-t s_{ax}).
\]
Consequently
\[
|\eta_{ax}-p_x\mu_{ax}|
=
(p_x-s_{ax})\mu_{ax}\exp(-t s_{ax})
\le
p_x\exp(-\epsilon t p_x),
\]
because \(0\le p_x-s_{ax}\le p_x\), \(0\le\mu_{ax}\le1\), and occupied-cell overlap gives
\(s_{ax}\ge\epsilon p_x\); when \(p_x=0\), both sides vanish. Since
\[
h_x-\theta_x=(\eta_{1x}-p_x\mu_{1x})-(\eta_{0x}-p_x\mu_{0x}),
\]
the triangle inequality gives the displayed heavy-branch bound. % lean: integral_hybridHeavyPools_bias_le

\emph{Step 3.} Combining the two preceding bounds,
\[
(\ell_x-h_x)^2
\le
\frac{8B^2}{\epsilon^2L^4}
+
8p_x^2\exp(-2\epsilon t p_x).
\]
To see this, write
\[
\ell_x-h_x=(\ell_x-\theta_x)-(h_x-\theta_x)
\]
and use \((r-s)^2\le 2r^2+2s^2\) with
\(|r|\le 2B/(\epsilon L^2)\) and
\(|s|\le 2p_x\exp(-\epsilon t p_x)\). % lean: sum_light_branchMean_sq_le

\emph{Step 4.} For every \(x\),
\[
p_x^2\exp(-2\epsilon t p_x)
\le
\frac{p_x}{2\epsilon e\,t}.
\]
Indeed,
\[
p_x^2\exp(-2\epsilon t p_x)
=
p_x\{p_x\exp(-(2\epsilon)t p_x)\}.
\]
The elementary envelope \(z\exp(-\alpha t z)\le(\alpha e t)^{-1}\), applied with
\(z=p_x\) and \(\alpha=2\epsilon\), gives
\[
p_x\exp(-(2\epsilon)t p_x)\le \frac{1}{2\epsilon e\,t}.
\]
Multiplying by the nonnegative factor \(p_x\) gives the displayed bound. The envelope itself follows from
\(e\,(\alpha t z)\le \exp(\alpha t z)\), multiplied by
\(\exp(-\alpha t z)\), and then divided by the positive number \(\alpha e t\). % lean: poisson_bias_envelope

\emph{Step 5.} Summing the pointwise bound over \(S_B\) gives
\[
\sum_{x\in S_B}(\ell_x-h_x)^2
\le
\sum_{x\in S_B}
\left\{
\frac{8B^2}{\epsilon^2L^4}
+
8\frac{p_x}{2\epsilon e\,t}
\right\}.
\]
Since \(|S_B|\le d\) and \(\sum_{x\in S_B}p_x\le\sum_{x\in[d]}p_x=1\),
\[
\sum_{x\in S_B}(\ell_x-h_x)^2
\le
\frac{8dB^2}{\epsilon^2L^4}
+
\frac{8}{2\epsilon e\,t}.
\]
Finally \(1\le e\), so \(8/(2\epsilon e\,t)\le 4/(\epsilon t)\). Substituting
\(\ell_x=\mathbb E P_x\) and \(h_x=\mathbb E H_x\) yields
\[
\sum_{x:\,p_x\le B}
\left(\mathbb E P_x-\mathbb E H_x\right)^2
\le
\frac{8dB^2}{\epsilon^2L^4}
+
\frac{4}{\epsilon t},
\]
as claimed. % lean: sum_light_branchMean_sq_le
\end{proof}

The comparison in \cref{obj:lem:hybrid-light-branch-mean-bound} is anchored by an exact arm-level identity for the heavy inverse-count mean. The identity expresses the inverse-count arm contribution as the desired cell term plus an exponentially damped residual.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:poisson-inverse-arm-mean-formula}[Inverse-count arm mean]
Let \(P\) be an observed-data law on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\), let \(x\in[d]\), and let \(a\in\{0,1\}\). Write \(p_x\) for the covariate-cell mass, \(s_{ax}\) for the treatment-covariate cell mass, \(q_{ax}\) for the outcome-marked mass, and \(\mu_{ax}:=q_{ax}/s_{ax}\) for the totalized outcome regression. If \(u>0\) and \(t>0\), and if \(K_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t s_{ax})\) and \(K_{1-a,x}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t s_{1-a,x})\) are independent, then
\[
\frac{q_{ax}}{u/u}\,
\mathbb E\!\left[1+\frac{K_{1-a,x}}{K_{ax}+1}\right]
=
p_x\mu_{ax}-(p_x-s_{ax})\mu_{ax}\exp(-t s_{ax}).
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:poisson-inverse-arm-mean-formula}]
1.  Put \(\bar a:=1-a\) and write
\[
\Lambda_a:=u q_{ax},\qquad
\Theta_a:=t s_{ax},\qquad
\Theta_{\bar a}:=t s_{\bar a x}.
\]
The cell probabilities are nonnegative, so \(s_{ax}\ge0\), \(s_{\bar a x}\ge0\), and \(q_{ax}\ge0\). Since \(u,t>0\), the nonnegative rates above are the real values of the corresponding Poisson intensities. Also,
\[
s_{ax}+s_{\bar a x}=p_x,
\]
because the two treatment arms partition the covariate cell \(x\). % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.poisson_inverse_arm_mean_formula

2.  Define
\[
\Omega_a:=
\mathbb E\!\left[
1+\frac{K_{\bar a x}}{K_{ax}+1}
\right],
\qquad
K_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(\Theta_a),\quad
K_{\bar a x}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(\Theta_{\bar a}),
\]
with the two counts independent. The product-Poisson identity used for this expectation is
\[
\Theta_a\Omega_a
=
\Theta_a+\Theta_{\bar a}\bigl(1-e^{-\Theta_a}\bigr).
\]
Indeed, for \(r(k):=(k+1)^{-1}\),
\[
\Omega_a
=
1+
\int v\,r(k)\,
d\bigl(\operatorname{Poi}(\Theta_a)\otimes\operatorname{Poi}(\Theta_{\bar a})\bigr)(k,v).
\]
Fubini factorizes the cross term as
\[
\int v\,r(k)\,d(\operatorname{Poi}(\Theta_a)\otimes\operatorname{Poi}(\Theta_{\bar a}))
=
\left(\int r(k)\,d\operatorname{Poi}(\Theta_a)(k)\right)
\left(\int v\,d\operatorname{Poi}(\Theta_{\bar a})(v)\right).
\]
The Poisson first moment gives
\[
\int v\,d\operatorname{Poi}(\Theta_{\bar a})(v)=\Theta_{\bar a},
\]
and the shifted reciprocal identity gives
\[
\Theta_a\int \frac{1}{k+1}\,d\operatorname{Poi}(\Theta_a)(k)
=
1-e^{-\Theta_a}.
\]
Multiplying the displayed formula for \(\Omega_a\) by \(\Theta_a\) yields the claimed identity. % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.poisson_inverse_weight_mean_mul_rate

3.  Substituting \(\Theta_a=t s_{ax}\), \(\Theta_{\bar a}=t s_{\bar a x}\), and \(s_{\bar a x}=p_x-s_{ax}\), the preceding display becomes
\[
(t s_{ax})\Omega_a
=
t s_{ax}
+
t(p_x-s_{ax})\bigl(1-e^{-t s_{ax}}\bigr).
\]
Because \(t>0\), cancellation of \(t\) gives
\[
s_{ax}\Omega_a
=
s_{ax}
+
(p_x-s_{ax})\bigl(1-e^{-t s_{ax}}\bigr).
\]
Multiplying by \(\mu_{ax}\) and rearranging,
\[
(s_{ax}\mu_{ax})\Omega_a
=
p_x\mu_{ax}
-
(p_x-s_{ax})\mu_{ax}e^{-t s_{ax}}.
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.poisson_inverse_arm_expectation_scalar

4.  Finally,
\[
q_{ax}=s_{ax}\mu_{ax}.
\]
This is immediate from \(\mu_{ax}=q_{ax}/s_{ax}\) when \(s_{ax}\ne0\). When \(s_{ax}=0\), the bound \(0\le q_{ax}\le s_{ax}\), obtained by summing the nonnegative outcome masses in the arm cell, gives \(q_{ax}=0\), and the same identity holds under the totalized division convention. Hence, since the normalized outcome count has mean \(q_{ax}\),
\[
q_{ax}\,
\mathbb E\!\left[1+\frac{K_{\bar a x}}{K_{ax}+1}\right]
=
(s_{ax}\mu_{ax})\Omega_a
=
p_x\mu_{ax}
-
(p_x-s_{ax})\mu_{ax}e^{-t s_{ax}},
\]
as claimed. % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.markedMass_eq_armMass_mul_outcomeMean
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:poisson-inverse-arm-mean-formula} supplies the cellwise bias formula used when the heavy branch is compared with the target ATE contribution. The exponential factor is controlled by the treatment--covariate intensity, so the identity connects the heavy branch to the same marginal scale that appears in \cref{obj:lem:poisson-inverse-count-risk}.

A second auxiliary bound controls the event that a truly heavy cell is nevertheless routed to the light polynomial branch. It combines the pilot probability of such a routing event with the polynomial second moment under the factorial-pool law.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:heavy-light-second-moment-sum}[Heavy light second moment]
Let \(d\) be a finite alphabet size, let \(P\) be an observed-data law on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\), and let \(n,m\) be integers. Suppose that:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Positive overlap parameter.)} The overlap parameter satisfies \(\epsilon>0\).
\item \textbf{(Sample size.)} The labeled sample size satisfies \(n\ge1\).
\item \textbf{(Calibration.)} The finite calibration predicate \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) from \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} holds.
\end{itemize}
Set
\[
u=\frac{M_0}{8},\qquad
t_p=\frac{M_p}{8},\qquad
t=\frac{M_f}{8},\qquad
L=L(n),\qquad
B=B(n,m,\epsilon),\qquad
k_0=k_0(n,m,\epsilon),
\]
with these quantities defined as in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}. For each \(x\in[d]\), let
\[
\pi_x
=
\mathbb P\{J_x\le k_0\},
\]
where, conditionally on \(P\), the pilot counts have independent components
\(J_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t_p s_{ax})\), \(a\in\{0,1\}\). Let \(\mu_x\) be the product law under which the outcome and factorial counts are independent with
\[
S_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(u q_{ax}),
\qquad
K_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t s_{ax}),
\qquad a\in\{0,1\},
\]
and define the light polynomial cell contribution
\[
P_x
=
\frac{S_{1x}}{u}\widehat W_{1,L,x}
-
\frac{S_{0x}}{u}\widehat W_{0,L,x},
\]
where the factorial lift is taken at degree \(L\), scale \(B\), and intensity \(t\). Then
\[
\sum_{x:\,p_x>B}\pi_x\,\mathbb E_{\mu_x}\!\left[P_x^2\right]
\le
132\,n^{-12}.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:heavy-light-second-moment-sum}]
Let
\[
\mathsf{Heavy}_B=\{x\in[d]:p_x>B\},
\qquad
p_x=\mathbb P_P\{X=x\}.
\]
The quantities \(u,t_p,t,L,B,k_0\) are those fixed in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}. For each \(x\), put
\[
\Xi_x
=
\int
\left[
\frac{S_{1x}}u\widehat W_{1,L,x}
-
\frac{S_{0x}}u\widehat W_{0,L,x}
\right]^2\,d\mu_x .
\]
Thus the left-hand side is \(\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Heavy}_B}\pi_x\Xi_x\).

\begin{enumerate}
\item The cell masses satisfy
\[
0\le p_x\le1
\quad\text{for every }x\in[d],
\qquad
\sum_{x\in[d]}p_x=1.
\]
Hence, for the finite set \(\mathsf{Heavy}_B\),
\[
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Heavy}_B}p_x\le1,
\qquad
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Heavy}_B}p_x^2
\le
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Heavy}_B}p_x
\le1.
\]
The calibration predicate gives \(u,t_p,t,B>0\), \(L\ge2\), \(L\le tB\), and \(1/32\le u/n\). Since \(n\ge1\),
\[
u\ge \frac n{32}\ge \frac1{32},
\qquad
u^{-1}\le32.
\]
These inequalities also cover the case \(\mathsf{Heavy}_B=\varnothing\), where the sums are zero. % lean: cellMass_sum_eq_one_annotation

\item Fix \(x\in\mathsf{Heavy}_B\), and define
\[
y_x=\frac{p_x}{B}.
\]
Because \(B>0\) and \(p_x>B\), \(y_x\ge1\). Also \(p_x=By_x\). The pilot count is
\[
J_x=J_{0x}+J_{1x},
\qquad
J_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t_ps_{ax}),
\]
with independent arms and \(s_{0x}+s_{1x}=p_x\). Since \(k_0=\lfloor t_pB/4\rfloor\) and \(t_pB\ge0\),
\[
k_0\le \frac{t_pB}{4}<\frac{t_pp_x}{4}.
\]
The standard Poisson lower-tail bound therefore gives
\[
\pi_x
=
\mathbb P\{J_x\le k_0\}
\le
\exp\!\left(-\frac{t_pp_x}{4}\right).
\]
For the light-polynomial second moment, \cref{obj:lem:explicit-chebyshev-calibration} supplies the factorial-lift bound
\[
\mathbb E\!\left[\widehat W_{a,L,x}^{\,2}\right]
\le
A_\star^L\left(1+\frac{p_x}{B}\right)^{2L},
\qquad a\in\{0,1\}.
\]
Writing \(q_{ax}=\mathbb P_P\{X=x,A=a,Y=1\}\), independence of \(S_{ax}\) and the factorial counts gives
\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
\left(\frac{S_{ax}}u\widehat W_{a,L,x}\right)^2
\right]
=
\left(\frac{q_{ax}}u+q_{ax}^2\right)
\mathbb E\!\left[\widehat W_{a,L,x}^{\,2}\right].
\]
Since \(0\le q_{ax}\le p_x\), and using \((r-s)^2\le2r^2+2s^2\),
\[
\Xi_x
\le
4\left(\frac{p_x}{u}+p_x^2\right)
A_\star^L\left(1+\frac{p_x}{B}\right)^{2L}.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\pi_x\Xi_x
\le
4\left(\frac{p_x}{u}+p_x^2\right)
A_\star^L(1+y_x)^{2L}
\exp\!\left(-\frac{t_pp_x}{4}\right).
\] % lean: calibrated_heavy_cell_light_secondMoment_le

\item The definition \(B=H_\epsilon L/\min(t_p,t)\), together with positivity of \(t_p,t\), gives
\[
H_\epsilon L\le t_pB.
\]
Therefore
\[
\exp\!\left(-\frac{t_pp_x}{4}\right)
\le
\exp\!\left(-\frac{H_\epsilon L y_x}{4}\right).
\]
The numerical choice of \(H_\epsilon\) in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} includes
\[
15+\frac{24}{c^\circ}\le \frac{H_\epsilon}{4}.
\]
Using \(\log A_\star\le13\), \(\log(1+y_x)\le y_x\), and \(y_x\ge1\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
\log\!\left[
A_\star^L(1+y_x)^{2L}
\exp\!\left(-\frac{H_\epsilon L y_x}{4}\right)
\right]
&=
L\log A_\star+2L\log(1+y_x)-\frac{H_\epsilon L y_x}{4} \\
&\le
15Ly_x-\frac{H_\epsilon L y_x}{4} \\
&\le
-\frac{24Ly_x}{c^\circ}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus
\[
A_\star^L(1+y_x)^{2L}
\exp\!\left(-\frac{t_pp_x}{4}\right)
\le
\exp\!\left(-\frac{24Ly_x}{c^\circ}\right).
\]
The calibration predicate also gives \(c^\circ b_n/2\le L\), where \(b_n=1+\lceil\log_2 n\rceil\). Since \(y_x\ge1\),
\[
12b_n\le \frac{24Ly_x}{c^\circ}.
\]
Finally \(b_n\) dominates \(\log n\) for \(n\ge1\), so
\[
\exp(-12b_n)\le n^{-12}.
\]
Combining the last three displays yields
\[
A_\star^L(1+y_x)^{2L}
\exp\!\left(-\frac{t_pp_x}{4}\right)
\le n^{-12}.
\]
Substituting this into the bound from the previous step gives, for every \(x\in\mathsf{Heavy}_B\),
\[
\pi_x\Xi_x
\le
4\left(\frac{p_x}{u}+p_x^2\right)n^{-12}.
\] % lean: factorial_growth_mul_heavy_exp_le

\item Summing the preceding cellwise inequality over \(x\in\mathsf{Heavy}_B\),
\[
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Heavy}_B}\pi_x\Xi_x
\le
4\left(
\frac{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Heavy}_B}p_x}{u}
+
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Heavy}_B}p_x^2
\right)n^{-12}.
\]
By the bounds from the first step,
\[
\frac{\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Heavy}_B}p_x}{u}
\le
u^{-1}
\le32,
\qquad
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Heavy}_B}p_x^2\le1.
\]
Therefore
\[
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Heavy}_B}\pi_x\Xi_x
\le
4(32+1)n^{-12}
=
132\,n^{-12}.
\]
This is the claimed bound. % lean: sum_heavy_light_secondMoment_le
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:heavy-light-second-moment-sum} makes the heavy-cell misclassification contribution negligible on the calibrated scale. Its summand contains both the pilot routing probability and the light-branch second moment, so it is the variance-side counterpart of the heavy-branch mean control.

\Cref{obj:lem:hybrid-heavy-branch-mean-bound} controls the contribution from heavy cells near the pilot decision boundary. The product factor \(\pi_x\eta_x\) records that only disagreement between the light and heavy branches matters, and the calibrated split in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} makes the summed contribution polynomially small in the labeled sample size.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:hybrid-heavy-branch-mean-bound}[Heavy branch mean bound]
Let \(d\) be a finite alphabet size, let \(\epsilon>0\), and let \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) as in \cref{obj:def:model-class}.  Let \(n\ge1\) and \(m\ge0\) be integers.  Assume:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Calibration.)} The hybrid-estimator calibration predicate \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} holds.
\item \textbf{(Block intensities.)} With \(M_0,n_p,n_f,m_p,m_f\) as in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}, set
\[
u=\frac{M_0}{8},\qquad
t_p=\frac{n_p+m_p}{8},\qquad
t=\frac{n_f+m_f}{8}.
\]
\item \textbf{(Cell laws.)} For each \(x\in[d]\), let \(\mu_x\) be the product law under which the outcome-block success counts \(S_{ax}\) are independent \(\operatorname{Poi}(u q_{ax})\) variables and the factorial-pool counts \(K_{ax}\) are independent \(\operatorname{Poi}(t s_{ax})\) variables, for \(a\in\{0,1\}\).
\end{itemize}
Let \(L=L(n)\), \(B=B(n,m,\epsilon)\), and \(k_0=k_0(n,m,\epsilon)\) be the degree, light-cell scale, and pilot threshold from \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}.  For each \(x\in[d]\), let
\[
\pi_x=\mathbb P\{J_x\le k_0\},
\qquad
\eta_x=\mathbb P\{J_x>k_0\},
\]
where \(J_x=J_{0x}+J_{1x}\) has independent components \(J_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t_p s_{ax})\).  Let \(P_x\) be the light polynomial cell contribution and \(H_x\) the heavy inverse-count cell contribution from \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}, evaluated under \(\mu_x\).  Then
\[
\sum_{x\in[d]:\,p_x>B}
\pi_x\eta_x
\left(\mathbb E_{\mu_x}P_x-\mathbb E_{\mu_x}H_x\right)^2
\le 282\,n^{-12}.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:hybrid-heavy-branch-mean-bound}]
Let
\[
\mathcal H=\{x\in[d]:p_x>B\},\qquad
E^L_x=\mathbb E_{\mu_x}P_x,\qquad
E^H_x=\mathbb E_{\mu_x}H_x .
\]
The calibration inequalities give \(u>0\), \(t_p>0\), \(t>0\), and \(B>0\). Also \(0\le p_x\le1\) for every \(x\), and \(\sum_xp_x=1\), hence
\[
\sum_{x\in\mathcal H}p_x\le1 .
\]
% lean: calibrationPredicate_positive_parameters, cellMass_mem_unitInterval, cellMass_sum_eq_one_annotation

Fix \(x\in\mathcal H\). We first establish the calibrated pilot-tail estimate
\[
\pi_x\le n^{-12}.
\]
For \(a\in\{0,1\}\), write \(s_{ax}\) for the treatment-covariate mass in arm \(a\), so \(p_x=s_{0x}+s_{1x}\). Under the pilot law, \(J_x=J_{0x}+J_{1x}\) has Poisson mean
\[
\lambda_x=t_p(s_{0x}+s_{1x})=t_pp_x,
\]
because the two pilot-arm counts are independent Poisson variables. The definition of \(k_0\) gives \(k_0\le t_pB/4\), and \(x\in\mathcal H\) gives \(B<p_x\). Since \(t_p>0\),
\[
k_0<\frac{t_pp_x}{4}=\frac{\lambda_x}{4}.
\]
For a Poisson variable with mean \(\lambda_x\), the following lower-tail estimate applies whenever the cutoff is below one quarter of the mean:
\[
\mathbb P\{J_x\le k_0\}\le \exp\{-\lambda_x/4\}.
\]
Indeed, for every \(\alpha>0\), Markov's inequality applied to \(e^{-\alpha J_x}\) gives
\[
\mathbb P\{J_x\le k_0\}
\le
\exp\{\lambda_x(e^{-\alpha}-1)+\alpha k_0\}.
\]
If \(k_0=0\), this is \(e^{-\lambda_x}\le e^{-\lambda_x/4}\). If \(k_0\ge1\), take \(\alpha=\log(\lambda_x/k_0)\). With \(\zeta_x=k_0/\lambda_x\le1/4\), the exponent becomes
\[
\lambda_x\{-1+\zeta_x-\zeta_x\log\zeta_x\}
\le -\lambda_x/4,
\]
since \(z-z\log z\le 1/4+(\log 4)/4<3/4\) on \(0\le z\le1/4\). Therefore
\[
\pi_x=\mathbb P\{J_x\le k_0\}\le \exp\{-t_pp_x/4\}.
\]
Let \(h_n=1+\lceil\log_2 n\rceil\) be the binary-length calibration quantity in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}. The tail calibration inequality
\[
15L+12h_n\le \frac{t_pB}{4},
\]
together with \(p_x>B\), yields
\[
\frac{t_pp_x}{4}\ge 12h_n.
\]
Thus
\[
\pi_x\le \exp\{-12h_n\}\le n^{-12},
\]
where the last inequality follows from \(n\ge1\) and \(h_n\ge \log n\).
% lean: calibrated_heavy_cell_pilot_le

The heavy inverse-count mean satisfies
\[
|E^H_x|\le 3p_x .
\]
To prove this, first write
\[
\theta_x=p_x(\mu_{1x}-\mu_{0x}),
\]
where, for \(a\in\{0,1\}\), \(q_{ax}\) is the outcome-marked mass, \(s_{ax}\) is the treatment-covariate mass, and the arm outcome regression is characterized by
\[
q_{ax}=s_{ax}\mu_{ax},\qquad 0\le \mu_{ax}\le 1 .
\]
For the arm-specific inverse-count means set
\[
\overline H_{a,x}
=
\mathbb E_{\mu_x}\!\left[
\frac{S_{ax}}{u}\,
\frac{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1}{K_{ax}+1}
\right],\qquad a\in\{0,1\}.
\]
By the definition of the heavy cell contribution,
\[
E^H_x=\overline H_{1,x}-\overline H_{0,x}.
\]
We next compute each arm mean. Since \(S_{ax}\) is independent of \((K_{0x},K_{1x})\) and \(\mathbb E S_{ax}=u q_{ax}\),
\[
\overline H_{a,x}
=
q_{ax}\,
\mathbb E\!\left[\frac{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1}{K_{ax}+1}\right].
\]
For the chosen arm \(a\), the fraction inside the expectation is
\[
\frac{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1}{K_{ax}+1}
=
1+\frac{K_{1-a,x}}{K_{ax}+1},
\]
where \(1-a\) denotes the opposite arm. Therefore \cref{obj:lem:poisson-inverse-arm-mean-formula}, applied with this arm and its opposite arm, gives the exact identity
\[
\overline H_{a,x}
=
 p_x\mu_{ax}-(p_x-s_{ax})\mu_{ax}\exp\{-t s_{ax}\}.
\]
Since \(x\in\mathcal H\), the cell is occupied. Hence \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) and \cref{obj:def:model-class,obj:ass:overlap} give, for both arms,
\[
\epsilon p_x\le s_{ax}\le p_x .
\]
Using this, \(0\le\mu_{ax}\le1\), and \(t>0\), the exact identity yields the armwise envelope
\[
\begin{aligned}
|\overline H_{a,x}-p_x\mu_{ax}|
&=\left|-(p_x-s_{ax})\mu_{ax}\exp\{-t s_{ax}\}\right|\\
&=(p_x-s_{ax})\mu_{ax}\exp\{-t s_{ax}\}\\
&\le p_x\exp\{-\epsilon t p_x\}.
\end{aligned}
\]
The first equality uses the displayed mean formula, the second uses nonnegativity of every factor, and the last step uses \(p_x-s_{ax}\le p_x\) and \(\exp\{-t s_{ax}\}\le\exp\{-\epsilon t p_x\}\). Hence
\[
\begin{aligned}
|E^H_x-\theta_x|
&=\left|\bigl(\overline H_{1,x}-p_x\mu_{1x}\bigr)
      -\bigl(\overline H_{0,x}-p_x\mu_{0x}\bigr)\right|\\
&\le |\overline H_{1,x}-p_x\mu_{1x}|
   + |\overline H_{0,x}-p_x\mu_{0x}|\\
&\le 2p_x\exp\{-\epsilon t p_x\}.
\end{aligned}
\]
% lean: integral_hybridHeavyPools_bias_le, poisson_inverse_arm_mean_formula, poisson_inverse_arm_bias_envelope
Since \(0\le p_x\le1\) and \(\mu_{1x},\mu_{0x}\in[0,1]\),
\[
|\theta_x|\le p_x .
\]
Also, because \(\epsilon>0\), \(t>0\), and \(p_x\ge0\),
\[
\exp\{-\epsilon t p_x\}\le1.
\]
Thus
\[
|E^H_x|
\le |E^H_x-\theta_x|+|\theta_x|
\le 2p_x+p_x
=3p_x .
\]
% lean: abs_integral_hybridHeavyPools_le_three_cellMass

Now \(\eta_x\le1\), \(\pi_x\ge0\), and \(\eta_x\ge0\). Using
\[
(E^L_x-E^H_x)^2\le 2(E^L_x)^2+2(E^H_x)^2
\]
and the preceding heavy-mean bound,
\[
(E^H_x)^2\le 9p_x^2\le 9p_x,
\]
where the last inequality uses \(0\le p_x\le1\). Hence
\[
\begin{aligned}
\pi_x\eta_x(E^L_x-E^H_x)^2
&\le \pi_x(E^L_x-E^H_x)^2\\
&\le 2\pi_x(E^L_x)^2+2\pi_x(E^H_x)^2\\
&\le 2\pi_x(E^L_x)^2+18p_x n^{-12}.
\end{aligned}
\]
% lean: sq_sub_le_two_sq_add_two_sq

Summing this pointwise estimate over \(x\in\mathcal H\) gives
\[
\sum_{x\in\mathcal H}\pi_x\eta_x(E^L_x-E^H_x)^2
\le
2\sum_{x\in\mathcal H}\pi_x(E^L_x)^2
+
18n^{-12}\sum_{x\in\mathcal H}p_x .
\]
% lean: sum_heavy_branchMean_sq_le

It remains to bound the first sum. For each \(x\), nonnegativity of variance under \(\mu_x\) gives
\[
(E^L_x)^2
\le
\mathbb E_{\mu_x}\!\left[P_x^2\right].
\]
Consequently,
\[
\sum_{x\in\mathcal H}\pi_x(E^L_x)^2
\le
\sum_{x\in\mathcal H}\pi_x\,\mathbb E_{\mu_x}\!\left[P_x^2\right].
\]
The heavy-light second-moment bound in \cref{obj:lem:heavy-light-second-moment-sum}, applied with the same values of \(u,t_p,t,L,B,k_0\), gives
\[
\sum_{x\in\mathcal H}\pi_x\,\mathbb E_{\mu_x}\!\left[P_x^2\right]
\le 132n^{-12}.
\]
% lean: sum_heavy_light_secondMoment_le, hybridLightPools_memLp

Combining the last two displays with \(\sum_{x\in\mathcal H}p_x\le1\),
\[
\sum_{x\in\mathcal H}\pi_x\eta_x(E^L_x-E^H_x)^2
\le
2\cdot132\,n^{-12}+18\,n^{-12}
=
282\,n^{-12}.
\]
This is the desired bound.
% lean: sum_heavy_branchMean_sq_le
\end{proof}

The preceding local estimates are assembled into separate variance and mean-bias bounds for the full Poisson hybrid statistic. These two summaries expose exactly which terms enter the final rate calculation.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:calibrated-hybrid-variance-bound}[Hybrid variance bound]
Let \(\epsilon\), the overlap parameter, satisfy \(0<\epsilon<1/2\). There is a constant \(C_{\mathrm{var}}>0\), depending only on \(\epsilon\), such that the following holds. Let \(n,m,d\) be integers and let \(P\) be an observed-data law on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\). Assume:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Sample size.)} \(n\ge 1\).
\item \textbf{(Model class.)} \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), as in \cref{obj:def:model-class}.
\item \textbf{(Calibration.)} The finite calibration predicate \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} holds.
\end{itemize}
Let
\[
M_0=\lfloor n/3\rfloor,\qquad
n_p=\bigl\lfloor (n-M_0)/2\bigr\rfloor,\qquad
n_f=n-M_0-n_p,\qquad
m_p=\lfloor m/2\rfloor,\qquad
m_f=m-m_p,
\]
and set
\[
u=M_0/8,\qquad
t_p=(n_p+m_p)/8,\qquad
t=(n_f+m_f)/8.
\]
Let \(L=L(n)\), \(B=B(n,m,\epsilon)\), \(k_0=k_0(n,m,\epsilon)\), and \(A_\star=7056\) be the degree, scale, pilot threshold, and factorial-moment constant from \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}. Under the independent Poisson count law in which, independently over \(x\in[d]\) and \(a\in\{0,1\}\),
\[
S_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(u q_{ax}),\qquad
J_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t_p s_{ax}),\qquad
K_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t s_{ax}),
\]
let
\[
\widetilde Z
=
\sum_{x\in[d]}
\left(
\mathbf 1\{J_{0x}+J_{1x}\le k_0\}P_x
+
\mathbf 1\{J_{0x}+J_{1x}>k_0\}H_x
\right),
\]
where \(P_x\) and \(H_x\) are the light-cell polynomial and heavy inverse-count cell contributions specified in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}. Then
\[
\operatorname{Var}(\widetilde Z)
\le
4A_\star^L
\left\{
\frac{\min\{1,dB\}}{u}+dB^2
\right\}
+
C_{\mathrm{var}}\left(u^{-1}+t^{-1}\right)
+
\frac{8dB^2}{\epsilon^2L^4}
+
\frac{4}{\epsilon t}
+
414\,n^{-12}.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:calibrated-hybrid-variance-bound}]
Choose \(C_{\mathrm{var}}\) to be the positive constant supplied, at the present overlap parameter \(\epsilon\), by \cref{obj:lem:poisson-inverse-count-risk}. Fix \(n,m,d\) and \(P\) satisfying the hypotheses. Write
\[
p_x=P(X=x),\qquad
q_{ax}=P(X=x,A=a,Y=1),\qquad
s_{ax}=P(X=x,A=a),
\]
and let \(J_x=J_{0x}+J_{1x}\). The calibration predicate in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} gives
\[
u>0,\qquad t_p>0,\qquad t>0,\qquad B>0,\qquad L\ge2,\qquad L\le tB .
\]
For each \(x\in[d]\), let \(\mathbb Q_x\) be the product law of the outcome and factorial counts
\[
S_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(u q_{ax}),\qquad
K_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t s_{ax}),\qquad a\in\{0,1\},
\]
with all four variables independent. Define
\[
\lambda_x=\mathbb P\{J_x\le k_0\},\qquad
\theta_x=\mathbb P\{J_x>k_0\}=1-\lambda_x,
\]
and, under \(\mathbb Q_x\),
\[
\bar P_x=\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}P_x,\qquad
\bar H_x=\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}H_x,\qquad
V^P_x=\operatorname{Var}_{\mathbb Q_x}(P_x),\qquad
V^H_x=\operatorname{Var}_{\mathbb Q_x}(H_x).
\]
The probabilities satisfy \(0\le\lambda_x,\theta_x\le1\). % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.calibrated_hybrid_variance_le

\begin{enumerate}
\item Since the pilot counts are independent of the outcome and factorial counts, the selected cell is a two-branch mixture. For each cell,
\[
\operatorname{Var}\!\left(
\mathbf 1\{J_x\le k_0\}P_x+\mathbf 1\{J_x>k_0\}H_x
\right)
=
\lambda_x V^P_x+\theta_x V^H_x
+\lambda_x\theta_x(\bar P_x-\bar H_x)^2 .
\]
The cells are independent under the product Poisson law, so the variance of the sum is the sum of the cell variances:
\[
\operatorname{Var}(\widetilde Z)
=
\sum_{x\in[d]}
\left\{
\lambda_x V^P_x+\theta_x V^H_x
+\lambda_x\theta_x(\bar P_x-\bar H_x)^2
\right\}.
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.variance_sum_hybridSelectedCell

\item Bound the light-branch variance part by splitting the cells at the scale \(B\). Put
\[
\mathsf{Light}_B=\{x\in[d]:p_x\le B\},\qquad
\mathsf{Heavy}_B=\{x\in[d]:p_x>B\}.
\]
For \(x\in\mathsf{Light}_B\), \(\lambda_x\le1\) and
\[
V^P_x\le \mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}P_x^2.
\]
The factorial-lift second-moment estimate in \cref{obj:lem:explicit-chebyshev-calibration}, together with
\((r-s)^2\le2r^2+2s^2\), gives
\[
\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}P_x^2
\le
4A_\star^L\left(\frac{p_x}{u}+p_x^2\right),
\qquad x\in\mathsf{Light}_B .
\]
Indeed, for each arm \(a\), the light factorial weight has second moment at most \(A_\star^L\), while
\[
\mathbb E\left[\left(\frac{S_{ax}}u\right)^2\right]
=
\frac{q_{ax}}u+q_{ax}^2
\le
\frac{p_x}{u}+p_x^2 .
\]
Summing over \(\mathsf{Light}_B\) and using
\[
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Light}_B}p_x\le \min\{1,dB\},
\qquad
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Light}_B}p_x^2\le dB^2,
\]
yields
\[
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Light}_B}\lambda_x V^P_x
\le
4A_\star^L
\left\{
\frac{\min\{1,dB\}}{u}+dB^2
\right\}.
\]
On the complementary cells, \cref{obj:lem:heavy-light-second-moment-sum} gives
\[
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Heavy}_B}\lambda_x\,\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}P_x^2
\le 132\,n^{-12}.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\sum_{x\in[d]}\lambda_x V^P_x
\le
4A_\star^L
\left\{
\frac{\min\{1,dB\}}{u}+dB^2
\right\}
+132\,n^{-12}.
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.sum_light_secondMoment_le

\item For the heavy-branch variance part, \cref{obj:lem:poisson-inverse-count-risk} applied to the all-heavy statistic gives
\[
\operatorname{Var}\!\left(\sum_{x\in[d]}H_x\right)
\le
C_{\mathrm{var}}\left(u^{-1}+t^{-1}\right).
\]
Independence across cells gives
\[
\operatorname{Var}\!\left(\sum_{x\in[d]}H_x\right)
=
\sum_{x\in[d]}V^H_x,
\]
and since \(0\le\theta_x\le1\),
\[
\sum_{x\in[d]}\theta_x V^H_x
\le
C_{\mathrm{var}}\left(u^{-1}+t^{-1}\right).
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.pilotHeavy_variance_bound_exists

\item It remains to bound the branch-selection mean term. On \(\mathsf{Light}_B\), the inequality \(\lambda_x\theta_x\le1\) and \cref{obj:lem:hybrid-light-branch-mean-bound} give
\[
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Light}_B}
\lambda_x\theta_x(\bar P_x-\bar H_x)^2
\le
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Light}_B}(\bar P_x-\bar H_x)^2
\le
\frac{8dB^2}{\epsilon^2L^4}
+
\frac{4}{\epsilon t}.
\]
On \(\mathsf{Heavy}_B\), \cref{obj:lem:hybrid-heavy-branch-mean-bound} gives
\[
\sum_{x\in\mathsf{Heavy}_B}
\lambda_x\theta_x(\bar P_x-\bar H_x)^2
\le
282\,n^{-12}.
\]
Thus
\[
\sum_{x\in[d]}
\lambda_x\theta_x(\bar P_x-\bar H_x)^2
\le
\frac{8dB^2}{\epsilon^2L^4}
+
\frac{4}{\epsilon t}
+
282\,n^{-12}.
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.sum_light_branchMean_sq_le

\item Combining the three displayed bounds in the variance decomposition gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{Var}(\widetilde Z)
&\le
4A_\star^L
\left\{
\frac{\min\{1,dB\}}{u}+dB^2
\right\}
+
C_{\mathrm{var}}\left(u^{-1}+t^{-1}\right)\\
&\quad+
\frac{8dB^2}{\epsilon^2L^4}
+
\frac{4}{\epsilon t}
+
(132+282)n^{-12}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Since \(132+282=414\), this is the asserted bound.
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.calibrated_hybrid_variance_le
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:calibrated-hybrid-variance-bound} combines the light-cell factorial moment bound, the inverse-count variance contribution, and the pilot-routing error into a single variance inequality. Its display is arranged to match the envelope calculation in \cref{obj:lem:calibrated-envelope-absorption} term by term.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:calibrated-hybrid-mean-bias}[Calibrated hybrid mean bias]
Fix an overlap constant \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).  For every \(n,m,d\in\mathbb N\) and every observed-data law \(P\) on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\), suppose that:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Sample size.)} \(1\le n\).
\item \textbf{(Model class.)} \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), as in \cref{obj:def:model-class}.
\item \textbf{(Calibration.)} The finite calibration predicate \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} holds.
\end{itemize}
Let \(M_0,n_p,n_f,m_p,m_f\) be the block sizes from \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}, and set
\[
u=\frac{M_0}{8},\qquad
t_p=\frac{n_p+m_p}{8},\qquad
t=\frac{n_f+m_f}{8}.
\]
Let \(L=L(n)\), \(B=B(n,m,\epsilon)\), and \(k_0=k_0(n,m,\epsilon)\) be the degree, light-cell scale, and pilot threshold from \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}.  For each \(x\in[d]\), let the hybrid selected cell mean be the expectation of the cell contribution selected at threshold \(k_0\), degree \(L\), scale \(B\), and intensities \((u,t_p,t)\) under the independent Poisson count law in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}.  Then
\[
\left|
\sum_{x\in[d]}
\mathbb E_P[Z_x]
-\tau(P)
\right|
\le
\frac{2dB}{\epsilon L^2}+6n^{-12},
\]
where \(\tau(P)\) is the average treatment effect functional in \cref{obj:def:ate-functional}.
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:calibrated-hybrid-mean-bias}]
For \(x\in[d]\) and \(a\in\{0,1\}\), write
\[
p_x=P\{X=x\},\qquad
s_{ax}=P\{X=x,A=a\},\qquad
q_{ax}=P\{X=x,A=a,Y=1\},
\]
and define the totalized outcome regression by the paper's total real-division convention
\[
\mu_{ax}:=\frac{q_{ax}}{s_{ax}},
\]
so that \(\mu_{ax}=0\) when \(s_{ax}=0\), because real division is totalized with \(r/0=0\).  Put
\[
\vartheta_x:=p_x(\mu_{1x}-\mu_{0x}).
\]
Then \(\tau(P)=\sum_x\vartheta_x\), \(\sum_xp_x=1\), and
\[
|\vartheta_x|
\le p_x,
\]
because \(p_x\ge0\) and the two binary outcome regressions lie in \([0,1]\).  Write \(b_n\) for the binary-length calibration quantity appearing in \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\).  Throughout this proof, \(\log\) denotes the natural logarithm; since \(b_n=1+\lceil\log_2 n\rceil\), \(n\ge1\) implies \(\log n=(\log 2)\log_2 n\le b_n\), and hence \(\exp(-12b_n)\le n^{-12}\).  We record the positivity consequences of the finite inequalities defining \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}.  Since \(24\le n\), we have \(n>0\) and \(n+m>0\), so the denominators in the block-proportion bounds are positive.  Those bounds give
\[
\frac1{32}\le \frac un,
\qquad
\frac1{32}\le \frac{t_p}{n+m},
\qquad
\frac1{32}\le \frac t{n+m},
\]
and hence \(u>0\), \(t_p>0\), and \(t>0\).  The scale inequality \(8\le t_pB\), together with \(t_p>0\), then gives \(B>0\).  The calibration-scale definition in \cref{obj:synth_7} makes \(L=L(n)\) a maximum whose first entry is \(2\); in particular \(L\ge2\).  The model condition in \cref{obj:def:model-class} places \(P\) in the occupied-cell overlap class, and \cref{obj:ass:overlap} gives, whenever \(p_x>0\),
\[
\epsilon p_x\le s_{0x},
\qquad
\epsilon p_x\le s_{1x}.
\]
When \(p_x=0\), the identity \(s_{0x}+s_{1x}=p_x\) and nonnegativity of the
two cell masses imply \(s_{0x}=s_{1x}=0\), so the same inequalities remain
valid. % lean: calibrated_hybrid_mean_bias_le

For each cell, let \(J_{0x}^{\mathrm p}\) and \(J_{1x}^{\mathrm p}\) be the two independent pilot arm counts, with
\[
J_{0x}^{\mathrm p}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t_p s_{0x}),
\qquad
J_{1x}^{\mathrm p}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t_p s_{1x}),
\qquad
J_x:=J_{0x}^{\mathrm p}+J_{1x}^{\mathrm p}.
\]
Define
\[
\pi_x
:=
\Pr\{J_x\le k_0\},
\qquad
\eta_x
:=
\Pr\{J_x>k_0\}.
\]
Thus \(0\le\pi_x,\eta_x\le1\) and \(\eta_x=1-\pi_x\).  Let \(\mathbb Q_x\) be the independent product law of four armwise Poisson counts
\[
S_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(u q_{ax}),
\qquad
K_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t s_{ax}),
\qquad a\in\{0,1\},
\]
with all four variables mutually independent.  These are ordinary nonnegative Poisson intensities, because \(u,t>0\) and the cell masses \(q_{ax}\) and \(s_{ax}\) are nonnegative.  Define the light and heavy branch means
\[
\Lambda_x
:=
\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}\!\left[
\frac{S_{1x}}u\,\widehat W_{1,L,x}
-
\frac{S_{0x}}u\,\widehat W_{0,L,x}
\right],
\]
and
\[
\Gamma_x
:=
\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}\!\left[
\frac{S_{1x}}u\,\frac{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1}{K_{1x}+1}
-
\frac{S_{0x}}u\,\frac{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1}{K_{0x}+1}
\right].
\]
Let \(M_x\) denote the selected cell mean under the independent Poisson count law described in the lemma: the pilot pool selects the light branch on \(\{J_x\le k_0\}\) and the heavy branch on \(\{J_x>k_0\}\), while the outcome and factorial pools are integrated under \(\mathbb Q_x\).  Thus
\[
M_x
=
\mathbb E\!\left[
\mathbf 1_{\{J_x\le k_0\}}\left(
\frac{S_{1x}}u\,\widehat W_{1,L,x}
-
\frac{S_{0x}}u\,\widehat W_{0,L,x}\right)
+
\mathbf 1_{\{J_x>k_0\}}\left(
\frac{S_{1x}}u\,\frac{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1}{K_{1x}+1}
-
\frac{S_{0x}}u\,\frac{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1}{K_{0x}+1}\right)
\right].
\]
Independence of the pilot pool from the two evaluation pools gives the exact identity
\[
M_x=\pi_x\Lambda_x+\eta_x\Gamma_x .
\]
% lean: hybridSelectedCellMean_eq_mixture
We now derive the first-moment identity from the armwise Poisson factorial moments, using the factorial-lift definition in \cref{obj:synth_4}.  For \(a\in\{0,1\}\), set
\[
\Lambda_{a,x}
:=
\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}\!\left[
\frac{S_{ax}}u\,\widehat W_{a,L,x}
\right],
\qquad
\Lambda_x=\Lambda_{1,x}-\Lambda_{0,x}.
\]
Under \(\mathbb Q_x\), the outcome count \(S_{ax}\) is independent of the factorial-pool counts, and
\[
\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}S_{ax}=u q_{ax}.
\]
Write the light polynomial as in \cref{obj:synth_4}:
\[
W_{a,L}(r_0,r_1)=\sum_{\rho_0,\rho_1}
 c^{(a)}_{\rho_0,\rho_1}r_0^{\rho_0}r_1^{\rho_1},
\]
with only finitely many nonzero coefficients.  Its factorial lift is
\[
\widehat W_{a,L,x}(K_{0x},K_{1x})
=
\sum_{\rho_0,\rho_1}c^{(a)}_{\rho_0,\rho_1}
\frac{(K_{0x})_{\rho_0}(K_{1x})_{\rho_1}}
     {t^{\rho_0+\rho_1}},
\]
where \((k)_\rho\) is the falling factorial.  If \(K\sim\operatorname{Poi}(ts)\) with \(s\ge0\), then, for every \(\rho\ge0\),
\[
\mathbb E (K)_\rho
=
\sum_{\ell=\rho}^\infty
\frac{\ell!}{(\ell-\rho)!}
 e^{-ts}\frac{(ts)^\ell}{\ell!}
=(ts)^\rho
\sum_{j=0}^\infty e^{-ts}\frac{(ts)^j}{j!}
=(ts)^\rho.
\]
Since \(t>0\) and the two factorial-pool counts are independent Poisson variables with means \(t s_{0x}\) and \(t s_{1x}\), this gives
\[
\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}
\frac{(K_{0x})_{\rho_0}(K_{1x})_{\rho_1}}
     {t^{\rho_0+\rho_1}}
=s_{0x}^{\rho_0}s_{1x}^{\rho_1}.
\]
Termwise summation over the finite coefficient expansion yields the first-moment identity
\[
\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}\widehat W_{a,L,x}=W_{a,L}(s_{0x},s_{1x}),
\]
and therefore
\[
\Lambda_{a,x}
=q_{ax}\,W_{a,L}(s_{0x},s_{1x}).
\]
Using \(s_{0x}+s_{1x}=p_x\) and
\[
W_{a,L}(s_{0x},s_{1x})
=
\frac{p_x}{B}G_L\!\left(\frac{s_{ax}}B\right),
\]
we obtain
\[
\Lambda_x
=
q_{1x}\frac{p_x}{B}G_L\!\left(\frac{s_{1x}}B\right)
-
q_{0x}\frac{p_x}{B}G_L\!\left(\frac{s_{0x}}B\right).
\]
% lean: integral_hybridLightPools

Consider first a light cell, \(p_x\le B\).  If \(p_x=0\), then \(s_{0x}=s_{1x}=q_{0x}=q_{1x}=0\), so \(\Lambda_x=\vartheta_x=0\) and the light-branch bias bound is immediate.  It remains in this paragraph to handle the case \(p_x>0\).  For \(a\in\{0,1\}\), set
\[
z_{ax}:=\frac{s_{ax}}B,
\qquad
E_{ax}:=E_L(z_{ax}).
\]
The overlap inequalities give \(s_{ax}\ge\epsilon p_x>0\) for \(a=0,1\), so \(z_{ax}>0\); the light-cell condition \(p_x\le B\), together with \(s_{ax}\le p_x\) and \(B>0\), gives \(z_{ax}\le1\).  Hence \(z_{ax}\in(0,1]\).  The factorial-moment calculation above has already identified the light-pool first moment.  Since \(L\ge2\) and \(z_{ax}\in(0,1]\), the interval part of \cref{obj:lem:explicit-chebyshev-calibration} applies and gives
\[
z_{ax}G_L(z_{ax})+E_{ax}=1,
\qquad
0\le E_{ax},
\qquad
E_{ax}\le\frac{1}{L^2z_{ax}} .
\]  Since
\(q_{ax}=s_{ax}\mu_{ax}\), the displayed formula for \(\Lambda_x\) becomes, in the case \(p_x>0\),
\[
\Lambda_x
=
p_x\mu_{1x}(1-E_{1x})
-
p_x\mu_{0x}(1-E_{0x}),
\]
and therefore
\[
|\Lambda_x-\vartheta_x|
\le
p_x\mu_{1x}E_{1x}+p_x\mu_{0x}E_{0x}
\le
\frac{p_xB}{L^2s_{1x}}+\frac{p_xB}{L^2s_{0x}}
\le
\frac{2B}{\epsilon L^2}.
\]
Together with the already settled case \(p_x=0\), this proves the light-branch bound for every light cell. % lean: light_exact_mean_bias_le

For the heavy branch, decompose the branch mean armwise by setting
\[
\Gamma_{a,x}
:=
\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}\!\left[
\frac{S_{ax}}u\left(1+\frac{K_{1-a,x}}{K_{ax}+1}\right)
\right],
\qquad a\in\{0,1\},
\]
so that \(\Gamma_x=\Gamma_{1,x}-\Gamma_{0,x}\).  By independence of \(S_{ax}\) from the factorial-pool counts and \(\mathbb E_{\mathbb Q_x}S_{ax}=uq_{ax}\),
\[
\Gamma_{a,x}
=
q_{ax}\,
\mathbb E\!\left[1+\frac{K_{1-a,x}}{K_{ax}+1}\right].
\]
Substituting the cell quantities \(p_x=s_{0x}+s_{1x}\), \(s_{ax}\), \(q_{ax}\), and \(\mu_{ax}=q_{ax}/s_{ax}\) in \cref{obj:lem:poisson-inverse-arm-mean-formula}, and using the total real-division simplification \(u/u=1\) that follows from \(u>0\), gives
\[
q_{ax}\,
\mathbb E\!\left[1+\frac{K_{1-a,x}}{K_{ax}+1}\right]
=
p_x\mu_{ax}-(p_x-s_{ax})\mu_{ax}\exp(-ts_{ax}),
\]
whose left side is \(\Gamma_{a,x}\).
The totalized zero-arm cases are covered as follows: if \(s_{ax}=0\), then \(0\le q_{ax}\le s_{ax}\) gives \(q_{ax}=0\), so \(\Gamma_{a,x}=0\), while \(\mu_{ax}=q_{ax}/s_{ax}=0\) and the right side is also zero.  If \(s_{ax}>0\), the quotient is the ordinary outcome regression and the same identity applies directly.  Thus, for each arm,
\[
|\Gamma_{a,x}-p_x\mu_{ax}|
=
(p_x-s_{ax})\mu_{ax}\exp(-ts_{ax}).
\]
If \(p_x=0\), this expression is zero.  If \(p_x>0\), occupied-cell overlap gives \(s_{ax}\ge\epsilon p_x\); together with \(0\le p_x-s_{ax}\le p_x\) and \(0\le\mu_{ax}\le1\), this yields
\[
|\Gamma_{a,x}-p_x\mu_{ax}|
\le
p_x\exp(-\epsilon t p_x).
\]
Summing the two armwise errors gives
\[
|\Gamma_x-\vartheta_x|
\le
2p_x\exp(-\epsilon t p_x).
\]
% lean: integral_hybridHeavyPools_bias_le

We next derive the pilot-selection estimate used on light cells. Since the two pilot arm counts are independent Poisson variables with means
\(t_p s_{0x}\) and \(t_p s_{1x}\), their sum satisfies
\[
J_x=J_{0x}^{\mathrm p}+J_{1x}^{\mathrm p}
\sim
\operatorname{Poi}\bigl(t_p(s_{0x}+s_{1x})\bigr)
=
\operatorname{Poi}(t_pp_x).
\]
Let
\[
\lambda_x^{\mathrm p}:=t_p p_x.
\]
For any
\(\alpha_{\mathrm{tail}}>0\), the exponential Markov bound applied to
\((1+\alpha_{\mathrm{tail}})^{J_x}\) gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\Pr\{J_x>k_0\}\exp(-\alpha_{\mathrm{tail}}\lambda_x^{\mathrm p})
&\le
\frac{\mathbb E(1+\alpha_{\mathrm{tail}})^{J_x}}
     {(1+\alpha_{\mathrm{tail}})^{k_0}}
\exp(-\alpha_{\mathrm{tail}}\lambda_x^{\mathrm p}) \\
&=
\left(1+\alpha_{\mathrm{tail}}\right)^{-k_0},
\end{aligned}
\]
because the Poisson probability generating function gives
\(\mathbb E(1+\alpha_{\mathrm{tail}})^{J_x}
=\exp(\alpha_{\mathrm{tail}}\lambda_x^{\mathrm p})\).  Taking
\(\alpha_{\mathrm{tail}}=\epsilon t/t_p\), which is positive since the
calibration predicate gives \(t_p>0\) and \(t>0\), yields
\[
\eta_x\exp(-\epsilon t p_x)
\le
\left(1+\epsilon\frac{t}{t_p}\right)^{-k_0}.
\]
By \(\bar\epsilon\le\epsilon\) from \cref{obj:synth_7} and \(t/t_p\ge0\),
\[
\left(1+\bar\epsilon\frac{t}{t_p}\right)^{k_0}
\le
\left(1+\epsilon\frac{t}{t_p}\right)^{k_0}.
\]
The calibration inequality in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} gives
\[
n^{12}\le\left(1+\bar\epsilon\frac{t}{t_p}\right)^{k_0},
\]
and hence, with positive denominators,
\[
\left(1+\epsilon\frac{t}{t_p}\right)^{-k_0}
\le
n^{-12}.
\]
Combining these estimates with the mixture identity yields, for \(p_x\le B\),
\[
|M_x-\vartheta_x|
\le
\frac{2B}{\epsilon L^2}+2p_x n^{-12}.
\]
% lean: calibrated_light_cell_heavy_probability_mul_exp_le

Now consider a heavy cell, \(B<p_x\).  Put
\[
\lambda_x^{\mathrm p}:=t_p p_x.
\]
We derive the light-pilot lower-tail estimate directly.  By the definition above, \(J_x=J_{0x}^{\mathrm p}+J_{1x}^{\mathrm p}\), and independence of the two pilot arm counts gives
\[
J_x\sim\operatorname{Poi}\bigl(t_p(s_{0x}+s_{1x})\bigr)
=
\operatorname{Poi}(\lambda_x^{\mathrm p}).
\]
Moreover, \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} defines the pilot threshold by
\[
k_0=\left\lfloor\frac{t_pB}{4}\right\rfloor.
\]
Because \(t_pB/4\ge0\), the defining floor inequality, after casting the
natural-valued threshold \(k_0\) to the reals, gives \(k_0\le t_pB/4\).
The heavy-cell inequality \(B<p_x\), multiplied by the positive number
\(t_p/4\), gives \(t_pB/4< t_pp_x/4=\lambda_x^{\mathrm p}/4\).  Thus
\[
k_0\le \frac{t_pB}{4}<\frac{\lambda_x^{\mathrm p}}4.
\]
With \(\theta=\log 2\), apply exponential Markov directly to
\(e^{-\theta J_x}\).  Since the event \(J_x\le k_0\) is the same as
\(e^{-\theta J_x}\ge e^{-\theta k_0}\), with the natural number \(k_0\)
cast to a real in the exponential, the floor inequality above gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\pi_x
&=\Pr\{J_x\le k_0\} \\
&\le
\exp\!\left(\theta k_0
+\lambda_x^{\mathrm p}(e^{-\theta}-1)\right) \\
&\le
\exp\!\left(\frac{\theta\lambda_x^{\mathrm p}}4
+\lambda_x^{\mathrm p}(e^{-\theta}-1)\right) \\
&=
\exp\!\left(\left(\frac{\theta}{4}-\frac12\right)\lambda_x^{\mathrm p}\right)
\le
\exp\!\left(-\frac{\lambda_x^{\mathrm p}}4\right),
\end{aligned}
\]
since \(e^{-\theta}=1/2\) and \(\theta\le1\).  The tail-calibration inequalities in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} include
\[
15L+12b_n\le \frac{t_pB}{4},
\]
so
\[
12b_n\le 15L+12b_n\le \frac{t_pB}{4}<\frac{\lambda_x^{\mathrm p}}4.
\]
Consequently
\[
\pi_x\le \exp(-12b_n)\le n^{-12},
\]
where the last comparison is the binary-length consequence recorded at the start of the proof. % lean: calibrated_heavy_cell_pilot_le

We next prove the auxiliary estimate for the wrong-light contribution on the same heavy cell:
\[
\pi_x|\Lambda_x|\le p_x n^{-12}.
\]
Put
\[
y_x:=\frac{p_x}{B},
\]
so \(y_x\ge1\).  First we bound the absolute light-branch mean.  The first-moment identity gives
\[
\Lambda_x
=
q_{1x}\frac{p_x}{B}G_L\!\left(\frac{s_{1x}}B\right)
-
q_{0x}\frac{p_x}{B}G_L\!\left(\frac{s_{0x}}B\right).
\]
For each arm, \(0\le s_{ax}\le p_x\) and \(0\le q_{ax}\le p_x\).  If
\[
G_L(z)=\sum_{\rho=0}^{L-2} g_\rho z^\rho,
\]
then the degree bound in \cref{obj:lem:chebyshev-factorial-certificate} justifies the displayed finite expansion with no terms beyond degree \(L-2\), while \cref{obj:lem:explicit-chebyshev-calibration} gives
\(2\|G_L\|_1\le14^L\).  Since \(0\le s_{ax}/B\le y_x\) and \(y_x\ge1\), each displayed exponent satisfies
\[
\left(\frac{s_{ax}}B\right)^\rho\le y_x^\rho\le(1+y_x)^\rho\le(1+y_x)^L.
\]
Multiplying this monomial bound by \(|g_\rho|\) and summing over the displayed finite support gives
\[
\left|G_L\!\left(\frac{s_{ax}}B\right)\right|
\le \sum_{\rho=0}^{L-2}|g_\rho|\left(\frac{s_{ax}}B\right)^\rho
\le \|G_L\|_1(1+y_x)^L.
\]
Also \(y_x\le(1+y_x)^L\), because \(L\ge2\) and \(y_x\ge0\).  Therefore
\[
|\Lambda_x|
\le
2p_xy_x\|G_L\|_1(1+y_x)^L
\le
p_x\,14^L(1+y_x)^{2L}.
\]
This coefficient-envelope argument uses the exact first-moment identity above, together with the degree and coefficient bounds for \(G_L\), and yields the displayed bound without invoking any second-moment estimate.
% lean: abs_integral_hybridLightPools_heavy_le
The lower-tail calculation above, kept in exponential form, gives
\[
\pi_x\le\exp\!\left(-\frac{t_pp_x}{4}\right).
\]
The explicit scale definition in \cref{obj:synth_7} gives
\[
B=\frac{H_\epsilon L}{\min(t_p,t)}.
\]
Since \(t_p>0\) and \(t>0\), their minimum is positive and the scale identity
gives \(H_\epsilon L=B\min(t_p,t)\).  As \(\min(t_p,t)\le t_p\) and
\(B>0\), multiplication preserves the inequality, so \(H_\epsilon L\le
t_pB\).  Thus, with \(p_x=By_x\),
\[
\exp\!\left(-\frac{t_pp_x}{4}\right)
\le
\exp\!\left(-\frac{H_\epsilon Ly_x}{4}\right).
\]
The environment \cref{obj:synth_7} gives \(A_\star=7056\),
\(c^\circ=1/256\), and
\[
\frac{H_\epsilon}{4}\ge 15+\frac{24}{c^\circ}.
\]
Elementary arithmetic applied to the displayed value of \(A_\star\) gives
\(14\le A_\star<e^{13}\).  Using these consequences, \(y_x\ge1\), and
\(\log(1+y_x)\le y_x\), we obtain
\[
L\log A_\star+2L\log(1+y_x)-\frac{H_\epsilon Ly_x}{4}
\le
