13Ly_x+2Ly_x-\left(15+\frac{24}{c^\circ}\right)Ly_x
=
-\frac{24Ly_x}{c^\circ}.
\]
Exponentiating gives the calibrated absorption bound
\[
A_\star^L(1+y_x)^{2L}
\exp\!\left(-\frac{H_\epsilon Ly_x}{4}\right)
\le
\exp\!\left(-\frac{24Ly_x}{c^\circ}\right).
\]
Combining the envelope for \(\Lambda_x\), the pilot-tail display, and \(14^L\le A_\star^L\),
\[
\pi_x|\Lambda_x|
\le
p_x\exp\!\left(-\frac{24Ly_x}{c^\circ}\right).
\]
Finally, the degree lower bound \(c^\circ b_n/2\le L\) in \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\), listed in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}, together with \(y_x\ge1\), implies
\[
12b_n\le \frac{24Ly_x}{c^\circ}.
\]
The binary-length consequence recorded at the start of the proof gives
\[
\exp\!\left(-\frac{24Ly_x}{c^\circ}\right)
\le
\exp(-12b_n)
\le
n^{-12}.
\]
Combining the last two displays proves
\[
\pi_x|\Lambda_x|\le p_xn^{-12}.
\]
% lean: calibrated_heavy_cell_light_mean_le

The intrinsic heavy-branch exponential is also absorbed on a heavy cell:
\[
\exp(-\epsilon t p_x)
\le
n^{-12}.
\]
Indeed, \(B<p_x\), \(\bar\epsilon\le\epsilon\), and the tail-calibration inequality in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle},
\[
12b_n\le \bar\epsilon tB,
\]
give \(12b_n\le \epsilon t p_x\), and hence, by the binary-length consequence recorded at the start of the proof,
\[
\exp(-\epsilon t p_x)\le \exp(-12b_n)\le n^{-12}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\eta_x|\Gamma_x-\vartheta_x|
\le
2p_xn^{-12}.
\]
Together with \(\pi_x|\vartheta_x|\le p_xn^{-12}\), the decomposition
\[
M_x-\vartheta_x
=
\pi_x\Lambda_x-\pi_x\vartheta_x+\eta_x(\Gamma_x-\vartheta_x)
\]
gives, for \(B<p_x\),
\[
|M_x-\vartheta_x|
\le
4p_xn^{-12}.
\]
% lean: calibrated_heavy_cell_bias_exp_le

The alternatives \(p_x\le B\) and \(B<p_x\) are disjoint and exhaustive, so
they partition \([d]\).  Summing the two cellwise bounds over these two regimes,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\left|
\sum_{x\in[d]}M_x-\tau(P)
\right|
&=
\left|
\sum_{x\in[d]}\bigl(M_x-\vartheta_x\bigr)
\right|  \\
&\le
\sum_{x\in[d]}
\left|M_x-\vartheta_x\right| \\
&\le
\sum_{x:\,p_x\le B}
\left(\frac{2B}{\epsilon L^2}+2p_xn^{-12}\right)
+
\sum_{x:\,B<p_x}4p_xn^{-12} \\
&\le
\frac{2dB}{\epsilon L^2}
+4n^{-12}\sum_{x\in[d]}p_x \\
&=
\frac{2dB}{\epsilon L^2}+4n^{-12}
\le
\frac{2dB}{\epsilon L^2}+6n^{-12}.
\end{aligned}
\]
This is the claimed bound. % lean: calibrated_hybrid_mean_bias_le
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:calibrated-hybrid-mean-bias} gives the matching aggregate bias control for the selected cell means. The bound contains the light-cell Chebyshev approximation term and a polynomially small pilot-boundary residual, which together form the squared bias term in \cref{obj:lem:calibrated-envelope-absorption}.

\Cref{obj:lem:calibrated-envelope-absorption} is the bookkeeping step that turns the local hybrid analysis into the global rate scale. Its left-hand side collects the light-cell factorial moment contribution, the heavy-cell inverse-count contribution, the Chebyshev approximation residual, the pilot-boundary term, and the finite prefix terms, while the right-hand side matches the two summands in \cref{obj:def:frontier-rate-handle}.

The calibrated hybrid statistic combines the light-cell polynomial with the all-heavy inverse-count weight. The construction below is the Poissonized counterpart of the finite-sample mixed estimator introduced in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_9}[Calibrated Poisson hybrid statistic]
Use the split sizes, intensities, constants, dyadic overlap certificate, and calibration predicate \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) from \(\cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}\). In the independent Poisson experiment with outcome-prefix length \(D_0\sim\operatorname{Poi}(u)\), pilot-prefix length \(D_p\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t_p)\), and factorial-prefix length \(D_f\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t)\), define \(Z^{\mathrm{mix}}\) as follows. If \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) is false, set
\[
Z^{\mathrm{mix}}:=0 .
\]
If \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) is true, return zero on the event that any Poisson prefix exceeds its corresponding pool. On the complementary valid-prefix event, let \(J_x\) be the pilot count in cell \(x\), let \(K_{ax}\) be the factorial-pool count in arm \(a\) and cell \(x\), and let \(S_{ax}\) be the outcome-block success count in arm \(a\) and cell \(x\). With
\[
H_L(z)=\frac{1-T_L(1-2z)}{2L^2},\qquad
E_L(z)=\frac{H_L(z)}{z},\qquad
G_L(z)=\frac{1-E_L(z)}{z},
\]
under polynomial continuation at zero, expand
\[
W_{a,L}(s_0,s_1)=\frac{s_0+s_1}{B}G_L(s_a/B)
=\sum_{r_0,r_1}c_{a,r_0,r_1}s_0^{r_0}s_1^{r_1}.
\]
For falling factorials \((k)_r\), put
\[
\widehat W_{a,L,x}
=\sum_{r_0,r_1}c_{a,r_0,r_1}
\frac{(K_{0x})_{r_0}(K_{1x})_{r_1}}{t^{r_0+r_1}},
\qquad
\widehat W^H_{a,x}
=\frac{K_{0x}+K_{1x}+1}{K_{ax}+1},
\]
and define the cell contribution
\[
Z_x=
\mathbf1\{J_x\le k_0\}
\left(\frac{S_{1x}}u\widehat W_{1,L,x}
-\frac{S_{0x}}u\widehat W_{0,L,x}\right)
+\mathbf1\{J_x>k_0\}
\left(\frac{S_{1x}}u\widehat W^H_{1,x}
-\frac{S_{0x}}u\widehat W^H_{0,x}\right).
\]
The calibrated Poisson hybrid statistic is the clipped aggregate
\[
Z^{\mathrm{mix}}
:=
\operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]}\!\left(\sum_{x=1}^d Z_x\right)
\]
on the valid-prefix event, together with the preceding zero values on invalid prefixes or failed calibration.
\end{definitionv}

The pilot threshold in \cref{obj:synth_9} chooses between two weight regimes at the cell level. Cells with small pilot counts use the bounded-degree Chebyshev lift, while cells with larger pilot counts use the inverse-count weight from \cref{obj:lem:poisson-inverse-count-risk}; clipping keeps the aggregate on the natural scale of the treatment effect.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:poisson-hybrid-risk}[Hybrid Poisson risk]
Fix an overlap constant \(\epsilon\) with \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).  There is a constant \(C_\epsilon\in(0,\infty)\), depending only on \(\epsilon\), such that the following holds for every \(n,m,d\) and every observed-data law \(P\):
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Sample size.)} \(n\ge 1\).
\item \textbf{(Alphabet size.)} \(d\ge 2\).
\item \textbf{(Model class.)} \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), as in \cref{obj:def:model-class}.
\end{itemize}
Let \(M_0=\lfloor n/3\rfloor\), \(n_p=\lfloor(n-M_0)/2\rfloor\), \(n_f=n-M_0-n_p\), \(m_p=\lfloor m/2\rfloor\), and \(m_f=m-m_p\), and set
\[
u=\frac{M_0}{8},
\qquad
t_p=\frac{n_p+m_p}{8},
\qquad
t=\frac{n_f+m_f}{8}.
\]
In the independent Poisson count experiment with these intensities, let \(Z^{\mathrm{mix}}\) be the calibrated hybrid statistic when the finite calibration predicate \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) holds and the constant \(0\) otherwise.  Then its mean-squared error satisfies
\[
\mathbb E_P\!\left[\bigl(Z^{\mathrm{mix}}-\tau(P)\bigr)^2\right]
\le
C_\epsilon\, r_\epsilon(n,m,d),
\]
where \(r_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) is the frontier rate in \cref{obj:def:frontier-rate-handle}, equivalently
\[
r_\epsilon(n,m,d)
=
\min\!\left\{
1,\,
\frac1n+
\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2\log^2(en)}
\right\}.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:poisson-hybrid-risk}]
Fix \(\epsilon\in(0,1/2)\).  Throughout, let \(M_0,n_p,n_f,m_p,m_f,u,t_p,t,L,B,k_0\) be the block sizes, Poisson intensities, degree, scale, and pilot threshold from \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}.  Write
\[
\operatorname{rad}_{n,m,d}
=
\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2\log^2(en)} ,
\]
so that \(r_\epsilon(n,m,d)=\min\{1,\operatorname{rad}_{n,m,d}\}\) by \cref{obj:def:frontier-rate-handle}.

\(\mathbf{1.}\) First record the uniform bounded-loss estimate.  For \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), the functional \(\tau(P)\) lies in \([-1,1]\), since it is a cell-mass average of differences of binary outcome regressions.  On the calibrated branch, the hybrid statistic is clipped to \([-1,1]\), so its squared distance from \(\tau(P)\) is at most \(4\) pointwise.  On the uncalibrated branch, the risk term is the deterministic quantity \(\tau(P)^2\), which is at most \(1\) and hence at most \(4\).  Therefore, for all \(n,m,d\),
\[
\mathbb E_P\!\left[(Z^{\mathrm{mix}}-\tau(P))^2\right]\le 4 .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.poissonHybridMSE_le_four

\(\mathbf{2.}\) We next record the calibrated variance envelope used when \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) holds.  For each \(x\in[d]\), let \(S_{ax}\), \(J_{ax}\), and \(K_{ax}\) be the independent outcome-block, pilot-pool, and factorial-pool Poisson counts from \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}, and put \(J_x=J_{0x}+J_{1x}\).  Let \(P_x\) and \(H_x\) be the light-cell polynomial and heavy inverse-count cell contributions from \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}, and define the unclipped selected hybrid sum
\[
\widetilde Z
=
\sum_{x=1}^d
\left(
\mathbf 1\{J_x\le k_0\}P_x
+
\mathbf 1\{J_x>k_0\}H_x
\right).
\]
By \cref{obj:lem:calibrated-hybrid-variance-bound}, there is a constant \(C_{\mathrm{var}}>0\), depending only on \(\epsilon\), such that, whenever \(n\ge1\), \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), and \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) holds,
\[
\operatorname{Var}_P(\widetilde Z)
\le
4A_\star^L
\left\{
\frac{\min\{1,dB\}}u+dB^2
\right\}
+
C_{\mathrm{var}}\left(u^{-1}+t^{-1}\right)
+
\frac{8dB^2}{\epsilon^2L^4}
+
\frac4{\epsilon t}
+
414\,n^{-12}.
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.calibrated_hybrid_variance_le

\(\mathbf{3.}\) Still under \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\), projection onto \([-1,1]\) cannot increase squared distance from \(\tau(P)\in[-1,1]\).  Hence
\[
\mathbb E_P\!\left[(Z^{\mathrm{mix}}-\tau(P))^2\right]
\le
\mathbb E_P\!\left[(\widetilde Z-\tau(P))^2\right]
=
\operatorname{Var}_P(\widetilde Z)
+
\bigl(\mathbb E_P\widetilde Z-\tau(P)\bigr)^2 .
\]
For the same hybrid branches, \cref{obj:lem:calibrated-hybrid-mean-bias} gives
\[
\left|\mathbb E_P\widetilde Z-\tau(P)\right|
\le
\frac{2dB}{\epsilon L^2}+6n^{-12}.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\mathbb E_P\!\left[(Z^{\mathrm{mix}}-\tau(P))^2\right]
\le
\operatorname{Var}_P(\widetilde Z)
+
\left(
\frac{2dB}{\epsilon L^2}+6n^{-12}
\right)^2 .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.poissonHybridMSE_le_variance_add_bias_sq

\(\mathbf{4.}\) Apply \cref{obj:lem:calibrated-envelope-absorption} with \(C_0=C_{\mathrm{var}}\).  That result gives a constant depending on \(\epsilon\) and \(C_{\mathrm{var}}\); since Step 2 chose \(C_{\mathrm{var}}\) depending only on \(\epsilon\), we may write this constant as \(C_{\mathrm{abs}}>0\), depending only on \(\epsilon\).  Under \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
&4A_\star^L
\left\{
\frac{\min\{1,dB\}}u+dB^2
\right\}
+
C_{\mathrm{var}}\left(u^{-1}+t^{-1}\right)
+
\frac{8dB^2}{\epsilon^2L^4}
+
\frac4{\epsilon t}
+
414\,n^{-12}
+
\left(
\frac{2dB}{\epsilon L^2}+6n^{-12}
\right)^2
\\
&\hspace{5em}\le
C_{\mathrm{abs}}\operatorname{rad}_{n,m,d}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Set
\[
C_{\mathrm{cal}}=\max\{4,C_{\mathrm{abs}}\}.
\]
If \(1\le \operatorname{rad}_{n,m,d}\), then \(r_\epsilon(n,m,d)=1\), and Step 1 gives
\[
\mathbb E_P\!\left[(Z^{\mathrm{mix}}-\tau(P))^2\right]
\le 4
\le C_{\mathrm{cal}}\,r_\epsilon(n,m,d).
\]
If \(\operatorname{rad}_{n,m,d}\le1\), then \(r_\epsilon(n,m,d)=\operatorname{rad}_{n,m,d}\).  Steps 2 and 3, followed by the absorption display, give
\[
\mathbb E_P\!\left[(Z^{\mathrm{mix}}-\tau(P))^2\right]
\le
C_{\mathrm{abs}}\operatorname{rad}_{n,m,d}
\le
C_{\mathrm{cal}}\,r_\epsilon(n,m,d).
\]
Thus the desired bound holds on the calibrated branch with constant \(C_{\mathrm{cal}}\).
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.calibrated_envelope_absorption_exists

\(\mathbf{5.}\) It remains to cover the finite set of uncalibrated sample sizes.  The uniform calibration-failure bound gives an integer \(n_\epsilon^\dagger\ge1\), depending only on \(\epsilon\), such that
\[
\neg\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad n<n_\epsilon^\dagger
\qquad\text{for all }n,m .
\]
Now set
\[
C_\epsilon=\max\{C_{\mathrm{cal}},\,4n_\epsilon^\dagger\}.
\]
If \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) holds, Step 4 gives the result because \(C_{\mathrm{cal}}\le C_\epsilon\).  If \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) fails, then \(n<n_\epsilon^\dagger\), so \(n\le n_\epsilon^\dagger\).  Since \(n\ge1\), the frontier rate is positive and dominates \(n^{-1}\): the second entry in the minimum is
\[
\frac1n+\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2\log^2(en)}\ge \frac1n,
\]
and \(1/n\le1\), so
\[
\frac1n\le r_\epsilon(n,m,d).
\]
Multiplying by the positive number \(n\) gives \(1\le n\,r_\epsilon(n,m,d)\).  Since \(n\le n_\epsilon^\dagger\) and \(r_\epsilon(n,m,d)>0\),
\[
1\le n\,r_\epsilon(n,m,d)
\le n_\epsilon^\dagger r_\epsilon(n,m,d).
\]
Combining this with Step 1 gives
\[
\mathbb E_P\!\left[(Z^{\mathrm{mix}}-\tau(P))^2\right]
\le4
\le
4n_\epsilon^\dagger r_\epsilon(n,m,d)
\le
C_\epsilon r_\epsilon(n,m,d).
\]
This proves the asserted bound for every \(n,m,d\) and every \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\).
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.poisson_hybrid_risk
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:poisson-hybrid-risk} establishes the Poisson risk bound at exactly the split sizes and intensities used by the finite estimator. The bound has the same two components as the rate in \cref{obj:def:frontier-rate-handle}: labeled outcomes contribute the \(1/n\) term, and treatment--covariate information contributes the alphabet-dependent term through the pilot and factorial pools.

\paragraph{Uniform finite-calibration input.}
The uncalibrated branch uses the formally checked helper
\(\texttt{calibrationPredicate\_failure\_bounded}\): for fixed \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), there is \(n_\epsilon^\dagger\ge1\) such that
\[
\neg\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad n<n_\epsilon^\dagger
\qquad\text{for every }m.
\]
This is the contrapositive of eventual validity of all finitely many calibration inequalities, uniformly over the auxiliary sample size. It reduces calibration failure to finitely many labeled sizes, where clipping gives risk at most \(4\) and \(r_\epsilon(n,m,d)\ge1/n\), so \(4\le4n_\epsilon^\dagger r_\epsilon(n,m,d)\).

It remains to connect the independent Poisson count analysis to the fixed sample sizes in the annotation experiment. The next definition packages the three independent Poisson pools used in that transfer.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_6}[Three-pool Poisson count law]
Fix an observed-data law \(P\), intensities \(u,t_p,t\ge0\), and write
\[
q_{ax}=P(X=x,A=a,Y=1),
\qquad
s_{ax}=P(X=x,A=a).
\]
The law \(\mathbb P^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{P,u,t_p,t}\) is the joint distribution of independent count triples
\[
(S_{ax},J_{ax},K_{ax})_{a\in\{0,1\},\,x\in[d]},
\]
where
\[
S_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(u q_{ax}),\qquad
J_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t_p s_{ax}),\qquad
K_{ax}\sim\operatorname{Poi}(t s_{ax}).
\]
Here \(S_{ax}\) is the outcome-success count from the outcome pool, \(J_{ax}\) is the pilot count, and \(K_{ax}\) is the factorial-pool treatment-covariate count. The product law makes all these counts independent across cells, arms, and pools.
\end{definitionv}

The three-pool law in \cref{obj:synth_6} mirrors the deterministic sample split in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}. Each pool has its own intensity, so the Poisson calculation can track outcome information, pilot classification, and polynomial weight estimation separately.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:fixed-sample-hybrid-transfer}[Fixed-sample hybrid transfer]
There is a universal constant \(C>0\) such that the following holds.  Let
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Overlap level.)} The overlap level \(\epsilon\) satisfies \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).
\item \textbf{(Sample sizes and alphabet.)} \(n\ge 1\), \(m\ge 0\), and \(d\ge 2\).
\item \textbf{(Model class.)} \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), with \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) as in \cref{obj:def:model-class}.
\end{itemize}
For the mixed estimator \(\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}\) in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}, the estimator is measurable as a function of the annotation sample and
\[
\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}}
\left[
  \left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2
\right]
\le
2\,\mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon)
+\frac{C}{n}.
\]
Here \(\tau(P)\) is the average treatment effect functional in \cref{obj:def:ate-functional}.  Writing
\(M_0=\lfloor n/3\rfloor\), \(n_p=\lfloor(n-M_0)/2\rfloor\),
\(n_f=n-M_0-n_p\), \(m_p=\lfloor m/2\rfloor\), \(m_f=m-m_p\),
\(M_p=n_p+m_p\), \(M_f=n_f+m_f\), \(u=M_0/8\), \(t_p=M_p/8\), and
\(t=M_f/8\), the Poisson hybrid risk is
\[
\mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon)
=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle
\int
\left\{
  Z^{\mathrm{mix}}(K)-\tau(P)
\right\}^2
\,d\mathbb P^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{P,u,t_p,t}(K),
&
\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m),\\[1.2em]
\displaystyle
\tau(P)^2,
&
\text{otherwise,}
\end{cases}
\]
where \(Z^{\mathrm{mix}}\) is the calibrated hybrid statistic of \cref{obj:synth_9}.
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:fixed-sample-hybrid-transfer}]
Take \(C=384\).  Fix \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), \(d\ge2\), and \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\).  The estimator is measurable because it is either the constant zero map or, on the calibrated branch of \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}, a finite linear combination of measurable prefix-count statistics.  It remains to prove the displayed risk bound. % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.measurable_mixedEstimator

1. Suppose first that \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) holds.  Put
\[
M_0=\lfloor n/3\rfloor,\qquad
M_p=n_p+m_p,\qquad
M_f=n_f+m_f,
\]
and
\[
u=\frac{M_0}{8},\qquad t_p=\frac{M_p}{8},\qquad t=\frac{M_f}{8}.
\]
Let \(\mathbb P_3\) be the three-pool product law whose complete-record coordinate has law \(P\) on
\([d]\times\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}\), while the two shared coordinates have law \(P_{XA}\) on
\([d]\times\{0,1\}\).  Let \(\boldsymbol\lambda=(u,t_p,t)\) and let the capacity vector be
\(\boldsymbol M=(M_0,M_p,M_f)\).  For a three-prefix array
\[
\mathfrak s=(\mathfrak s_0,\mathfrak s_p,\mathfrak s_f),
\]
define
\[
\Psi_{n,m,d,\epsilon}(\mathfrak s)
=
Z^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,\epsilon,u,t}
\bigl(\operatorname{hist}(\mathfrak s_0),
      \operatorname{hist}(\mathfrak s_p),
      \operatorname{hist}(\mathfrak s_f)\bigr),
\]
where the first histogram records complete outcome-labeled observations and the last two record treatment-covariate observations.  This prefix statistic is measurable and satisfies
\[
-1\le \Psi_{n,m,d,\epsilon}(\mathfrak s)\le 1
\quad\text{for every }\mathfrak s,
\qquad
-1\le \tau(P)\le 1,
\]
the first bound coming from clipping in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} and the second from \cref{obj:def:ate-functional} together with occupied-cell overlap in \cref{obj:def:model-class}. % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.finitePrefixHybridStatistic_mem_Icc; CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.ateFunctional_mem_Icc_neg_one_one

2. For fixed pools
\[
\mathfrak p=(\mathfrak p_0,\mathfrak p_p,\mathfrak p_f)
\in
\bigl([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\bigr)^{M_0}
\times
\bigl([d]\times\{0,1\}\bigr)^{M_p}
\times
\bigl([d]\times\{0,1\}\bigr)^{M_f},
\]
define the Rao--Blackwellized prefix statistic by
\[
\operatorname{RB}(\mathfrak p)
=
\sum_{r_0,r_p,r_f\ge0}
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(u)=r_0\}
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t_p)=r_p\}
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t)=r_f\}
\,\Psi^\circ(\mathfrak p;r_0,r_p,r_f),
\]
where
\[
\Psi^\circ(\mathfrak p;r_0,r_p,r_f)
=
\begin{cases}
\Psi_{n,m,d,\epsilon}
\bigl(\mathfrak p_0[1{:}r_0],
      \mathfrak p_p[1{:}r_p],
      \mathfrak p_f[1{:}r_f]\bigr),
& r_0\le M_0,\ r_p\le M_p,\ r_f\le M_f,\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}
\end{cases}
\]
Under \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\), expanding the finite sums in \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} gives the pointwise identity
\[
\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}(\mathcal L_n,\mathcal U_m)
=
\operatorname{RB}\bigl(\mathfrak P(\mathcal L_n,\mathcal U_m)\bigr),
\]
where \(\mathfrak P(\mathcal L_n,\mathcal U_m)\) is the deterministic three-pool split formed from the first \(M_0\) complete labeled records, the next \(n_p\) labeled records projected to \((X,A)\) together with the first \(m_p\) auxiliary records, and the remaining \(n_f\) projected labeled records together with the remaining \(m_f\) auxiliary records.  By \cref{obj:def:annotation-experiment}, this split has law equal to the fixed-pool law with coordinate law \(\mathbb P_3\) and capacities \(\boldsymbol M\).  Hence
\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
\left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2
\right]
=
\int
\bigl\{\operatorname{RB}(\mathfrak p)-\tau(P)\bigr\}^2
\,d\mathbb P_{\mathrm{fix}}(\mathfrak p).
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.prefixRaoBlackwellStatistic_hybridFixedPools; CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.hybridFixedPools_map_annotationLaw

3. The finite-pool-to-Poisson comparison used here is the following.  For any prefix statistic with values in an interval \([a,b]\), any target and overflow value in \([a,b]\), and independent Poisson requested lengths with means \(\boldsymbol\lambda\),
\[
\int
\bigl\{\operatorname{RB}(\mathfrak p)-\theta\bigr\}^2
\,d\mathbb P_{\mathrm{fix}}(\mathfrak p)
\le
\int
\bigl\{\Psi(\mathfrak s)-\theta\bigr\}^2
\,d\mathbb P_{\mathrm{Pois}}(\mathfrak s)
+
(b-a)^2
\sum_{\iota\in\{0,p,f\}}
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(\lambda_\iota)>M_\iota\}.
\]
Indeed, \(\operatorname{RB}\) is the conditional expectation of the capped randomized-prefix statistic given the fixed pools, so Jensen's inequality bounds its squared risk by the capped randomized-prefix risk.  Off the overflow event the capped and uncapped prefixes agree; on overflow both the capped value and the target lie in \([a,b]\), so the extra squared loss is bounded by \((b-a)^2\) times the overflow probability.  Applying this with \(a=-1\), \(b=1\), \(\theta=\tau(P)\), overflow value \(0\), and \(\Psi=\Psi_{n,m,d,\epsilon}\) gives
\[
\int
\bigl\{\operatorname{RB}(\mathfrak p)-\tau(P)\bigr\}^2
\,d\mathbb P_{\mathrm{fix}}(\mathfrak p)
\le
\int
\bigl\{\Psi_{n,m,d,\epsilon}(\mathfrak s)-\tau(P)\bigr\}^2
\,d\mathbb P_{\mathrm{Pois}}(\mathfrak s)
+
4\bigl[
\pi_0+\pi_p+\pi_f
\bigr],
\]
where
\[
\pi_0=\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(u)>M_0\},\qquad
\pi_p=\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t_p)>M_p\},\qquad
\pi_f=\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(t)>M_f\}.
\]
The image of the independent Poisson-prefix law under the three histograms is exactly
\(\mathbb P^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{P,u,t_p,t}\), and therefore
\[
\int
\bigl\{\Psi_{n,m,d,\epsilon}(\mathfrak s)-\tau(P)\bigr\}^2
\,d\mathbb P_{\mathrm{Pois}}(\mathfrak s)
=
\mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon)
\]
on the calibrated branch. % lean: Causalean.Stat.FiniteRaoBlackwell.IndependentPoissonPrefix.FiniteFamily.finiteAlphabet_threePoolSharedLaw_risk_le; CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.hybridPrefixCounts_map_eq_hybridPoissonCountLaw

4. It remains to bound the three overflow probabilities.  The calibration predicate gives \(24\le n\).  From the definitions of the integer blocks,
\[
n\le4M_0,\qquad n\le4M_p,\qquad n\le4M_f,
\]
and all three capacities are positive.  For any positive integer \(M_\bullet\) satisfying \(n\le4M_\bullet\),
\[
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(M_\bullet/8)>M_\bullet\}
\le
\frac{8}{M_\bullet}
\le
\frac{32}{n}.
\]
To prove the first inequality, let \(X_\bullet\sim\operatorname{Poi}(M_\bullet/8)\).  The overflow event is contained in the self-normalized Bernstein event at parameter \(M_\bullet/4\), because
\[
\sqrt{2\cdot\frac{M_\bullet}{8}\cdot\frac{M_\bullet}{4}}
=\frac{M_\bullet}{4}
\]
and \(X_\bullet>M_\bullet\) implies
\[
X_\bullet-\frac{M_\bullet}{8}
>\frac{7M_\bullet}{8}
>\frac{M_\bullet}{4}+2\cdot\frac{M_\bullet}{4}.
\]
The Poisson Bernstein bound for this larger event gives
\[
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(M_\bullet/8)>M_\bullet\}
\le
\exp(-M_\bullet/4)
=
\frac1{\exp(M_\bullet/4)}
\le
\frac{4}{M_\bullet}
\le
\frac{8}{M_\bullet},
\]
where \(1+M_\bullet/4\le \exp(M_\bullet/4)\) gives the penultimate inequality.
Consequently
\[
\pi_0+\pi_p+\pi_f\le \frac{96}{n},
\]
and the preceding display yields
\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
\left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2
\right]
\le
\mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon)
+\frac{384}{n}.
\]
Since \(\mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon)\ge0\), this is at most
\[
2\,\mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon)
+\frac{384}{n}.
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.poisson_eighth_tail; CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.fixed_sample_hybrid_transfer

5. If \(\mathsf C_\epsilon(n,m)\) fails, then \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle} sets
\(\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}=0\), while the definition of the hybrid Poisson risk gives
\[
\mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon)=\tau(P)^2.
\]
Because the annotation law is a probability measure,
\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
\left\{\widehat\tau^{\mathrm{mix}}_{n,m,d}-\tau(P)\right\}^2
\right]
=
\tau(P)^2
\le
2\tau(P)^2+\frac{384}{n}
=
2\,\mathcal R^{\mathrm{Pois}}_{\mathrm{hyb}}(P;n,m,\epsilon)+\frac{384}{n},
\]
using \(n\ge1\).  The two cases prove the asserted bound with the universal constant \(C=384\). % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.fixed_sample_hybrid_transfer
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:fixed-sample-hybrid-transfer} converts the Poisson risk calculation into the fixed-\(n,m\) annotation experiment of \cref{obj:def:annotation-experiment}. The additional \(C/n\) term is on the labeled-outcome scale already present in the rate, so combining \cref{obj:lem:poisson-hybrid-risk,obj:lem:fixed-sample-hybrid-transfer} yields the finite-sample upper-bound ingredient used in \cref{obj:thm:uniform-mixed-upper}.

The final calibration statements make the Chebyshev constants explicit. The first controls the coefficient norm of the shifted quotient, and the second records the numerical bounds used in the finite calibration predicate of \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:shifted-chebyshev-quotient-coefficient-bound}[Shifted quotient coefficient bound]
Let \(L\) be a positive integer, and let \(G_L\) be the polynomial continuation at zero of \((1-E_L(z))/z\) from \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}.  For a real polynomial \(p(z)=\sum_i a_i z^i\), write
\[
\|p\|_1=\sum_i |a_i|
\]
for its coefficient \(\ell^1\)-norm.  Then
\[
\|G_L\|_1\le 6^L .
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:shifted-chebyshev-quotient-coefficient-bound}]
Let \(L\ge 1\). We use the notation for \(H_L,E_L,G_L\) from \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}. The phrase ``polynomial continuation at zero'' is used through the quotient identities
\[
zE_L(z)=H_L(z),\qquad zG_L(z)=1-E_L(z),
\]
with equality in \(\mathbb R[z]\); the cancellations below use only that multiplication by the nonzero indeterminate \(z\) is injective on \(\mathbb R[z]\).

\begin{enumerate}
\item Define, for every \(\alpha\in\mathbb N\),
\[
\theta_{L,\alpha}
=
(-1)^\alpha
\frac{2^{2\alpha+3}}{L(L+\alpha+2)}
\binom{L+\alpha+2}{2\alpha+4},
\]
and put
\[
\widetilde G_L(z)
=
\sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}\theta_{L,\alpha}z^\alpha .
\]
We first derive the shifted-Chebyshev coefficient expansion used below.
Write \(Q_r(z)=T_r(1-2z)\).  The initial polynomials and the defining
Chebyshev recurrence give
\[
Q_0(z)=1,\qquad Q_1(z)=1-2z,\qquad
Q_{r+2}(z)=2(1-2z)Q_{r+1}(z)-Q_r(z).
\]
For \(r\in\mathbb N\) and \(\nu\in\mathbb Z\), define
\[
\beta_{r,\nu}:=
\begin{cases}
1, & \nu=0,\\[0.3em]
(-1)^\nu\dfrac{r}{r+\nu}
\binom{r+\nu}{2\nu}4^\nu, & 1\le\nu\le r,\\[0.8em]
0, & \nu<0\text{ or }r<\nu .
\end{cases}
\]
Comparing coefficients in the displayed recurrence reduces the induction to
\[
\beta_{r+2,\nu}
=2\beta_{r+1,\nu}-4\beta_{r+1,\nu-1}-\beta_{r,\nu}.
\]
For \(1\le\nu\le r\), write
\[
A_{r,\nu}:=\frac{r}{r+\nu}\binom{r+\nu}{2\nu}.
\]
Set \(A_{r,0}:=1\).  For \(\nu\ge1\), the factorial form is
\[
A_{r,\nu}
=\frac{r(r+\nu-1)!}{(2\nu)!(r-\nu)!},
\]
After cancelling the common factor
\((-1)^\nu4^\nu\), the coefficient recurrence is
\[
A_{r+2,\nu}
=2A_{r+1,\nu}+A_{r+1,\nu-1}-A_{r,\nu}.
\]
For \(1\le\nu\le r\), multiply this equality by
\((2\nu)!(r+2-\nu)!/(r+\nu-1)!\).  It becomes
\[
\begin{aligned}
&(r+2)(r+\nu+1)(r+\nu)\\
&\quad =
2(r+1)(r+\nu)(r+2-\nu)
+(r+1)(2\nu)(2\nu-1)\\
&\qquad\quad
-r(r+2-\nu)(r+1-\nu),
\end{aligned}
\]
and expanding both sides proves it.  At \(\nu=0\), the term
\(\beta_{r+1,-1}\) is zero by the outside-range convention, and the identity
becomes \(1=2\cdot1-0-1\).  At \(\nu=r+1\) and \(\nu=r+2\), the same
zero convention leaves, respectively,
\[
\begin{aligned}
(-1)^{r+1}(r+2)4^{r+1}
&=2\left((-1)^{r+1}\frac{4^{r+1}}2\right)
-4\left((-1)^r(r+1)4^r\right),\\
(-1)^{r+2}\frac{4^{r+2}}2
&=-4\left((-1)^{r+1}\frac{4^{r+1}}2\right),
\end{aligned}
\]
so the recurrence holds there as well.  Since the formula agrees with \(Q_0,Q_1\),
induction proves, for every real \(z\),
\[
T_L(1-2z)
=
1+
\sum_{1\le \nu\le L}
(-1)^\nu\frac{L}{L+\nu}
\binom{L+\nu}{2\nu}4^\nu z^\nu .
\]
The \(\nu=1\) term is \(-2L^2z\).  For the remaining terms write
\(\nu=\alpha+2\).  Then
\[
(-1)^{\alpha+2}\frac{L}{L+\alpha+2}
\binom{L+\alpha+2}{2\alpha+4}4^{\alpha+2}z^{\alpha+2}
=
2L^2\theta_{L,\alpha}z^{\alpha+2},
\]
so, with \(Q_L(z)=T_L(1-2z)\),
\[
Q_L(z)
=
1-2L^2z+2L^2z^2\widetilde G_L(z).
\]
Using these quotient identities, since \(Q_L(0)=1\), the polynomial
\[
H_L(z)=\frac{1-Q_L(z)}{2L^2}
\]
has zero constant term. Moreover \(Q_L'(0)=-2T_L'(1)=-2L^2\), so \(H_L'(0)=1\), and hence the quotient identity for \(E_L\) gives \(E_L(0)=1\). Thus \(1-E_L\) has zero constant term, as required for the quotient identity defining \(G_L\). Using the displayed formula for \(Q_L\),
\[
H_L(z)
=\frac{1-Q_L(z)}{2L^2}
=z-z^2\widetilde G_L(z)
=z(1-z\widetilde G_L(z)).
\]
Thus
\[
zE_L(z)=z(1-z\widetilde G_L(z)).
\]
Because multiplication by the nonzero indeterminate \(z\) is cancellative in \(\mathbb R[z]\),
\[
E_L(z)=1-z\widetilde G_L(z).
\]
Consequently
\[
zG_L(z)=1-E_L(z)=z\widetilde G_L(z),
\]
and the same cancellation gives
\[
G_L(z)=\widetilde G_L(z)
\]
as polynomials. % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.chebG_eq_explicit

\item Since
\[
\widetilde G_L(z)=\sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}\theta_{L,\alpha}z^\alpha,
\]
every coefficient of \(\widetilde G_L\) outside the range \(0\le \alpha<L-1\) is zero. Hence
\[
\|G_L\|_1
=
\|\widetilde G_L\|_1
=
\sum_{\alpha\in\operatorname{supp}(\widetilde G_L)}
   |[z^\alpha]\widetilde G_L|
\le
\sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}|\theta_{L,\alpha}|.
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.explicitG_support_subset

\item The last sum is the positive coefficient sum at \(1\):
\[
\sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}|\theta_{L,\alpha}|
=
\sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}|\theta_{L,\alpha}|\,1^\alpha .
\]
The coefficient formula also gives
\[
|\theta_{L,\alpha}|
=
\frac{2^{2\alpha+3}}{L(L+\alpha+2)}
\binom{L+\alpha+2}{2\alpha+4},
\]
because \(L>0\) and all factors except \((-1)^\alpha\) are nonnegative. % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.DiscreteAteMinimaxLoggap.gpos

\item It remains to bound this positive coefficient sum. Write
\[
\Theta_L^+
=
\sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}|\theta_{L,\alpha}|.
\]
This is the positive coefficient sum at the argument \(1\). The sign identity
\((-1)^\alpha\theta_{L,\alpha}=|\theta_{L,\alpha}|\) gives
\[
\Theta_L^+
=
\sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}\theta_{L,\alpha}(-1)^\alpha
=
\widetilde G_L(-1).
\]
Substituting \(z=-1\) in the identity
\[
Q_L(z)=1-2L^2z+2L^2z^2\widetilde G_L(z)
\]
and using \(Q_L(-1)=T_L(3)\) gives
\[
T_L(3)=1+2L^2+2L^2\Theta_L^+ .
\]
Since \(\Theta_L^+\ge0\) and \(L\ge1\),
\[
\Theta_L^+
\le
2L^2\Theta_L^+
\le
1+2L^2+2L^2\Theta_L^+
=
T_L(3).
\]
It remains to bound \(T_L(3)\). Put \(a_\nu=T_\nu(3)\). The Chebyshev recurrence gives
\[
a_0=1,\qquad a_1=3,\qquad
a_{\nu+2}=6a_{\nu+1}-a_\nu .
\]
Let \(\lambda_+=3+\sqrt8\) and \(\lambda_-=3-\sqrt8\).  Since
\(\lambda_++\lambda_-=6\) and \(\lambda_+\lambda_-=1\), both roots satisfy
\(x^2=6x-1\).  Consequently
\[
b_\nu:=\frac{\lambda_+^\nu+\lambda_-^\nu}{2}
\]
satisfies \(b_{\nu+2}=6b_{\nu+1}-b_\nu\), while
\[
b_0=1=a_0,\qquad b_1=\frac{\lambda_++\lambda_-}{2}=3=a_1.
\]
Induction in the common recurrence gives \(a_\nu=b_\nu\), so
\[
a_\nu
=
\frac{(3+\sqrt8)^\nu+(3-\sqrt8)^\nu}{2},
\]
and in particular \(a_\nu\ge0\), because \(2<\sqrt8<3\) makes both
\(\lambda_+\) and \(\lambda_-\) positive. A two-step induction now gives \(a_\nu\le6^\nu\): the cases \(\nu=0,1\) are immediate, and if the bound holds for \(\nu\) and \(\nu+1\), then
\[
a_{\nu+2}=6a_{\nu+1}-a_\nu
\le 6a_{\nu+1}
\le 6\cdot 6^{\nu+1}
=6^{\nu+2}.
\]
Thus \(T_L(3)=a_L\le6^L\), and therefore
\[
\sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}|\theta_{L,\alpha}|
=
\Theta_L^+
\le 6^L .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.DiscreteAteMinimaxLoggap.gpos_bound

\item Combining the preceding inequalities yields
\[
\|G_L\|_1
\le
\sum_{0\le \alpha<L-1}|\theta_{L,\alpha}|
\le
6^L,
\]
which proves the claim. % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.coefficientL1_chebG_le
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:shifted-chebyshev-quotient-coefficient-bound} gives a direct coefficient bound for the quotient polynomial \(G_L\). This estimate is the coefficient-growth input behind the explicit factorial second-moment calibration.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:explicit-chebyshev-calibration}[Explicit Chebyshev calibration]
Let \(T_j\) be the Chebyshev polynomial of degree \(j\), set \(Q_j(z)=T_j(1-2z)\), and let \(\|\cdot\|_1\) denote coefficient \(\ell^1\)-norm.  For every integer \(L\ge2\), the polynomial continuations \(E_L\) and \(G_L\) satisfy the following calibration.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Coefficient bounds.)}
\[
\|Q_L\|_1\le 7^L,
\qquad
2\|G_L\|_1\le 14^L .
\]
\item \textbf{(Factorial-lift second moment.)}
For every \(a\in\{0,1\}\), every \(t>0\), \(B>0\), and every \(s_0,s_1\ge0\) with \(L\le tB\), set \(p=s_0+s_1\).  If \(K_b\sim\operatorname{Poi}(ts_b)\) independently and \(\widehat W_{a,L}\) denotes the factorial lift of the light-cell polynomial based on \(G_L\), then
\[
E\widehat W_{a,L}^{2}
\le
7056^L\left(1+\frac{p}{B}\right)^{2L},
\]
and, whenever \(p\le B\),
\[
E\widehat W_{a,L}^{2}\le 7056^L .
\]
\item \textbf{(Interval identities.)}
For every \(z\in[0,1]\),
\[
0\le E_L(z),
\qquad
E_L(z)\le 1,
\qquad
zG_L(z)+E_L(z)=1 .
\]
Moreover, for every \(z\in(0,1]\),
\[
E_L(z)\le \frac{1}{L^2z}.
\]
\end{itemize}
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:explicit-chebyshev-calibration}]
Fix \(L\ge2\), so \(L>0\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item Write
\[
Q_L(z)=1+\sum_{j=1}^L q_{L,j}z^j,
\qquad q_{L,j}=[z^j]Q_L .
\]
The shifted Chebyshev expansion used for \(Q_L(z)=T_L(1-2z)\) gives
\[
\|Q_L\|_1
 =1+\sum_{j=1}^L |q_{L,j}|
 =T_L(3).
\]
The standard growth bound for Chebyshev polynomials at \(3\) gives \(T_L(3)\le 6^L\), and hence
\[
\|Q_L\|_1\le 6^L\le 7^L .
\]
For the quotient polynomial, \cref{obj:lem:shifted-chebyshev-quotient-coefficient-bound} gives
\[
\|G_L\|_1\le 6^L .
\]
Since \(L\ge2\),
\[
2\|G_L\|_1
 \le 2\cdot 6^L
 \le 2^L7^L
 =14^L .
\]
This proves the two coefficient bounds. % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.coefficientL1_chebQ_le; CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.coefficientL1_chebG_le

\item Fix \(a\in\{0,1\}\), \(t>0\), \(B>0\), and \(s_0,s_1\ge0\), and assume \(L\le tB\). Put
\[
\mathscr D=tB,\qquad p=s_0+s_1,\qquad \eta=1+\frac pB .
\]
Then \(\mathscr D>0\), \(\mathscr D(s_b/B)=ts_b\) for \(b=0,1\), and \(\eta\ge1\). Let
\[
\mathscr I_L=\{j\in\mathbb N:[z^j]G_L\ne0\}.
\]
To identify this index set, write
\[
Q_L(z)=1+q_{L,1}z+\sum_{r=2}^L q_{L,r}z^r,
\qquad q_{L,1}=-2L^2.
\]
The definitions of \(H_L\) and \(E_L\) give the scaled identity
\[
zE_L(z)=\frac{1-Q_L(z)}{2L^2}.
\]
Using the displayed value of \(q_{L,1}\), this identity gives
\[
E_L(z)=1-\sum_{r=2}^L\frac{q_{L,r}}{2L^2}z^{r-1},
\]
and the quotient definition of \(G_L\) gives
\[
G_L(z)=\frac{1-E_L(z)}z
=\sum_{r=2}^L\frac{q_{L,r}}{2L^2}z^{r-2}.
\]
Thus
\[
\mathscr I_L\subseteq\{0,\ldots,L-2\},
\]
with the assertion empty if the displayed sum has no nonzero terms, and every \(j\in\mathscr I_L\) satisfies \(j+1\le L\). For \(K=(K_0,K_1)\), with \(K_a=K_1\) when \(a=1\) and \(K_a=K_0\) when \(a=0\), define
\[
X_j(K)
=
\frac{(K_a)_{j+1}}{\mathscr D^{j+1}}
+
\frac{K_{1-a}(K_a)_j}{\mathscr D^{j+1}},
\qquad j\in\mathscr I_L .
\]
If \(\mathscr I_L\) is empty, the following sum is zero. With \(K_b\sim\operatorname{Poi}(ts_b)\) independently, the identities \(\mathscr D(s_b/B)=ts_b\) give the factorial lift as
\[
\widehat W_{a,L}
=
\sum_{j\in\mathscr I_L} [z^j]G_L\,X_j(K).
\]

For the moment bound, first use the one-dimensional Poisson falling-factorial estimate: if
\(\mathfrak N_z\sim\operatorname{Poi}(\mathscr D z)\), \(z\ge0\), and \(r,v\le L\), then
\[
\mathbb E\frac{(\mathfrak N_z)_r(\mathfrak N_z)_v}{\mathscr D^{r+v}}
=
\sum_{h=0}^{\min(r,v)}
\binom rh\binom vh h!\,
\frac{(\mathscr D z)^{r+v-h}}{\mathscr D^{r+v}}.
\]
For \(0\le h\le\min(r,v)\), the corresponding summand satisfies
\[
\binom rh\binom vh h!\,
\frac{(\mathscr D z)^{r+v-h}}{\mathscr D^{r+v}}
\le
\frac{(rv)^h}{h!\mathscr D^h}(1+z)^{r+v}
\le
\frac{L^h}{h!}(1+z)^{r+v},
\]
because \(rv/\mathscr D\le L\), which follows from \(r,v\le L\) and \(L\le\mathscr D\), and because \(z^{r+v-h}\le(1+z)^{r+v}\). Therefore
\[
\mathbb E\frac{(\mathfrak N_z)_r(\mathfrak N_z)_v}{\mathscr D^{r+v}}
\le
\sum_{h=0}^{L}\frac{L^h}{h!}(1+z)^{r+v}
\le
3^L(1+z)^{r+v},
\]
where the last inequality is the elementary finite-series bound \(\sum_{h=0}^L L^h/h!\le3^L\).

This estimate gives the same-arm square bound. For every \(0\le r\le L\), integrating out the unused arm in the product law and applying the preceding estimate with \(z=s_a/B\) and orders \((r,r)\) gives
\[
\mathbb E\left(\frac{(K_a)_r}{\mathscr D^r}\right)^2
\le
3^L\left(1+\frac{s_a}{B}\right)^{2r}.
\]
Since \(1+s_a/B\le\eta\), \(\eta\ge1\), and \(2r\le2L\),
\[
\mathbb E\left(\frac{(K_a)_r}{\mathscr D^r}\right)^2
\le 3^L\eta^{2L}.
\]
In particular, for \(j\in\mathscr I_L\), using \(j+1\le L\),
\[
\mathbb E\left(\frac{(K_a)_{j+1}}{\mathscr D^{j+1}}\right)^2
\le
3^L\eta^{2L}.
\]

The cross-arm square bound follows by the product structure. For \(j\in\mathscr I_L\), independence and \((K_{1-a})_1=K_{1-a}\) give
\[
\mathbb E\left(\frac{K_{1-a}(K_a)_j}{\mathscr D^{j+1}}\right)^2
=
\mathbb E\left(\frac{(K_a)_j}{\mathscr D^j}\right)^2
\mathbb E\left(\frac{(K_{1-a})_1}{\mathscr D}\right)^2.
\]
Applying the one-dimensional estimate to arm \(a\) with orders \((j,j)\) and to arm \(1-a\) with orders \((1,1)\) yields
\[
\mathbb E\left(\frac{K_{1-a}(K_a)_j}{\mathscr D^{j+1}}\right)^2
\le
3^L\left(1+\frac{s_a}{B}\right)^{2j}
\cdot
3^L\left(1+\frac{s_{1-a}}{B}\right)^2.
\]
Now \(1+s_a/B\le\eta\), \(1+s_{1-a}/B\le\eta\), \(\eta\ge1\), and \(2j+2\le2L\). Hence
\[
\left(1+\frac{s_a}{B}\right)^{2j}
\left(1+\frac{s_{1-a}}{B}\right)^2
\le
\eta^{2j+2}
\le
\eta^{2L},
\]
and therefore
\[
\mathbb E\left(\frac{K_{1-a}(K_a)_j}{\mathscr D^{j+1}}\right)^2
\le
9^L\eta^{2L}.
\]
Since \((\xi+\upsilon)^2\le2\xi^2+2\upsilon^2\),
\[
\mathbb E X_j(K)^2
\le
2\cdot 3^L\eta^{2L}+2\cdot 9^L\eta^{2L}
\le
36^L\eta^{2L}
=
\bigl(6^L\eta^L\bigr)^2 .
\]
The finite-sum \(L^2\) inequality
\[
\mathbb E\left(\sum_{j\in\mathscr I_L} c_jX_j\right)^2
\le
\left(\sum_{j\in\mathscr I_L}|c_j|\right)^2
\max_{j\in\mathscr I_L}\mathbb E X_j^2
\]
therefore yields
\[
\mathbb E\widehat W_{a,L}^2
\le
\|G_L\|_1^2\bigl(6^L\eta^L\bigr)^2
\le
(6^L)^2\bigl(6^L\eta^L\bigr)^2
=
1296^L\eta^{2L}.
\]
Because \(1296\le7056\),
\[
\mathbb E\widehat W_{a,L}^2
\le
7056^L\left(1+\frac pB\right)^{2L}.
\]
If \(p\le B\), then \(\eta\le2\), and hence
\[
\mathbb E\widehat W_{a,L}^2
\le
1296^L2^{2L}
=
5184^L
\le
7056^L .
\]
This proves both second-moment assertions. % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.armMonomial_sum_sq_le; CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.explicit_chebyshev_calibration

\item Let \(z\in[0,1]\). The scaled identity for the continuation is
\[
zE_L(z)=(2L^2)^{-1}\{1-Q_L(z)\}=
\frac{1-Q_L(z)}{2L^2}.
\]
At \(z=0\), the derivative identity \(T_L'(1)=L^2\) gives \(E_L(0)=1\). For \(z>0\), put
\[
\theta=\arccos(1-2z).
\]
Then \(Q_L(z)=\cos(L\theta)\), so \(|Q_L(z)|\le1\). Hence \(1-Q_L(z)\ge0\), and the scaled identity gives \(E_L(z)\ge0\). Also
\[
1-Q_L(z)
=
1-\cos(L\theta)
\le
L^2(1-\cos\theta)
=
2L^2z,
\]
so \(zE_L(z)\le z\), whence \(E_L(z)\le1\). Finally, \(|Q_L(z)|\le1\) gives \(1-Q_L(z)\le2\), and the normalization by \(2L^2\) in the scaled identity gives
\[
zE_L(z)
=
\frac{1-Q_L(z)}{2L^2}
\le
\frac1{L^2},
\]
and division by \(z>0\) gives
\[
E_L(z)\le\frac1{L^2z}.
\]
The quotient definition gives the polynomial identity
\[
zG_L(z)+E_L(z)=1
\]
for every real \(z\), and therefore on \([0,1]\). % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.chebE_interval_bounds; CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.chebG_defining_identity
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:explicit-chebyshev-calibration} supplies the numerical coefficient bounds used to instantiate \cref{obj:lem:chebyshev-factorial-certificate} in the calibrated estimator. In particular, the constant \(7056\) gives the explicit factorial-lift second-moment scale that appears in the finite calibration of \cref{obj:def:mixed-estimator-handle}.


\section{Common-marginal lower bounds}\label{sec:appendix-lower}

The converse in \cref{obj:thm:common-marginal-converse} rests on priors whose treatment--covariate marginal is shared across branches while their average treatment effect centers are separated. This appendix collects the finite approximation and transfer statements that make that construction compatible with the annotation experiment in \cref{obj:def:annotation-experiment}. The first object is a two-cell calculation that isolates the statistical issue in its smallest form.

\begin{definitionv}{def:finite-small-instance}[Finite small instance \(\mathfrak C_{\mathrm{small}}\)]
Let
\[
\epsilon=\frac14,\qquad d=2,\qquad p_1=p_2=\frac12,\qquad e_1=e_2=\frac12,\qquad \mu_{01}=\mu_{02}=0.
\]
For \(n=m=1\), independence of the samples gives
\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
2\mathbf 1\{\mathcal L_1=((1,1,1))\}
\left(\mathbf 1\{\mathcal U_1=((1,0))\}+\mathbf 1\{\mathcal U_1=((1,1))\}\right)
\right]
=2q_{11}p_1,
\]
where \(q_{11}\) is the outcome-marked mass for treatment-covariate cell \((1,1)\). At \(\mu_{11}=1\), this equals \(1/4\), while the treated-cell target is
\[
p_1\mu_{11}=\frac12.
\]
Define \(\Pi_0^{\mathrm{small}}\) to put unit mass on the law with
\[
\mu_{11}=\mu_{12}=0,
\]
and define \(\Pi_1^{\mathrm{small}}\) to put unit mass on the law with
\[
\mu_{11}=1,\qquad \mu_{12}=0.
\]
Under both priors,
\[
P_{XA}(x,a)=\frac14
\quad\text{for all }(x,a),
\]
whereas
\[
\tau(P)=0
\]
under \(\Pi_0^{\mathrm{small}}\), and
\[
\tau(P)=\frac12
\]
under \(\Pi_1^{\mathrm{small}}\). Consequently, the auxiliary laws agree for every \(m\), and the total variation distance between the two labeled laws is
\[
1-\left(\frac34\right)^n.
\]
These identities constitute \(\mathfrak C_{\mathrm{small}}\).
\end{definitionv}

The instance in \cref{obj:def:finite-small-instance} makes concrete the role of outcome labels. The two priors \leanref{sym:\Pi_0^{\mathrm{small}}}{\ensuremath{\Pi_0^{\mathrm{small}}}} and \leanref{sym:\Pi_1^{\mathrm{small}}}{\ensuremath{\Pi_1^{\mathrm{small}}}} share the same treatment--covariate marginal \leanref{sym:P_{XA}}{\ensuremath{P_{XA}}}, so auxiliary observations identify the common table while the target separation remains attached to the outcome-marked mass \leanref{sym:q_{ax}}{\ensuremath{q_{ax}}}. The calculation \leanref{sym:\mathfrak C_{\mathrm{small}}}{\ensuremath{\mathfrak C_{\mathrm{small}}}} is finite, but it has the same common-marginal structure used in the high-dimensional lower bound.

The next statement supplies the approximation-duality ingredient. It constructs a signed finite measure on a short interval whose first \(L\) moments vanish while the inverse-weight functional retains a fixed gap. This is the same polynomial approximation mechanism that appears in nonsmooth functional lower bounds for discrete distributions \citep{JiaoVenkatHanWeissman2015,WuYang2016,ValiantValiant2017,OrlitskySureshWu2016}.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:finite-alternation-duality}[Finite alternation duality]
Fix an overlap constant \(\epsilon\) with \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), and set
\[
\kappa=\frac{1-2\epsilon}{\epsilon}.
\]
There exist constants \(\gamma_\epsilon>0\) and \(c_\epsilon>0\) such that, for every integer \(L\ge 2\) and every \(B>0\), with
\[
a=\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{L^2},
\]
one has \(a/\kappa\le B\), and there are distinct points \(p_0,\ldots,p_{L+1}\in [a/\kappa,B]\) and real weights \(w_0,\ldots,w_{L+1}\) satisfying
\[
\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}|w_i|=1,\qquad
\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}w_i p_i^j=0\quad\text{for every }0\le j\le L,
\]
and
\[
c_\epsilon\le \sum_{i=0}^{L+1}w_i\,\frac{p_i}{p_i+a}.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:finite-alternation-duality}]
\begin{enumerate}
\item Set
\[
D_\epsilon=\frac{\kappa}{(1+\kappa)(2+\kappa)},\qquad
\gamma_\epsilon=\min\left\{\frac{\kappa}{4},\frac{D_\epsilon\kappa}{32}\right\},\qquad
c_\epsilon=\frac{D_\epsilon}{8}.
\]
Since \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), we have \(\kappa>0\). Hence \(D_\epsilon>0\), \(\gamma_\epsilon>0\), and \(c_\epsilon>0\). Also \(D_\epsilon<1\), because
\[
\kappa<(1+\kappa)(2+\kappa)
\]
for \(\kappa>0\). % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.finite_alternation_duality

\item Fix \(L\ge2\) and \(B>0\), and put
\[
a=\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{L^2}.
\]
Then \(a>0\). Since \(\gamma_\epsilon\le \kappa/4\) and \(L^2\ge4\),
\[
\frac{a}{\kappa}
=\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{\kappa L^2}
\le B.
\]
Moreover,
\[
\frac{a}{\kappa}\le \frac{B}{16},
\]
because \(\gamma_\epsilon\le \kappa/4\) and \(L^2\ge4\). In particular \(a/\kappa<B\). % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.finite_alternation_duality

\item Let
\[
I=\left[\frac{a}{\kappa},B\right],
\qquad
f(p)=\frac{p}{p+a}.
\]
The pole \(-a\) lies to the left of \(I\), since \(a>0\) and \(a/\kappa>0\). Thus \(f\) is continuous on \(I\). Let \(E\) be the best uniform error for approximating \(f\) on \(I\) by polynomials of degree at most \(L\). The finite alternation theorem supplies an alternation dual certificate: distinct nodes in \(I\), normalized weights annihilating all monomials of degree at most \(L\), and an associated degree-\(L\) best approximating polynomial. % lean: Causalean.Mathlib.Analysis.FinitePolynomialAlternationDuality.exists_alternationDualCertificate

\item We claim
\[
c_\epsilon\le E.
\]
Suppose instead that \(E<c_\epsilon\). Define
\[
p_-=\frac{a}{\kappa},\qquad p_+=\frac{2a}{\kappa}.
\]
Then \(p_-<p_+\), and both points belong to \(I\). Indeed \(p_-=a/\kappa\), while
\[
p_+=\frac{2\gamma_\epsilon B}{\kappa L^2}\le B
\]
because \(\gamma_\epsilon\le \kappa\) and \(L^2\ge4\). At these two points,
\[
f(p_-)=\frac{1}{1+\kappa},\qquad
f(p_+)=\frac{2}{2+\kappa},
\]
so
\[
f(p_+)-f(p_-)
=\frac{\kappa}{(1+\kappa)(2+\kappa)}
=D_\epsilon.
\]
Let \(Q\) be the best approximating polynomial from the alternation certificate. For every \(p\in I\),
\[
|f(p)-Q(p)|\le E.
\]
Consequently,
\[
D_\epsilon-2E
\le Q(p_+)-Q(p_-).
\]
By the mean-value theorem there is a point \(\xi\in[p_-,p_+]\subset I\) such that
\[
Q'(\xi)=\frac{Q(p_+)-Q(p_-)}{p_+-p_-},
\]
and therefore
\[
\frac{D_\epsilon-2E}{p_+-p_-}\le |Q'(\xi)|.
\]
Since \(E<c_\epsilon=D_\epsilon/8\), the numerator is positive. % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.finite_alternation_duality

\item On \(I\), the target satisfies \(0\le f(p)\le1\). Combining this with \(|f(p)-Q(p)|\le E\) gives
\[
|Q(p)|\le 1+E\qquad (p\in I),
\]
or equivalently
\[
\|Q\|_{\infty,I}\le 1+E.
\]
Markov's inequality on the interval \(I=[a/\kappa,B]\), applied to the degree-\(L\) polynomial \(Q\), gives
\[
\|Q'\|_{\infty,I}
\le
\frac{2L^2}{B-a/\kappa}\,\|Q\|_{\infty,I}
\le
\frac{2L^2}{B-a/\kappa}(1+E).
\]
Because \(a/\kappa\le B/16\), we have \(B/2\le B-a/\kappa\). Also \(E<c_\epsilon<1\). Hence
\[
|Q'(\xi)|
\le
\frac{2L^2}{B-a/\kappa}(1+E)
\le
\frac{2L^2}{B/2}\cdot2
=
\frac{8L^2}{B}.
\]
Combining this with the previous lower bound and using
\[
p_+-p_-=\frac{a}{\kappa}
=\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{\kappa L^2}
\]
yields
\[
\frac{(D_\epsilon-2E)\kappa L^2}{\gamma_\epsilon B}
\le
\frac{8L^2}{B}.
\]
After cancellation,
\[
(D_\epsilon-2E)\kappa\le 8\gamma_\epsilon.
\]
But \(E<D_\epsilon/8\), so \(D_\epsilon-2E>3D_\epsilon/4\), while
\[
8\gamma_\epsilon\le \frac{D_\epsilon\kappa}{4}.
\]
This is impossible. Therefore \(c_\epsilon\le E\). % lean: Causalean.Mathlib.Analysis.FinitePolynomialAlternationDuality.markov_derivative_Icc

\item Let the alternation certificate have nodes \(p_0,\ldots,p_{L+1}\in I\) and weights \(\alpha_0,\ldots,\alpha_{L+1}\). They satisfy
\[
\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}|\alpha_i|=1,
\qquad
\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}\alpha_i p_i^j=0\quad(0\le j\le L),
\]
and
\[
\left|\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}\alpha_i f(p_i)\right|=E.
\]
Choose
\[
s=
\begin{cases}
1,&\text{if }\sum_i\alpha_i f(p_i)\ge0,\\
-1,&\text{if }\sum_i\alpha_i f(p_i)<0,
\end{cases}
\qquad
w_i=s\alpha_i .
\]
Then the nodes remain distinct and lie in \(I\), and
\[
\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}|w_i|
=
\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}|\alpha_i|
=1.
\]
For every \(0\le j\le L\),
\[
\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}w_i p_i^j
=
s\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}\alpha_i p_i^j
=0.
\]
Finally,
\[
\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}w_i\frac{p_i}{p_i+a}
=
s\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}\alpha_i f(p_i)
=
\left|\sum_{i=0}^{L+1}\alpha_i f(p_i)\right|
=
E
\ge c_\epsilon.
\]
These nodes and weights give the required finite moment dual certificate on \([a/\kappa,B]\). % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.finite_alternation_duality
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:finite-alternation-duality} turns polynomial indistinguishability into target separation. The moment cancellations control likelihood differences after Poissonization, while the final display gives a fixed gap for the inverse-weight expression that enters the treatment-effect contrast.

The rare-cell prior packages this dual certificate into a common-marginal experiment. For the transfer arguments, it is useful to state the construction as a recipe separate from any particular choice of sample intensities. The next definition records the raw rare-cell masses, the two branch laws, and the raw target centers that the lower-bound reductions manipulate; the later uniform-intensity lemma supplies the exact Poisson witness actually used by the converse proof.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_20}[Common-marginal rare-cell recipe]
A common-marginal rare-cell recipe with \(k\) rare cells at scale \((a,B,L)\) is a finite random construction \(\mathcal R\) consisting of a generator law \(\operatorname{generator}_{\mathcal R}\) for a latent draw \(w\), together with two branch laws indexed by \(b\in\{0,1\}\). For each generated \(w\), the recipe specifies rare-cell raw masses \(p_i(w)\in[0,B]\), \(i=1,\ldots,k\), branch-specific outcome regressions, and any reservoir mass needed to normalize the construction. The two branches have the same treatment--covariate raw masses and treatment propensities, so after normalization they induce the same \(P_{XA}\) table, while their outcome regressions may differ.

For each branch \(b\in\{0,1\}\), let \(P_b(w)\) be the normalized law on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\) induced by the recipe, and let
\[
\operatorname{rawTarget}_{\mathcal R}(b,w)
\]
be the unnormalized target contribution assigned by the rare cells in branch \(b\). The branch center is
\[
\theta_b=\int \operatorname{rawTarget}_{\mathcal R}(b,w)\,
d\operatorname{generator}_{\mathcal R}(w),
\]
and the raw-center separation is \(\Delta=|\theta_1-\theta_0|\). The recipe induces, for each branch \(b\), the mixture experiment obtained by first drawing
\(w\sim\operatorname{generator}_{\mathcal R}\), then sampling from \(P_b(w)\) for labeled observations and from the common marginal \(P_{b,XA}(w)\) for auxiliary observations. Its raw Poisson version uses labeled intensity \(u\) and auxiliary intensity \(v\) with the same generated law \(P_b(w)\), so the treatment--covariate component is common across the two branches and the likelihood discrepancy is carried by the labeled outcome-bearing component.
\end{definitionv}

\Cref{obj:synth_20} fixes the bookkeeping used by the lower-bound priors. The random raw masses determine a common treatment--covariate table, and the branch-specific outcome regressions create a raw-center separation before normalization. This separation is the quantity later converted into minimax risk.

The raw Poisson comparison enters several concentration and transfer statements, so the notation for the branch predictive laws is named explicitly before those estimates.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:common-marginal-recipe-target-concentration}[Prior target concentration]
Fix \(\epsilon\in\mathbb R\), a common-marginal calibration at overlap level \(\epsilon\) with dual-gap constant \(c_\epsilon\), and parameters \(n,m,d,L,k\in\mathbb N\) and \(B,a,u,v,C,\rho\in\mathbb R\).  Let \(\mathcal R\) be a common-marginal rare-cell recipe with atom-generator measure \(\Gamma\), branch laws \(P_b(w)\), raw targets \(r_b(w)\), raw total mass \(S(w)\), and branch priors
\[
\Pi_b=\Gamma\circ P_b^{-1},
\qquad
\theta_b=\int r_b(w)\,d\Gamma(w),
\qquad b\in\{0,1\}.
\]
Assume the recipe bounds hold at intensities \((u,v)\) with constants \((C,\rho)\) and dual gap \(c_\epsilon\):
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Finite-atom construction.)}  The recipe uses the finite-atom construction.
\item \textbf{(Model support.)}  Each prior is supported on \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) of \cref{obj:def:model-class}.
\item \textbf{(Common table.)}  The two priors induce the same law of the random treatment-covariate table.
\item \textbf{(Separation.)}  The raw centers satisfy
\[
c_\epsilon k a\le |\theta_1-\theta_0|.
\]
\item \textbf{(Mass and variance bounds.)}  Under the usual measurability and integrability conditions,
\[
\int S(w)\,d\Gamma(w)=1,\qquad
\operatorname{Var}_\Gamma(S)\le CkBa,\qquad
\max_{b\in\{0,1\}}\operatorname{Var}_\Gamma(r_b)\le CkBa.
\]
\item \textbf{(Mixture proximity.)}  The two raw mixture laws at labeled intensity \(u\) and auxiliary intensity \(v\) have total variation distance at most
\[
Cuka\rho^L.
\]
\end{itemize}
Then for every branch \(b\in\{0,1\}\) and every \(\Delta\) with \(0<\Delta\le2\), the branch prior concentrates its average treatment effect \(\tau(P)\), defined in \cref{obj:def:ate-functional}, around its raw center:
\[
\Pi_b\!\left\{P:\frac{\Delta}{4}<|\tau(P)-\theta_b|\right\}
\le
\frac{512\,CkBa}{\Delta^2}.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-recipe-target-concentration}]
Fix \(b\in\{0,1\}\).  Define the two exceptional events in the atom space by
\[
\mathsf A_{\mathrm{tar}}
:=
\left\{w:\frac{\Delta}{16}<|r_b(w)-\theta_b|\right\},
\qquad
\mathsf A_{\mathrm{mass}}
:=
\left\{w:\frac{\Delta}{16}<|S(w)-1|\right\}.
\]
We first record the normalization implication used below.  Outside a \(\Gamma\)-null set the recipe gives
\[
r_b(w)=S(w)\tau(P_b(w)),
\qquad
S(w)>0.
\]
Suppose
\[
\frac{\Delta}{4}<|\tau(P_b(w))-\theta_b|,
\qquad
w\notin \mathsf A_{\mathrm{tar}}\cup\mathsf A_{\mathrm{mass}}.
\]
The second condition means
\[
|r_b(w)-\theta_b|\le \frac{\Delta}{16},
\qquad
|S(w)-1|\le \frac{\Delta}{16}.
\]
The raw-center bound supplied by the recipe normalization is
\[
|\theta_b|\le 1.
\]
Hence
\[
|S(w)\theta_b-\theta_b|
=
|(S(w)-1)\theta_b|
\le \frac{\Delta}{16}.
\]
Using \(r_b(w)=S(w)\tau(P_b(w))\), the triangle inequality gives
\[
S(w)|\tau(P_b(w))-\theta_b|
=
|S(w)\tau(P_b(w))-S(w)\theta_b|
\le
\frac{\Delta}{8}.
\]
Also \(|S(w)-1|\le\Delta/16\) and \(\Delta\le2\) imply
\[
S(w)\ge 1-\frac{\Delta}{16}\ge \frac78 .
\]
Therefore
\[
\frac78\,|\tau(P_b(w))-\theta_b|
\le
S(w)|\tau(P_b(w))-\theta_b|
\le \frac{\Delta}{8},
\]
which contradicts \(\Delta/4<|\tau(P_b(w))-\theta_b|\).  Thus, for \(\Gamma\)-almost every \(w\),
\[
\frac{\Delta}{4}<|\tau(P_b(w))-\theta_b|
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
w\in\mathsf A_{\mathrm{tar}}\cup\mathsf A_{\mathrm{mass}} .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.commonMarginal_normalizedTarget_event

By the definition of the branch prior as the pushforward of \(\Gamma\) under \(P_b\), with the two Boolean branches handled separately,
\[
\Pi_b\!\left\{P:\frac{\Delta}{4}<|\tau(P)-\theta_b|\right\}
=
\Gamma\!\left\{w:\frac{\Delta}{4}<|\tau(P_b(w))-\theta_b|\right\}.
\]
The implication above yields
\[
\Pi_b\!\left\{P:\frac{\Delta}{4}<|\tau(P)-\theta_b|\right\}
\le
\Gamma(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{tar}}\cup\mathsf A_{\mathrm{mass}}).
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.commonMarginal_prior_target_concentration

We next bound the two exceptional probabilities.  Chebyshev's inequality, applied under \(\Gamma\) with mean
\[
\theta_b=\int r_b(w)\,d\Gamma(w),
\]
and the assumed variance bound
\[
\operatorname{Var}_{\Gamma}(r_b)\le CkBa,
\]
gives
\[
\Gamma(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{tar}})
\le
\frac{CkBa}{(\Delta/16)^2}.
\]
Similarly, since
\[
\int S(w)\,d\Gamma(w)=1,
\qquad
\operatorname{Var}_{\Gamma}(S)\le CkBa,
\]
Chebyshev's inequality gives
\[
\Gamma(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{mass}})
\le
\frac{CkBa}{(\Delta/16)^2}.
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.commonMarginal_target_concentration

Subadditivity of measure gives
\[
\Gamma(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{tar}}\cup\mathsf A_{\mathrm{mass}})
\le
\Gamma(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{tar}})+\Gamma(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{mass}}).
\]
Combining the preceding displays,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\Pi_b\!\left\{P:\frac{\Delta}{4}<|\tau(P)-\theta_b|\right\}
&\le
\frac{CkBa}{(\Delta/16)^2}
+
\frac{CkBa}{(\Delta/16)^2}  \\
&=
\frac{512\,CkBa}{\Delta^2},
\end{aligned}
\]
where the final equality uses \(\Delta>0\).  This is the desired bound for the fixed branch \(b\), and hence for both branches.
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.commonMarginal_prior_target_concentration
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:synth_21} separates the prior randomness in the raw masses from the Poisson sampling randomness. With this notation, total variation between the two branch mixtures measures the experiment-level testing difficulty after the common treatment--covariate table has been integrated over.

The next two lemmas isolate the normalization estimates used by the transfer. The first converts raw target control into concentration of the average treatment effect, and the second records the corresponding lower-tail control for the raw scale.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_21}[Raw Poisson prior-predictive mixture laws]
Fix a common-marginal rare-cell recipe \(\mathcal R\) with atom generator \(\Gamma\), branch laws \(P_b(w)\) for \(b\in\{0,1\}\), and branch priors \(\Pi_b=\Gamma\circ P_b^{-1}\). At labeled and auxiliary Poisson intensities \(u\) and \(v\), define \(\mathsf Q_b\) to be the branch-\(b\) raw prior-predictive law obtained by first drawing \(w\sim\Gamma\), forming the raw branch law \(P_b(w)\), and then sampling the independent raw Poisson experiment with labeled intensity \(u\) and auxiliary intensity \(v\) from that raw law and its treatment--covariate marginal. Equivalently,
\[
\mathsf Q_b
=
\int
\Bigl\{
\operatorname{PoiExp}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{raw}}\bigl(P_b(w)\bigr)
\Bigr\}\,d\Gamma(w)
=
\int
\operatorname{PoiExp}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{raw}}(P)\,d\Pi_b(P),
\qquad b\in\{0,1\},
\]
where \(\operatorname{PoiExp}_{u,v}^{\mathrm{raw}}(P)\) denotes the product Poisson law for the outcome-labeled raw cell counts at intensity \(u\) and the auxiliary treatment--covariate raw cell counts at intensity \(v\).
\end{definitionv}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:common-marginal-recipe-scale-tail}[Small-scale prior tail]
Fix \(\epsilon\in\mathbb R\), a common-marginal calibration with positive constants including dual-gap constant \(c_\epsilon\le 1/4\), integers \(n,m,d,L,k\ge0\), and reals \(B,a,u,v,C,\rho\).  Let \(\mathcal R\) be a common-marginal rare-cell recipe with atom-generator \(\Gamma\), branch laws \(P_b(w)\), branch priors \(\Pi_b=\Gamma\circ P_b^{-1}\), raw total mass \(S(w)\), raw targets \(r_b(w)\), and measurable raw scale \(s_{\mathcal R}\) satisfying \(s_{\mathcal R}(P_b(w))=S(w)\) for \(\Gamma\)-almost every \(w\).

Assume that the recipe bounds hold for \(\mathcal R\) at \((u,v,c_\epsilon,C,\rho)\):
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Finite-atom construction.)} The recipe uses the finite-atom construction.
\item \textbf{(Model support and common table.)} The two priors are supported on \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) from \cref{obj:def:model-class}, and they induce the same distribution of the random treatment-covariate table \(P_{XA}\).
\item \textbf{(Center separation.)} If \(\theta_b\) denotes the raw prior center for branch \(b\), then
\[
c_\epsilon k a \le |\theta_1-\theta_0|.
\]
\item \textbf{(Mass and variance bounds.)} Under the usual measurability and integrability conditions,
\[
\int S\,d\Gamma=1,\qquad
\operatorname{Var}_{\Gamma}(S)\le CkBa,\qquad
\max\{\operatorname{Var}_{\Gamma}(r_0),\operatorname{Var}_{\Gamma}(r_1)\}\le CkBa.
\]
\item \textbf{(Mixture distance.)} The two raw Poisson mixture laws generated by the recipe satisfy
\[
\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1)\le Cuka\rho^L.
\]
\end{itemize}
Then, for each branch \(b\in\{0,1\}\),
\[
\Pi_b\!\left\{P:\max\{s_{\mathcal R}(P),0\}<\frac12\right\}
\le 4CkBa .
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-recipe-scale-tail}]
\begin{enumerate}
\item Define the mass-tail event on the atom space by
\[
\mathsf{MassTail}
:=
\Bigl\{w:\frac12<|S(w)-1|\Bigr\}.
\]
The mass assumptions give
\[
\int S\,d\Gamma=1,
\qquad
\operatorname{Var}_{\Gamma}(S)\le CkBa.
\]
Since \(\Gamma\) is a probability law, Chebyshev's inequality at radius \(1/2\) yields
\[
\Gamma(\mathsf{MassTail})
=
\Gamma\Bigl\{w:\frac12<|S(w)-1|\Bigr\}
\le
\frac{\operatorname{Var}_{\Gamma}(S)}{(1/2)^2}
\le
4CkBa .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.commonMarginal_mass_concentration

\item For a fixed branch \(b\in\{0,1\}\), set
\[
\mathsf{SmallScale}_b
:=
\Bigl\{P:\max\{s_{\mathcal R}(P),0\}<\frac12\Bigr\}.
\]
Because \(\Pi_b=\Gamma\circ P_b^{-1}\),
\[
\Pi_b(\mathsf{SmallScale}_b)
=
\Gamma\{w:P_b(w)\in\mathsf{SmallScale}_b\}.
\]
For \(\Gamma\)-almost every \(w\), the recipe supplies both identities
\[
s_{\mathcal R}(P_b(w))=S(w)
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
0<S(w).
\]
On this full-measure set, \(P_b(w)\in\mathsf{SmallScale}_b\) implies
\[
S(w)=\max\{S(w),0\}<\frac12.
\]
Hence \(S(w)-1<-1/2\), and therefore
\[
|S(w)-1|=1-S(w)>\frac12.
\]
Thus
\[
\{w:P_b(w)\in\mathsf{SmallScale}_b\}
\subseteq \mathsf{MassTail}
\quad\text{up to a \(\Gamma\)-null set,}
\]
and consequently
\[
\Pi_b(\mathsf{SmallScale}_b)\le \Gamma(\mathsf{MassTail}).
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.commonMarginal_prior_scale_tail

\item The preceding containment is applied to the two branch cases separately: for \(b=0\) it uses
\[
\Pi_0=\Gamma\circ P_0^{-1},
\]
and for \(b=1\) it uses
\[
\Pi_1=\Gamma\circ P_1^{-1}.
\]
Combining the appropriate branch identity with the bound from the first step gives, for each
\(b\in\{0,1\}\),
\[
\Pi_b\!\left\{P:\max\{s_{\mathcal R}(P),0\}<\frac12\right\}
\le
\Gamma(\mathsf{MassTail})
\le
4CkBa .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.commonMarginal_prior_scale_tail
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-recipe-target-concentration,obj:lem:common-marginal-recipe-scale-tail} make the raw construction usable after normalization. The target concentration bound relates the branch centers \(\theta_b\) to the actual functional values under the induced priors, while the scale-tail bound keeps the normalizing mass away from the region where raw-to-normalized conversion would amplify the rare-cell perturbation.

The next transfer lemma applies the recipe at fixed Poisson intensities large enough to dominate the fixed sample sizes. Its assumptions combine a total-variation bound with a scale condition that controls the random normalization.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:common-marginal-recipe-transfer}[Recipe transfer bound]
Let \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), where \(\epsilon\) is the overlap level.  Fix a common-marginal calibration at level \(\epsilon\), consisting of positive constants including \(\gamma_\epsilon\) and dual gap \(\delta_\epsilon\le 1/4\), a constant \(C_\epsilon>0\), and a real number \(\rho_\epsilon\).  Let \(n,m,d,L,k\) be nonnegative integers, let \(B,a\in\mathbb R\), and let \(\mathcal R\) be a common-marginal rare-cell recipe with \(k\) rare cells at scale \((a,B,L)\).  For each branch \(b\in\{0,1\}\), define the raw prior center
\[
\theta_b=\int \operatorname{rawTarget}_{\mathcal R}(b,w)\,dG_{\mathcal R}(w),
\]
where \(G_{\mathcal R}\) is the atom-generator measure of the recipe, and put
\[
\Delta=|\theta_1-\theta_0|.
\]
Assume:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Sample and dimension.)} \(n\ge 1\) and \(d\ge 2\).
\item \textbf{(Poisson-prior data.)} With
\[
u=65536\,n
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
v=65536\,(n+m),
\]
the recipe has bandwidth \(B>0\), shift
\[
a=\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{L^2},
\]
rare-shift size
\[
ka\le \delta_\epsilon,
\]
and the recipe intensities agree with \(u\) and \(v\).
\item \textbf{(Bandwidth and likelihood bounds.)} The realized scale satisfies
\[
(u+v)B\le b_\epsilon L,
\]
where \(b_\epsilon\) is the calibration's uniform-bandwidth constant, and the recipe satisfies the common-marginal Poisson-prior bounds at gap \(\delta_\epsilon\), intensity pair \((u,v)\), variance constant \(C_\epsilon\), and decay constant \(\rho_\epsilon\).
\item \textbf{(Mixture affinity.)} The two prior-predictive raw Poisson-count laws generated by the two recipe priors and the recipe's experiment kernel have total variation distance at most
\[
\frac18 .
\]
\item \textbf{(Variance scale.)} The recipe scale obeys
\[
kBa\le \frac{\Delta^2}{65536\,C_\epsilon}.
\]
\end{itemize}
Then the minimax risk of \cref{obj:def:minimax-risk} satisfies
\[
R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\ge \frac{\Delta^2}{65536}.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-recipe-transfer}]
Let
\[
\Pi_b:=\text{the branch-\(b\) prior induced by \(\mathcal R\)},\qquad
\theta_b:=\int r_b(w)\,d\Gamma(w),\qquad b\in\{0,1\},
\]
where \(\Gamma\) is the atom-generator measure and \(r_b\) is the raw target of the recipe.  Thus
\[
\Delta=|\theta_1-\theta_0|.
\]
Also put
\[
u:=65536\,n,\qquad v:=65536\,(n+m),
\]
and, for every law \(P\), define the totalized nonnegative recipe scale
\[
s_+(P):=\max\{s_{\mathcal R}(P),0\}.
\]

\begin{enumerate}
\item The witness bounds imply
\[
|\theta_0|\le 1,\qquad |\theta_1|\le 1.
\]
Indeed, each \(\theta_b\) is the \(\Gamma\)-mean of the raw target, the raw target is \(S(w)\tau(P_b(w))\), \(|\tau(P_b(w))|\le1\) on \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), and the recipe bounds give \(\int S\,d\Gamma=1\).  Hence
\[
0\le\Delta\le 2.
\]
The intensities satisfy \(u\ge0\) and \(v\ge0\).  The branch priors are probability measures, and the support part of the witness gives the required nonempty model class. % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.commonMarginal_rawPriorCenter_abs_le_one

\item Let \(\mathscr K_{\rm raw}\) be the ordered raw Poisson-count experiment which, at law \(P\), draws independent ordered Poisson samples from the labeled mark law and the auxiliary mark law with intensities
\[
u\,s_+(P)
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
v\,s_+(P).
\]
Let \(\mathsf Q_b\) be the prior-predictive law obtained from \(\Pi_b\) through \(\mathscr K_{\rm raw}\).  We now identify this law as a Markov image of the raw count law in the statement.  Let \(\mathscr K_{\rm cnt}\) denote the recipe's raw Poisson-count kernel before ordering.  Let \(\operatorname{reindex}\) be the deterministic map that rewrites a raw count table as labeled-mark and auxiliary-mark count functions, and let \(\operatorname{order}\) be the paired-histogram kernel that, conditional on those two count functions, orders the corresponding multiset of labeled marks and the corresponding multiset of auxiliary marks uniformly and independently.  Put
\[
\operatorname{Rec}:=\operatorname{order}\circ\operatorname{reindex} .
\]
For every law \(P\), the raw count kernel factors through this reconstruction as
\[
\operatorname{Rec}\circ \mathscr K_{\rm cnt}(P)
=
\mathscr K_{\rm raw}(P).
\]
Indeed, after the deterministic reindexing, the labeled and auxiliary count functions have independent Poisson coordinates with means \(u\,s_+(P)\) times the labeled mark law and \(v\,s_+(P)\) times the auxiliary mark law; ordering each histogram gives exactly the ordered independent Poisson samples with those two intensities.  Therefore, for each branch,
\[
\mathcal R_{\rm raw,b}
:=\int \mathscr K_{\rm cnt}(P)\,d\Pi_b(P)
\qquad\text{satisfies}\qquad
\mathsf Q_b
=\int \mathscr K_{\rm raw}(P)\,d\Pi_b(P)
=(\operatorname{Rec})_{\#}\mathcal R_{\rm raw,b}.
\]
Total variation contracts under a Markov kernel, and the assumed raw mixture affinity gives
\[
\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1)
\le
\operatorname{TV}(\mathcal R_{\rm raw,0},\mathcal R_{\rm raw,1})
\le \frac18 .
\] % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.commonMarginal_predictive_reconstruction_contraction

\item Set
\[
\alpha_{\rm floor}:=\frac1{100}.
\]
The fixed experiment has the parametric two-point floor
\[
\frac{\alpha_{\rm floor}}{n}\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).
\]
To see this, take
\[
\delta_n:=\frac{2}{5\sqrt n}.
\]
Choose a cell \(x_\star\in[d]\).  Under both laws below, let \(X=x_\star\) almost surely and let the treatment propensity be \(1/2\).  Let the control outcome mean be \(1/2\) under both laws; let the treated outcome mean be \(1/2\) for \(P_{\rm par,0}\) and \(1/2+\delta_n\) for \(P_{\rm par,1}\).  Since \(n\ge1\),
\[
0\le\delta_n\le\frac25,
\]
so these are valid binary outcome laws, and since \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), both belong to \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\).  Their treatment-covariate marginals agree, their average treatment effects are
\[
\tau(P_{\rm par,0})=0,
\qquad
\tau(P_{\rm par,1})=\delta_n,
\]
and hence the auxiliary samples have identical laws.  For the labeled samples, the standard Bernoulli product testing bound applies because
\[
n\cdot
\frac{(1/2)\delta_n^2}{(1/2)(1-1/2)}
=
\frac{8}{25}
\le \log 2,
\]
and therefore
\[
\operatorname{TV}\!\bigl(P_{\rm par,0}^{\otimes n},P_{\rm par,1}^{\otimes n}\bigr)
\le\frac12.
\]
Le Cam's two-point bound with separation \(\delta_n\) yields
\[
R_\epsilon(n,m,d)
\ge
\frac{(\delta_n/2)^2}{4}
=
\frac1{100\,n}.
\]
For the fixed-sample decision problem with the class-gated clipped squared loss, let
\[
\mathfrak R_{\rm dec}
:=
\inf_{\psi}
\sup_P
\int
\ell_\epsilon(P,\psi)\,d\mathscr K_{\rm fix}(P),
\]
where the infimum is over measurable fixed-sample rules, \(\mathscr K_{\rm fix}\) is the fixed labeled-and-auxiliary experiment, and
\[
\ell_\epsilon(P,z)
:=
\begin{cases}
\bigl(\operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]}(z)-\tau(P)\bigr)^2,& P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon},\\
0,& P\notin\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}.
\end{cases}
\]
We use the following elementary comparison between \cref{obj:def:minimax-risk} and the class-gated decision problem:
\[
R_\epsilon(n,m,d)
\le
\mathfrak R_{\rm dec}.
\]
Indeed, every measurable fixed-sample rule \(\psi\) induces the measurable estimator
\[
\widetilde T_\psi:=\operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]}\circ\psi .
\]
For every \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), the class-gated loss evaluates the same clipped rule, so
\[
\int \ell_\epsilon(P,\psi(y))\,d\mathscr K_{\rm fix}(P)(y)
=
E_{P^{\otimes n}\otimes P_{XA}^{\otimes m}}
\!\left[\bigl(\widetilde T_\psi-\tau(P)\bigr)^2\right].
\]
Thus the worst-case on-class decision risk of \(\psi\) is exactly the worst-case mean-squared risk of \(\widetilde T_\psi\).  Since \(\widetilde T_\psi\) is one admissible estimator in \(\mathcal T_{n,m,d}\), \cref{obj:def:minimax-risk} is no larger than that worst-case risk; taking the infimum over \(\psi\) gives the displayed comparison.  Combining it with the parametric floor gives
\[
\frac{\alpha_{\rm floor}}{n}
\le
\mathfrak R_{\rm dec}.
\] % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.commonMarginal_explicit_parametric_floor

\item If \(\Delta=0\), then the desired lower bound is \(0\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\), which follows from the preceding floor since \(n\ge1\).  Hence assume for the rest of the proof that
\[
0<\Delta.
\]
The variance-scale hypothesis and \(C_\epsilon>0\) give
\[
C_\epsilon kBa\le \frac{\Delta^2}{65536}.
\]
By \cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-recipe-target-concentration}, for each \(b\in\{0,1\}\),
\[
\Pi_b\!\left\{P:\frac{\Delta}{4}<|\tau(P)-\theta_b|\right\}
\le
\frac{512\,C_\epsilon kBa}{\Delta^2}
\le
\frac1{128}
\le
\frac1{16}.
\]
The assumed common-marginal Poisson-prior bounds also include the raw-center separation
\[
\delta_\epsilon k a\le |\theta_1-\theta_0|=\Delta,
\]
which is the center-separation hypothesis required below.
By \cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-recipe-scale-tail}, again for each \(b\in\{0,1\}\),
\[
\Pi_b\{P:s_+(P)<1/2\}
\le
4C_\epsilon kBa
\le
\frac{\Delta^2}{16384}.
\] % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.commonMarginal_prior_target_concentration

\item The two fuzzy hypotheses with centers \(\theta_0,\theta_1\), radius \(\Delta/4\), prior tail bounds \(1/16,1/16\), and predictive total variation bound \(1/8\) give the raw lower bound as follows.  For a measurable raw rule \(\varphi\), define
\[
\mathcal B_b(\varphi)
:=
\int^{+}
\int^{+}
\ell_\epsilon(P,\varphi(y))\,d\mathscr K_{\rm raw}(P)(y)\,d\Pi_b(P)
\in\overline{\mathbb R}_+,
\qquad b\in\{0,1\}.
\]
These are the branch Bayes decision risks for the class-gated clipped loss.  Since the two branch priors are supported on \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), the integrand on the support is
\[
\ell_\epsilon(P,\varphi(y))
=
\bigl(\operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]}(\varphi(y))-\tau(P)\bigr)^2.
\]
Let \(\mathsf Q_b\) be the branch predictive laws from step 2, and first suppose \(\theta_0\le\theta_1\).  Put
\[
\theta_{\rm mid}:=\frac{\theta_0+\theta_1}{2},
\qquad
\mathsf A_\varphi
:=
\bigl\{y:\operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]}(\varphi(y))\ge\theta_{\rm mid}\bigr\},
\]
and define the good prior events
\[
\mathsf G_0:=\bigl\{P:|\tau(P)-\theta_0|\le\Delta/4\bigr\},
\qquad
\mathsf G_1:=\bigl\{P:|\tau(P)-\theta_1|\le\Delta/4\bigr\}.
\]
Because \(\theta_1-\theta_0=|\theta_1-\theta_0|\ge\Delta\), on \(\mathsf G_0\cap\mathsf A_\varphi\) the clipped decision is at least \(\Delta/4\) above \(\tau(P)\), while on \(\mathsf G_1\cap\mathsf A_\varphi^c\) it is at least \(\Delta/4\) below \(\tau(P)\).  Hence
\[
\mathcal B_0(\varphi)
\ge
\frac{\Delta^2}{16}
\Pr_{\Pi_0\otimes\mathscr K_{\rm raw}}(\mathsf G_0\cap\mathsf A_\varphi),
\qquad
\mathcal B_1(\varphi)
\ge
\frac{\Delta^2}{16}
\Pr_{\Pi_1\otimes\mathscr K_{\rm raw}}(\mathsf G_1\cap\mathsf A_\varphi^c).
\]
The concentration bounds from step 4 give
\[
\Pr_{\Pi_0\otimes\mathscr K_{\rm raw}}(\mathsf G_0^c)\le\frac1{16},
\qquad
\Pr_{\Pi_1\otimes\mathscr K_{\rm raw}}(\mathsf G_1^c)\le\frac1{16},
\]
and therefore
\[
\mathcal B_0(\varphi)
\ge
\frac{\Delta^2}{16}\left(\mathsf Q_0(\mathsf A_\varphi)-\frac1{16}\right),
\qquad
\mathcal B_1(\varphi)
\ge
\frac{\Delta^2}{16}\left(1-\mathsf Q_1(\mathsf A_\varphi)-\frac1{16}\right).
\]
Using \(\max\{x_0,x_1\}\ge(x_0+x_1)/2\) and the total-variation bound,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\max\{\mathcal B_0(\varphi),\mathcal B_1(\varphi)\}
&\ge
\frac{\Delta^2}{32}
\left(1-\bigl(\mathsf Q_1(\mathsf A_\varphi)-\mathsf Q_0(\mathsf A_\varphi)\bigr)-\frac1{16}-\frac1{16}\right)\\
&\ge
\frac{\Delta^2}{32}
\left(1-\frac18-\frac1{16}-\frac1{16}\right)
=
\frac{3\Delta^2}{128}.
\end{aligned}
\]
If \(\theta_1\le\theta_0\), the same calculation is applied with the two branches interchanged.  Thus, for every measurable raw rule \(\varphi\),
\[
\frac{3\Delta^2}{128}
\le
\max\Bigl\{
\mathcal B_0(\varphi),\mathcal B_1(\varphi)
\Bigr\}
\]
in \(\overline{\mathbb R}_+\). % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.commonMarginal_raw_fuzzy_lower

\item The loss is bounded by
\[
0\le \ell_\epsilon(P,z)\le 4
\]
because both \(\operatorname{clip}_{[-1,1]}(z)\) and \(\tau(P)\) lie in \([-1,1]\) on the model class, and the loss is \(0\) off the model class.  The Poisson retention tails used to transfer raw rules to fixed-sample rules are as follows.  For any integer \(\mathfrak n\ge1\) and any Poisson mean \(\lambda\ge32768\,\mathfrak n\),
\[
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(\lambda)<\mathfrak n\}
\le
\exp(-\lambda/8)
\le
\frac1{4096\,\mathfrak n}.
\]
Indeed, with \(z_\lambda=\lambda/8\),
\[
\sqrt{2\lambda z_\lambda}=\lambda/2,
\]
and \(\{\operatorname{Poi}(\lambda)<\mathfrak n\}\subseteq
\{\lambda-\operatorname{Poi}(\lambda)>\lambda/2\}\); the Poisson Bernstein lower-tail bound gives the exponential term, and \(\exp(-z_\lambda)\le 1/z_\lambda\le1/(4096\mathfrak n)\).

On the event \(s_+(P)\ge1/2\), the labeled Poisson mean satisfies
\[
u\,s_+(P)\ge 32768\,n,
\]
so the labeled retention failure probability is at most \(1/(4096n)\).  Similarly,
\[
v\,s_+(P)\ge 32768\,(n+m),
\]
and the event of retaining fewer than \(m\) auxiliary observations is contained in the event of retaining fewer than \(n+m\) observations; hence the auxiliary retention failure probability is at most
\[
\frac1{4096(n+m)}\le \frac1{4096n}.
\] % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.commonMarginal_poisson_lower_tail

\item We next transfer the raw fuzzy lower bound to the fixed experiment by the prefix coupling.  Fix any measurable fixed-sample decision rule \(\psi\).  Define its transferred raw rule \(\varphi_\psi\) as follows.  Given an ordered raw output \(y_{\rm raw}\), set
\[
\varphi_\psi(y_{\rm raw})
=
\begin{cases}
\psi\bigl(\text{the first \(n\) labeled marks and the first \(m\) auxiliary marks in \(y_{\rm raw}\)}\bigr),
& \text{if those marks are present},\\
\zeta_\star,& \text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\]
The rule uses only the first \(m\) auxiliary marks, while the raw auxiliary Poisson process has intensity \(v\,s_+(P)=65536(n+m)s_+(P)\); the auxiliary tail estimate below therefore bounds the event of having fewer than \(m\) auxiliary marks by the larger-threshold event of having fewer than \(n+m\).  Here \(\zeta_\star\in\mathbb R\) is any fixed real-valued action.  For a law \(P\), let
\[
\mathsf F_P
:=
\bigl\{\text{the ordered raw draw contains fewer than \(n\) labeled marks or fewer than \(m\) auxiliary marks}\bigr\}.
\]
On \(\mathsf F_P^c\), the first \(n\) labeled marks and first \(m\) auxiliary marks have exactly the fixed-sample law \(\mathscr K_{\rm fix}(P)\), because the ordered raw experiment is generated by independent Poisson samples from the same labeled and auxiliary mark kernels and the fixed experiment keeps the first prescribed counts.

For branch \(b\), the exceptional probability under the branch prior predictive is bounded by integrating the conditional failure probability.  Splitting according to the scale event gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\Pr_{\Pi_b\otimes\mathscr K_{\rm raw}}(\mathsf F_P)
&\le
\Pi_b\{P:s_+(P)<1/2\} \\
&\quad+
\int_{\{P:s_+(P)\ge1/2\}}
\left[
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(u s_+(P))<n\}
+
\Pr\{\operatorname{Poi}(v s_+(P))<m\}
\right]d\Pi_b(P).
\end{aligned}
\]
The scale-tail bound from step 4 and the Poisson retention bounds from step 6 therefore yield, for each \(b\in\{0,1\}\),
\[
\Pr_{\Pi_b\otimes\mathscr K_{\rm raw}}(\mathsf F_P)
\le
\frac{\Delta^2}{16384}
+\frac1{4096n}
+\frac1{4096n}.
\]
Since \(0\le\ell_\epsilon\le4\), the Bayes risk of the transferred raw rule satisfies
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal B_b(\varphi_\psi)
&\le
\int
\int \ell_\epsilon(P,\psi(y))\,d\mathscr K_{\rm fix}(P)(y)\,d\Pi_b(P)\\
&\quad+
4\left(
\frac{\Delta^2}{16384}
+\frac1{4096n}
+\frac1{4096n}
\right)\\
&\le
\sup_{P}\int \ell_\epsilon(P,\psi(y))\,d\mathscr K_{\rm fix}(P)(y)
+
4\left(
\frac{\Delta^2}{16384}
+\frac1{4096n}
