+\frac1{4096n}
\right).
\end{aligned}
\]
Combining this with the raw fuzzy bound above gives, for every measurable \(\psi\),
\[
\frac{3\Delta^2}{128}
\le
\sup_{P}\int \ell_\epsilon(P,\psi(y))\,d\mathscr K_{\rm fix}(P)(y)
+
4\left(
\frac{\Delta^2}{16384}
+\frac1{4096n}
+\frac1{4096n}
\right).
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\frac{3\Delta^2}{128}
-
4\left(
\frac{\Delta^2}{16384}
+\frac1{4096n}
+\frac1{4096n}
\right)
\le
\sup_{P}\int \ell_\epsilon(P,\psi(y))\,d\mathscr K_{\rm fix}(P)(y).
\]
Taking the infimum over \(\psi\) gives
\[
\frac{3\Delta^2}{128}
-
4\left(
\frac{\Delta^2}{16384}
+\frac1{4096n}
+\frac1{4096n}
\right)
\le
\mathfrak R_{\rm dec}.
\]
The floor inequality from step 3 also gives \(\alpha_{\rm floor}/n\le\mathfrak R_{\rm dec}\), so
\[
\max\left\{
\frac{3\Delta^2}{128}
-
4\left(
\frac{\Delta^2}{16384}
+\frac1{4096n}
+\frac1{4096n}
\right),
\,
\frac{\alpha_{\rm floor}}{n}
\right\}
\le
\mathfrak R_{\rm dec}.
\]
For the reverse comparison to step 3, start with any measurable estimator \(T\) in
\cref{obj:def:minimax-risk} and use the same function as the fixed-sample decision rule
\(\psi_T\).  On \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), clipping cannot increase squared error because
\(\tau(P)\in[-1,1]\), while off the model class the class-gated loss is zero.  Hence the
worst-case decision risk of \(\psi_T\) is no larger than the worst-case mean-squared risk of
\(T\).  Taking the infimum over \(T\) gives
\[
\mathfrak R_{\rm dec}
\le
R_\epsilon(n,m,d),
\]
and consequently
\[
\max\left\{
\frac{3\Delta^2}{128}
-
4\left(
\frac{\Delta^2}{16384}
+\frac1{4096n}
+\frac1{4096n}
\right),
\,
\frac{\alpha_{\rm floor}}{n}
\right\}
\le
R_\epsilon(n,m,d).
\] % lean: Causalean.Stat.FiniteRaoBlackwell.IndependentPoissonPrefix.RandomScaleMinimaxTransfer.randomScale_twoFuzzy_minimax_lower_transfer

\item It remains only to read off the deterministic arithmetic.  From the floor bound,
\[
R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\ge \frac{\alpha_{\rm floor}}{n}>0.
\]

First suppose
\[
\frac1n\le \Delta^2.
\]
Then
\[
4\left(\frac{\Delta^2}{16384}
+\frac1{4096n}
+\frac1{4096n}\right)
\le
4\left(\frac{\Delta^2}{16384}
+\frac{2\Delta^2}{4096}\right)
=
\frac{9\Delta^2}{4096}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\frac{3\Delta^2}{128}
-
4\left(\frac{\Delta^2}{16384}
+\frac1{4096n}
+\frac1{4096n}\right)
\ge
\frac{87\Delta^2}{4096}
\ge
\frac{\Delta^2}{65536}.
\]
The first branch of the maximum in the preceding display gives
\[
R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\ge \frac{\Delta^2}{65536}.
\]

Now suppose
\[
\Delta^2<\frac1n.
\]
Since \(\alpha_{\rm floor}=1/100\),
\[
\frac{\Delta^2}{65536}
<
\frac1{65536\,n}
\le
\frac1{100\,n}
=
\frac{\alpha_{\rm floor}}{n}.
\]
The second branch of the maximum gives the same conclusion.  Combining the two cases,
\[
R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\ge \frac{\Delta^2}{65536}.
\] % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.fixed_sample_common_marginal_recipe_transfer_rule
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-recipe-transfer} converts the raw recipe separation into a lower bound for \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) under the fixed annotation sample sizes. The total-variation requirement supplies the testing affinity, and the variance-scale requirement keeps the normalization fluctuations small relative to the squared raw gap.

The Poisson construction is then converted to a general fixed-size annotation experiment. The transfer statement tracks the raw normalization explicitly, so the resulting lower bound applies to the minimax risk in \cref{obj:def:minimax-risk}.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:fixed-sample-common-marginal-transfer}[Fixed-sample transfer]
There exist numerical constants \(C_{\mathrm{fixed}}>0\) and \(c_{\mathrm{risk}}>0\) such that the following holds.  For every \(0<\epsilon<1/2\) and every \(C_\epsilon>0\), there is a constant \(c_{\epsilon,C_\epsilon}>0\) such that, for every common-marginal calibration at overlap level \(\epsilon\) with positive calibration constants and dual gap \(\delta_\epsilon\le 1/4\), every \(\rho\in\mathbb R\), every \(n,m,d,L,k\in\mathbb N\), every \(B,a\in\mathbb R\), and every common-marginal recipe \(R\) at these indices, the implication below holds.

Write \(G_R\) for the recipe's rare-cell generator measure and \(\theta_{R,b}(w)\) for its raw target on branch \(b\).  Let
\[
\Delta
=
\int \theta_{R,1}(w)\,dG_R(w)
-
\int \theta_{R,0}(w)\,dG_R(w).
\]
Assume:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Sample and dimension.)} \(n\ge 1\) and \(d\ge 2\).
\item \textbf{(Witness data.)} The recipe carries a common-marginal Poisson-prior witness at labeled and auxiliary intensities
\[
u=C_{\mathrm{fixed}}n,
\qquad
v=C_{\mathrm{fixed}}(n+m).
\]
Equivalently, the witness supplies
\[
(u+v)B\le b_\epsilon L,
\qquad
B>0,
\qquad
a=\gamma_\epsilon B/L^2,
\qquad
ka\le \delta_\epsilon,
\]
identifies the recipe's labeled and auxiliary intensities with \(u\) and \(v\), and satisfies the corresponding common-marginal bounds at dual gap \(\delta_\epsilon\), variance-bound constant \(C_\epsilon\), and likelihood-decay parameter \(\rho\).
\item \textbf{(Testing distance.)} The raw prior-predictive mixture laws \(M_0\) and \(M_1\) generated by the two branches of the recipe satisfy
\[
\operatorname{TV}(M_0,M_1)\le \frac18 .
\]
\item \textbf{(Concentration scale.)} The realized scale obeys
\[
kBa\le c_{\epsilon,C_\epsilon}\Delta^2 .
\]
\end{itemize}
Then the minimax risk \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) from \cref{obj:def:minimax-risk} satisfies
\[
c_{\mathrm{risk}}\Delta^2
\le
R_\epsilon(n,m,d).
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:fixed-sample-common-marginal-transfer}]
Take
\[
C_{\mathrm{fixed}}=65536,
\qquad
c_{\mathrm{risk}}=\frac1{65536}.
\]
Fix \(0<\epsilon<1/2\) and a positive variance-bound constant \(C_\epsilon\), and set
\[
c_{\epsilon,C_\epsilon}:=\frac{1}{65536\,C_\epsilon}>0.
\]
Let a calibration, a recipe, and all parameters satisfy the hypotheses of the statement.  Put
\[
\bar\theta_b:=\int \theta_b(w)\,dG(w),\qquad b\in\{0,1\},
\]
so that the raw-center separation in the statement is
\[
\Delta=\bar\theta_1-\bar\theta_0.
\]
The recipe's raw prior centers are exactly these two numbers:
\[
\bar\theta_1-\bar\theta_0
=
\int \theta_1(w)\,dG(w)-\int \theta_0(w)\,dG(w).
\]
The Poisson-prior witness in the hypotheses identifies the recipe's labeled and auxiliary intensities as
\[
u=65536\,n,
\qquad
v=65536\,(n+m).
\]
Separately, the concentration-scale hypothesis, the definition of \(c_{\epsilon,C_\epsilon}\), and \(\Delta=\bar\theta_1-\bar\theta_0\) give
\[
kBa
\le
c_{\epsilon,C_\epsilon}\Delta^2
=
\frac{\Delta^2}{65536\,C_\epsilon}
=
\frac{1}{65536\,C_\epsilon}
\left|\bar\theta_1-\bar\theta_0\right|^2.
\]
Together with \(n\ge1\), \(d\ge2\), and \(\operatorname{TV}(M_0,M_1)\le1/8\), these are precisely the hypotheses of \cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-recipe-transfer}, applied with the present calibration, recipe, variance-bound constant \(C_\epsilon\), likelihood-decay parameter \(\rho\), and intensities \(u=65536n\), \(v=65536(n+m)\).  That result yields, for the minimax risk \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) of \cref{obj:def:minimax-risk},
\[
R_\epsilon(n,m,d)
\ge
\frac{1}{65536}
\left|\bar\theta_1-\bar\theta_0\right|^2.
\]
Since \(|\bar\theta_1-\bar\theta_0|^2=\Delta^2\) and \(c_{\mathrm{risk}}=1/65536\), this is exactly
\[
c_{\mathrm{risk}}\Delta^2\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).
\] % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.fixed_sample_common_marginal_transfer
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:fixed-sample-common-marginal-transfer} is the bridge from a Poisson fuzzy-hypothesis calculation to the fixed-\(n,m\) risk. The testing-distance condition supplies indistinguishability, the concentration-scale condition controls the random raw normalization, and the conclusion converts the raw-center separation \(\Delta\) into a lower bound for \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\).

A separate one-dimensional ingredient records the unavoidable parametric contribution of the labeled sample. It is used together with the rare-cell construction to cover regimes in which the lower-bound rate is governed by outcome labels.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:parametric-label-floor}[Parametric label floor]
Let \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) be the minimax risk in \cref{obj:def:minimax-risk}. There exists a universal constant \(c>0\) such that, whenever
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Overlap level.)} \(0<\epsilon<1/2\),
\item \textbf{(Labeled sample size.)} \(n\ge 1\),
\item \textbf{(Auxiliary sample size.)} \(m\) is a nonnegative integer,
\item \textbf{(Alphabet size.)} \(d\ge 2\),
\end{itemize}
the minimax risk satisfies
\[
R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\ge \frac{c}{n}.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:parametric-label-floor}]
Set \(c=1/100\). Fix \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), \(n\ge1\), \(m\ge0\), and \(d\ge2\). Define
\[
\eta_n:=\frac{2}{5\sqrt n}.
\]
Then \(\eta_n\ge0\), and
\[
\eta_n^2=\frac{4}{25n}.
\]
Consider two laws \(P_{\mathrm{null}}\) and \(P_{\mathrm{pert}}\) in \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) with uniform covariate distribution on \([d]\), propensity \(e_x=1/2\) in every cell, control regression \(1/2\) in every cell, and treated regression respectively \(1/2\) and \(1/2+\eta_n\). Since \(n\ge1\), \(\eta_n\le2/5\), hence \(1/2+\eta_n\le1\). The overlap condition follows from \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), so both laws belong to \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\). % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.parametric_label_floor

For the labeled samples, the parametric total-variation bound applies once
\[
n\cdot \frac{(1/2)\eta_n^2}{(1/2)(1-1/2)}\le \log 2 .
\]
Using the displayed value of \(\eta_n^2\), the left side is \(8/25\), and \(8/25\le \log 2\). Therefore
\[
\operatorname{TV}\!\left(P_{\mathrm{null}}^{\otimes n},
P_{\mathrm{pert}}^{\otimes n}\right)\le \frac12 .
\]
% lean: Causalean.Estimation.MinimaxATE.Parametric.tvDist_productLaw_le_half

The auxiliary treatment-covariate marginals of the two laws are equal:
\[
(P_{\mathrm{null}})_{XA}=(P_{\mathrm{pert}})_{XA}.
\]
Indeed, both laws have the same uniform covariate distribution and the same propensity \(e_x=1/2\) in every cell; summing over the binary outcome coordinate gives the same mass for each \((x,a)\). Hence their \(m\)-fold auxiliary product laws are identical. The product total-variation inequality gives
\[
\operatorname{TV}\!\left(
P_{\mathrm{null}}^{\otimes n}\otimes (P_{\mathrm{null}})_{XA}^{\otimes m},
P_{\mathrm{pert}}^{\otimes n}\otimes (P_{\mathrm{pert}})_{XA}^{\otimes m}
\right)
\le
\operatorname{TV}\!\left(P_{\mathrm{null}}^{\otimes n},P_{\mathrm{pert}}^{\otimes n}\right)+0
\le \frac12 .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.parametricFloor_auxMarginal_eq

The two average treatment effects are
\[
\tau(P_{\mathrm{null}})=0,
\qquad
\tau(P_{\mathrm{pert}})=\eta_n.
\]
The first identity follows because the two outcome regressions coincide in every cell, and the second because every cell has treated-minus-control contrast \(\eta_n\) and the cell masses sum to one. Thus, with \(s_n:=\eta_n/2\),
\[
2s_n=\eta_n
\le
\bigl|\tau(P_{\mathrm{null}})-\tau(P_{\mathrm{pert}})\bigr|.
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.parametricFloorPertLaw_ate

We now record the two-point risk consequence used for \cref{obj:def:minimax-risk}. Let \(T\in\mathcal T_{n,m,d}\) be any estimator, and let
\[
\mathsf A_0:=\{z:s_n\le |T(z)-\tau(P_{\mathrm{null}})|\},
\qquad
\mathsf A_1:=\{z:s_n\le |T(z)-\tau(P_{\mathrm{pert}})|\}.
\]
The testing bound at separation \(2s_n\), together with the preceding total-variation bound, yields
\[
\max\Bigl\{
\Pr_{P_{\mathrm{null}}^{\otimes n}\otimes(P_{\mathrm{null}})_{XA}^{\otimes m}}(\mathsf A_0),
\Pr_{P_{\mathrm{pert}}^{\otimes n}\otimes(P_{\mathrm{pert}})_{XA}^{\otimes m}}(\mathsf A_1)
\Bigr\}
\ge \frac14 .
\]
Moreover, by the elementary inequality
\[
s_n^2\,\Pr\{s_n\le |T-\theta|\}
\le
\mathbb E[(T-\theta)^2],
\]
applied under each of the two laws, the larger of the two mean-squared risks is at least \(s_n^2/4\). Taking the supremum over \(P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) and then the infimum over \(T\in\mathcal T_{n,m,d}\) gives
\[
\frac{s_n^2}{4}\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.annotationMinimaxRisk_ge_two_model_tv

Finally,
\[
\frac{s_n^2}{4}
=
\frac{(\eta_n/2)^2}{4}
=
\frac{1}{100\,n}.
\]
Consequently \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\ge 1/(100n)\), which proves the claim with the universal constant \(c=1/100\). % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.parametric_label_floor
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:parametric-label-floor} fixes the labeled-outcome scale uniformly over auxiliary sample sizes and alphabet sizes. This lower bound matches the \(1/n\) component in \cref{obj:def:frontier-rate-handle}, so the remaining common-marginal construction can focus on the alphabet-dependent contribution.

One final finite-product identity records how independent coordinates can be embedded into a larger product experiment while adding a distinguished coordinate and deterministic padding. This bookkeeping is useful when the rare-cell construction is placed inside a larger finite alphabet with reservoir coordinates.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:finite-product-padding-identity}[Finite product padding identity]
Let \(I\) and \(J\) be finite index sets, and let \(\mathsf Y\) be a measurable space whose singletons are measurable. Suppose the following conditions hold:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Embedding.)} \(f:I\to J\) is injective.
\item \textbf{(Distinguished coordinate.)} \(r\in J\) satisfies \(r\ne f(i)\) for every \(i\in I\).
\item \textbf{(Coordinate laws.)} \(\mu_i\), \(i\in I\), and \(\nu\) are probability measures on \(\mathsf Y\), and \(y_0\in\mathsf Y\).
\end{itemize}
Define \(\mathsf A:\mathsf Y^I\times\mathsf Y\to\mathsf Y^J\) by
\[
\mathsf A(z,y)(j)=
\begin{cases}
y, & j=r,\\
z_i, & j=f(i)\ \text{for the unique } i\in I,\\
y_0, & j\in J\setminus\bigl(\{r\}\cup f(I)\bigr).
\end{cases}
\]
Define the coordinate laws \(\mathsf H_j\), \(j\in J\), by
\[
\mathsf H_j=
\begin{cases}
\nu, & j=r,\\
\mu_i, & j=f(i)\ \text{for the unique } i\in I,\\
\delta_{y_0}, & j\in J\setminus\bigl(\{r\}\cup f(I)\bigr).
\end{cases}
\]
Then
\[
\mathsf A_\#\left(\left(\bigotimes_{i\in I}\mu_i\right)\otimes\nu\right)
=
\bigotimes_{j\in J}\mathsf H_j .
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:finite-product-padding-identity}]
\begin{enumerate}
\item
Write
\[
\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}
  :=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in I}\mu_i\right)\otimes \nu
  \quad\text{on }\mathsf Y^I\times\mathsf Y,
\qquad
\Lambda_{\mathrm{out}}
  :=\bigotimes_{j\in J}\mathsf H_j
  \quad\text{on }\mathsf Y^J .
\]
The map \(\mathsf A\) is measurable. Indeed, for each \(j\in J\), the \(j\)-th coordinate of
\(\mathsf A(z,y)\) is the second projection if \(j=r\), is the \(i\)-th coordinate projection
from \(z\) if \(j=f(i)\), and is the constant \(y_0\) on the remaining coordinates. The condition
\(r\ne f(i)\) separates the distinguished coordinate from the embedded ones, and injectivity of
\(f\) makes the embedded coordinate \(i\) unique. Since \(I\) and \(J\) are finite, coordinatewise
measurability gives measurability of \(\mathsf A\). It is therefore enough, by uniqueness of finite
product measures on measurable rectangles, to prove that for every family of measurable sets
\[
(\mathsf G_j)_{j\in J},
\qquad
\mathsf G_j\in\mathcal Y ,
\]
one has
\[
\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}\!\left(\mathsf A^{-1}
  \{w\in\mathsf Y^J:\forall j\in J,\ w_j\in\mathsf G_j\}\right)
=
\prod_{j\in J}\mathsf H_j(\mathsf G_j).
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.pi_prod_map_finitePadding

\item
First suppose that there is an unused coordinate \(j_\circ\in J\) such that
\[
j_\circ\ne r,\qquad
j_\circ\ne f(i)\ \text{for every }i\in I,
\qquad
y_0\notin \mathsf G_{j_\circ}.
\]
For every \((z,y)\in\mathsf Y^I\times\mathsf Y\), the definition of \(\mathsf A\) gives
\[
\mathsf A(z,y)_{j_\circ}=y_0.
\]
Thus
\[
\mathsf A^{-1}\{w:\forall j,\ w_j\in\mathsf G_j\}=\varnothing,
\]
so the left-hand side is \(0\). On the right-hand side, the same coordinate has
\[
\mathsf H_{j_\circ}=\delta_{y_0},
\qquad
\mathsf H_{j_\circ}(\mathsf G_{j_\circ})
  =\delta_{y_0}(\mathsf G_{j_\circ})=0,
\]
because \(y_0\notin\mathsf G_{j_\circ}\). Hence
\[
\prod_{j\in J}\mathsf H_j(\mathsf G_j)=0,
\]
and the required rectangle identity holds in this case.
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.pi_prod_map_finitePadding

\item
It remains to consider the complementary case:
\[
\forall j\in J,\quad
j\ne r\ \text{and}\ \bigl(\forall i\in I,\ j\ne f(i)\bigr)
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
y_0\in\mathsf G_j .
\]
Then the preimage of the rectangle is exactly
\[
\mathsf A^{-1}\{w:\forall j,\ w_j\in\mathsf G_j\}
=
\{z\in\mathsf Y^I:\forall i\in I,\ z_i\in\mathsf G_{f(i)}\}
\times \mathsf G_r .
\]
For the forward inclusion, membership in the rectangle gives the \(r\)-coordinate condition
\(y\in\mathsf G_r\), and for each \(i\in I\) it gives
\[
z_i=\mathsf A(z,y)_{f(i)}\in\mathsf G_{f(i)},
\]
using \(r\ne f(i)\) and injectivity of \(f\). Conversely, if \(z_i\in\mathsf G_{f(i)}\) for all
\(i\) and \(y\in\mathsf G_r\), then each coordinate \(j\in J\) is checked by the three alternatives
in the definition of \(\mathsf A\): the coordinate \(r\) gives \(y\), a coordinate \(f(i)\) gives
\(z_i\), and an unused coordinate gives \(y_0\in\mathsf G_j\) by the displayed complementary
condition.
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.pi_prod_map_finitePadding

\item
Using the preceding preimage identity and the finite product formula,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}\!\left(\mathsf A^{-1}
  \{w:\forall j,\ w_j\in\mathsf G_j\}\right)
&=
\left(\bigotimes_{i\in I}\mu_i\right)
  \{z:\forall i,\ z_i\in\mathsf G_{f(i)}\}\,
  \nu(\mathsf G_r) \\
&=
\left(\prod_{i\in I}\mu_i(\mathsf G_{f(i)})\right)\nu(\mathsf G_r).
\end{aligned}
\]
The product over \(J\) has the same value. The distinguished coordinate contributes
\[
\mathsf H_r(\mathsf G_r)=\nu(\mathsf G_r),
\]
each embedded coordinate contributes, for \(i\in I\),
\[
\mathsf H_{f(i)}(\mathsf G_{f(i)})=\mu_i(\mathsf G_{f(i)}),
\]
again by \(r\ne f(i)\) and injectivity of \(f\), and every unused coordinate \(j\) contributes
\[
\mathsf H_j(\mathsf G_j)=\delta_{y_0}(\mathsf G_j)=1
\]
by the complementary condition. Since \(f\) is injective, the embedded coordinates are indexed
without repetition, and therefore
\[
\prod_{j\in J}\mathsf H_j(\mathsf G_j)
=
\nu(\mathsf G_r)\prod_{i\in I}\mu_i(\mathsf G_{f(i)}).
\]
This agrees with the preceding expression for the pushforward on every measurable rectangle, so
\[
\mathsf A_\#\Lambda_{\mathrm{in}}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{out}},
\]
which is the desired identity.
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.pi_prod_map_finitePadding
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\Cref{obj:lem:finite-product-padding-identity} formalizes the product-measure preservation behind this padding step. Injectivity keeps the rare coordinates distinct, the distinguished coordinate carries the added law, and the remaining coordinates are fixed at point masses, so the assembled law is the intended product over the larger index set.

The final calibration statements choose constants so that the rare-cell prior works uniformly across the intensity range needed in \cref{obj:thm:common-marginal-converse}. The first gives a ready-to-use recipe with explicit counting, variance, and total-variation controls; the second packages these ingredients into the common-marginal certificate family used by the main converse.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:common-marginal-uniform-intensity}[Uniform intensity common marginal]
For every overlap level \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), there are positive constants
\[
r_\epsilon,\quad D_\epsilon,\quad b_{0,\epsilon},\quad b_\epsilon,\quad
\gamma_\epsilon,\quad q_\epsilon,\quad c_\epsilon,\quad \delta_\epsilon,\quad C_\epsilon
\]
and a number \(0<\rho_\epsilon<1\), with \(c_\epsilon\le 1/4\), such that the following calibration facts hold:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Bandwidth slack.)} \(131072\,b_{0,\epsilon}\le b_\epsilon\).
\item \textbf{(Degree floor.)} \(2\le D_\epsilon\).
\item \textbf{(Rare-cell scale.)}
\[
q_\epsilon\gamma_\epsilon b_{0,\epsilon}\le c_\epsilon,
\qquad
q_\epsilon=
\frac{c_\epsilon}{2\gamma_\epsilon b_{0,\epsilon}},
\qquad
4\le q_\epsilon .
\]
\item \textbf{(Dimension scale.)}
\[
\delta_\epsilon r_\epsilon\le \frac1{65536},
\qquad
\delta_\epsilon\le \frac{c_\epsilon^4}{16},
\qquad
\delta_\epsilon\le
\left(\frac{c_\epsilon\gamma_\epsilon b_{0,\epsilon}}
{2(D_\epsilon+1)}\right)^2 .
\]
\item \textbf{(Variance and counting calibration.)}
\[
8(D_\epsilon+1)b_{0,\epsilon}
\le \frac{c_\epsilon^3}{65536\,C_\epsilon},
\]
\[
\frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)}
{(1/(65536\,C_\epsilon))c_\epsilon^2\gamma_\epsilon}
\le q_\epsilon,
\]
and
\[
\left\{
\frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)^2}
{(1/(65536\,C_\epsilon))c_\epsilon^2\gamma_\epsilon}
\right\}^2
\le r_\epsilon .
\]
\item \textbf{(Mixture decay.)} For every integer \(n\ge1\),
\[
C_\epsilon(65536n)c_\epsilon
\rho_\epsilon^{\lceil D_\epsilon\log(en)\rceil}
\le \frac18 .
\]
\end{itemize}

For every \(n,m,L,d,k\) and every \(u,v,B,a\), if
\begin{itemize}
\item \(n\ge1\), \(L\ge2\), and \(d\ge2\);
\item \(u\ge0\), \(v\ge0\), and \(u+v>0\);
\item \(B>0\), \((u+v)B\le b_\epsilon L\), and
\[
a=\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{L^2};
\]
\item \(ka\le c_\epsilon\) and \(k+1\le d\),
\end{itemize}
then there is a common-marginal rare-cell recipe with \(k\) rare cells at scale \((a,B,L)\) whose labeled and auxiliary Poisson intensities are \(u\) and \(v\). Its null and alternative priors are the two branch priors of the recipe, and they satisfy:
\begin{itemize}
\item the recipe is in the finite-atom construction regime, and both priors are supported on \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\);
\item the induced random treatment-covariate tables \(P_{XA}\) have exactly the same law under the two priors;
\item the raw total mass has mean one;
\item the raw target centers obey
\[
c_\epsilon ka
\le
\left|
\theta_1-\theta_0
\right|,
\]
where \(\theta_1\) and \(\theta_0\) are the alternative and null raw prior centers;
\item the raw total-mass variance is at most
\[
C_\epsilon kBa,
\]
and the larger of the two raw target variances is at most
\[
C_\epsilon kBa;
\]
\item in the raw Poisson experiment with labeled intensity \(u\) and auxiliary intensity \(v\), the two prior-predictive mixture laws have total variation distance at most
\[
C_\epsilon uka\,\rho_\epsilon^L .
\]
\end{itemize}
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-uniform-intensity}]
Fix \(0<\epsilon<1/2\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item By \cref{obj:lem:finite-alternation-duality}, with
\[
\kappa_\epsilon:=\frac{1-2\epsilon}{\epsilon}>0,
\]
there are \(\gamma_\epsilon>0\) and \(\bar c_\epsilon>0\) such that every \(L\ge2\) and \(B>0\), with \(a=\gamma_\epsilon B/L^2\), admit a finite signed certificate on \([a/\kappa_\epsilon,B]\), annihilating monomials through degree \(L\), whose signed integral of \(p/(p+a)\) is at least \(\bar c_\epsilon\). The one-coordinate marked-Poisson moment-matching estimate supplies constants \(\bar b_\epsilon>0\), \(\bar C_\epsilon>0\), and \(\rho_\epsilon\in(0,1)\) with the following uniform property for the complete normalized certificate object that generates the coordinate mixtures. For every finite index set \(\mathcal I\) and every normalized finite signed moment certificate
\[
\mathfrak C=\{(p_\iota,\omega_\iota):\iota\in\mathcal I\}
\]
of order \(L\), meaning that its signed measure \(\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal I}\omega_\iota\delta_{p_\iota}\) has normalized variation \(\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal I}|\omega_\iota|=1\) and the certificate data include the moment equations through degree \(L\), if its variation measure is supported on \([a/\kappa_\epsilon,B]\) and the support scale satisfies \((u+v)B\le \bar b_\epsilon L\), then the two branch marked-Poisson predictive laws \(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1\) associated with that same certificate obey
\[
\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1)
 \le \bar C_\epsilon\,u\,a\,\rho_\epsilon^L .
\] Set
\[
C_\epsilon:=\max\{\bar C_\epsilon,1\},\qquad
c_\epsilon:=\min\{\bar c_\epsilon,1/4\},\qquad
b_\epsilon:=\bar b_\epsilon .
\]
Let
\[
D_\epsilon
:=\max\left\{
2,\,
\frac{\max\{1,\log(8C_\epsilon 65536\,c_\epsilon)+2\}}
{-\log\rho_\epsilon}
\right\},
\qquad
s_\epsilon:=\frac1{65536\,C_\epsilon}.
\]
Define
\[
b_{0,\epsilon}
:=
\min\left\{
\frac{b_\epsilon}{131072},\,
\frac{c_\epsilon}{8\gamma_\epsilon},\,
\frac{s_\epsilon c_\epsilon^3}{8(D_\epsilon+1)}
\right\},
\]
\[
q_\epsilon:=\frac{c_\epsilon}{2\gamma_\epsilon b_{0,\epsilon}},
\qquad
r_\epsilon
:=
\left(
\max\left\{
1,\,
\frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)^2}{s_\epsilon c_\epsilon^2\gamma_\epsilon}
\right\}
\right)^2,
\]
and
\[
\delta_\epsilon
:=
\min\left\{
\frac{c_\epsilon^4}{16},\,
\left(\frac{c_\epsilon\gamma_\epsilon b_{0,\epsilon}}
{2(D_\epsilon+1)}\right)^2,\,
\frac{1}{65536\,r_\epsilon}
\right\}.
\]
All these constants are positive, \(0<\rho_\epsilon<1\), and \(c_\epsilon\le1/4\). The first five displayed calibrations follow immediately from the three minima and the definitions: \(131072\,b_{0,\epsilon}\le b_\epsilon\), \(D_\epsilon\ge2\),
\[
q_\epsilon\gamma_\epsilon b_{0,\epsilon}
=\frac{c_\epsilon}{2}\le c_\epsilon,\qquad
q_\epsilon=\frac{c_\epsilon}{2\gamma_\epsilon b_{0,\epsilon}},\qquad
q_\epsilon\ge4,
\]
and
\[
\delta_\epsilon r_\epsilon\le\frac1{65536},\qquad
\delta_\epsilon\le\frac{c_\epsilon^4}{16},\qquad
\delta_\epsilon\le
\left(\frac{c_\epsilon\gamma_\epsilon b_{0,\epsilon}}
{2(D_\epsilon+1)}\right)^2 .
\]
Moreover,
\[
8(D_\epsilon+1)b_{0,\epsilon}
\le
\frac{c_\epsilon^3}{65536\,C_\epsilon},
\]
and the same bound on \(b_{0,\epsilon}\) gives
\[
\frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)}
{(1/(65536\,C_\epsilon))c_\epsilon^2\gamma_\epsilon}
\le q_\epsilon .
\]
The definition of \(r_\epsilon\) gives
\[
\left\{
\frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)^2}
{(1/(65536\,C_\epsilon))c_\epsilon^2\gamma_\epsilon}
\right\}^2
\le r_\epsilon .
\]
Finally, put \(\lambda_\epsilon:=-\log\rho_\epsilon>0\) and
\(M_\epsilon:=8C_\epsilon 65536\,c_\epsilon\). Since
\[
\lambda_\epsilon D_\epsilon
\ge \max\{1,\log M_\epsilon+2\},
\]
for every \(n\ge1\),
\[
\rho_\epsilon^{\lceil D_\epsilon\log(en)\rceil}
\le \frac1{M_\epsilon n}.
\]
Thus
\[
C_\epsilon(65536n)c_\epsilon
\rho_\epsilon^{\lceil D_\epsilon\log(en)\rceil}
\le
\frac{C_\epsilon 65536\,n\,c_\epsilon}{8C_\epsilon 65536\,c_\epsilon\,n}
=\frac18 .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.common_marginal_uniform_intensity

\item Now fix \(n,m,L,d,k,u,v,B,a\) satisfying the hypotheses in the statement. By the bandwidth slack,
\[
(u+v)B\le b_\epsilon L=\bar b_\epsilon L,
\]
and \(a=\gamma_\epsilon B/L^2>0\). Applying \cref{obj:lem:finite-alternation-duality} at this \(L,B\) gives distinct points \(p_j\in[a/\kappa_\epsilon,B]\) and weights \(\omega_j\) with
\[
\sum_j|\omega_j|=1,\qquad
\sum_j \omega_j p_j^\ell=0\quad(0\le \ell\le L),
\qquad
c_\epsilon\le
\sum_j\omega_j\frac{p_j}{p_j+a}.
\]
Let
\[
\sigma:=\sum_j\omega_j\delta_{p_j},
\qquad
|\sigma|:=\sum_j|\omega_j|\delta_{p_j},
\]
and define the polar sign on all \(p\in\mathbb R\) by
\[
h(p):=\sum_j {\bf 1}_{\{p=p_j\}}\operatorname{sgn}(\omega_j).
\]
Then \(|\sigma|\) is a probability measure, \(|h(p)|\le1\), and
\[
\sigma=h\,|\sigma|,\qquad
c_\epsilon\le \int \frac{p}{p+a}\,d\sigma(p).
\]
Also, every certificate node satisfies \(p_j\ge a/\kappa_\epsilon>0\), so \(0\) is not one of the nodes. Hence the displayed definition gives \(h(0)=0\).
Define the zero-inflated scalar law
\[
\nu(E)
:=
\int_E \frac{a}{p+a}\,d|\sigma|(p)
+
\left(1-\int \frac{a}{p+a}\,d|\sigma|(p)\right){\bf 1}_E(0).
\]
Since \(p\ge a/\kappa_\epsilon>0\) on the support of \(|\sigma|\),
\[
0\le \frac{a}{p+a}\le 1
\quad |\sigma|\text{-a.e.}
\]
and \(|\sigma|\) has total mass one. Hence
\[
0\le \int \frac{a}{p+a}\,d|\sigma|(p)\le 1,
\]
so the coefficient of \(\delta_0\) is nonnegative and \(\nu\) is a probability measure supported on
\(\{0\}\cup[a/\kappa_\epsilon,B]\). Its mean \(\bar p:=\int p\,d\nu(p)\) is computed directly from the definition of \(\nu\): the atom at zero contributes nothing, and therefore
\[
\bar p
=
\int p\frac{a}{p+a}\,d|\sigma|(p)
=
a\int \frac{p}{p+a}\,d|\sigma|(p).
\]
Because \(0\le p/(p+a)\le1\) on the support, this gives
\[
0\le\bar p\le a .
\]
Let \(\mathsf G:=\nu^{\otimes k}\). For every vector \(\boldsymbol\xi\in\mathbb R^k\), set
\[
\widehat\xi_i
:=
\begin{cases}
\xi_i,& \xi_i\in\{p_j\}_j,\\
0,& \xi_i\notin\{p_j\}_j .
\end{cases}
\]
Under \(\mathsf G\), \(\widehat{\boldsymbol\xi}=\boldsymbol\xi\) almost surely, and the range of \(\widehat{\boldsymbol\xi}\) is finite. Choose one reservoir cell \(x_\star\in[d]\) and distinct rare cells \(x_1,\ldots,x_k\in[d]\setminus\{x_\star\}\), possible because \(k+1\le d\). Put
\[
p_\star:=1-k\bar p .
\]
Since \(k a\le c_\epsilon\le1/4\), one has \(p_\star\ge3/4>0\). Define the raw cell masses and raw total mass by
\[
\Omega_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)
:=
p_\star{\bf 1}_{\{x=x_\star\}}
+\sum_{i=1}^k \widehat\xi_i{\bf 1}_{\{x=x_i\}},
\qquad
S(\boldsymbol\xi)
:=
p_\star+\sum_{i=1}^k\widehat\xi_i .
\]
Then \(S(\boldsymbol\xi)>0\) and
\[
S(\boldsymbol\xi)=\sum_{x=1}^d\Omega_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x).
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.common_marginal_uniform_intensity

\item Define the propensity field on all cells by
\[
e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)
:=
\begin{cases}
1/2, & x=x_\star,\\
1/2, & x\ne x_\star\ \text{and}\ \sum_i\widehat\xi_i{\bf 1}_{\{x=x_i\}}=0,\\
\displaystyle
\epsilon\,
\frac{\sum_i\widehat\xi_i{\bf 1}_{\{x=x_i\}}+a}
{\sum_i\widehat\xi_i{\bf 1}_{\{x=x_i\}}},
& \text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\]
If the last line applies, the denominator is some nonzero \(\widehat\xi_i\in[a/\kappa_\epsilon,B]\), so
\[
\epsilon
\le
\epsilon\left(1+\frac{a}{\widehat\xi_i}\right)
\le
\epsilon(1+\kappa_\epsilon)
=1-\epsilon .
\]
The other cases give \(e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)=1/2\in[\epsilon,1-\epsilon]\). For branches \(b\in\{0,1\}\), define control outcome means to be zero and treated outcome means by
\[
\mu_{1,\boldsymbol\xi}^{(0)}(x_i)
:=
\frac{1-h(\widehat\xi_i)}2,\qquad
\mu_{1,\boldsymbol\xi}^{(1)}(x_i)
:=
\frac{1+h(\widehat\xi_i)}2,
\]
with \(\mu_{1,\boldsymbol\xi}^{(b)}(x_\star)=0\) and
\(\mu_{1,\boldsymbol\xi}^{(b)}(x)=0\) on unused cells. Since \(|h|\le1\), all outcome means lie in \([0,1]\). Let \(P_b(\boldsymbol\xi)\) be the law on \([d]\times\{0,1\}^2\) with cell mass
\[
p_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x):=\frac{\Omega_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)}{S(\boldsymbol\xi)},
\]
propensity \(e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)\), zero control outcome mean, and treated outcome mean \(\mu_{1,\boldsymbol\xi}^{(b)}(x)\). This is a probability law because each raw mass is nonnegative and
\[
\sum_{x=1}^d p_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)
=
\frac{\sum_x\Omega_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)}{S(\boldsymbol\xi)}=1 .
\]
For the reservoir cell, the raw mass is \(p_\star>0\) and the propensity is \(1/2\), which lies in \([\epsilon,1-\epsilon]\). For an unused cell, the raw mass is zero, so the occupied-cell overlap condition has no constraint; the totalized propensity there is \(1/2\). For a rare cell \(x_i\), if \(\widehat\xi_i=0\) then its raw mass is zero. If \(\widehat\xi_i\ne0\), then \(\widehat\xi_i\in[a/\kappa_\epsilon,B]\), and the calculation above gives
\[
\epsilon\le e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x_i)
=\epsilon\left(1+\frac a{\widehat\xi_i}\right)
\le 1-\epsilon .
\]
The control outcome means are zero, and the treated outcome means are either zero or of the form \((1\pm h(\widehat\xi_i))/2\), hence lie in \([0,1]\). Thus every occupied cell satisfies the overlap bounds and all outcome means are legal binary means, so \(P_b(\boldsymbol\xi)\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) by \cref{obj:def:model-class}. Define
\[
\Pi_b:=(P_b)_\#\mathsf G,\qquad b\in\{0,1\}.
\]
These are the two branch priors of the recipe, and the finite range of \(\widehat{\boldsymbol\xi}\) places the recipe in the finite-atom construction regime. The labeled and auxiliary intensities stored in the recipe are, by construction, \(u\) and \(v\). Since \(p_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)\) and \(e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)\) are independent of \(b\),
\[
(P\mapsto P_{XA})_\#\Pi_0
=
(P\mapsto P_{XA})_\#\Pi_1 .
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.common_marginal_uniform_intensity

\item The raw total mass has mean one:
\[
\int S(\boldsymbol\xi)\,d\mathsf G(\boldsymbol\xi)
=
p_\star+\sum_{i=1}^k\int \xi_i\,d\mathsf G(\boldsymbol\xi)
=
1-k\bar p+k\bar p
=
1 .
\]
For branch \(b\), the raw target is
\[
T_b(\boldsymbol\xi)
:=
\sum_{i=1}^k
\widehat\xi_i\,
\frac{1+(-1)^{1-b}h(\widehat\xi_i)}2 .
\]
It satisfies the raw version of \cref{obj:def:ate-functional}. Indeed, the control outcome means are zero, so
\[
\tau(P_b(\boldsymbol\xi))
=\sum_{x=1}^d
\frac{\Omega_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x)}{S(\boldsymbol\xi)}
\mu_{1,\boldsymbol\xi}^{(b)}(x).
\]
Multiplying by \(S(\boldsymbol\xi)\) cancels the normalization. The reservoir and unused cells contribute zero, and the rare-cell terms give
\[
S(\boldsymbol\xi)\,\tau(P_b(\boldsymbol\xi))
=
\sum_{i=1}^k
\widehat\xi_i\,
\frac{1+(-1)^{1-b}h(\widehat\xi_i)}2
=T_b(\boldsymbol\xi).
\]
Moreover,
\[
\int
\left[
p\,\frac{1+h(p)}2
-
p\,\frac{1-h(p)}2
\right]d\nu(p)
=
\int p h(p)\,d\nu(p).
\]
The atom of \(\nu\) at zero contributes zero. On the nonzero support, \(d\nu=(a/(p+a))\,d|\sigma|\) and \(d\sigma=h\,d|\sigma|\), so
\[
\int p h(p)\,d\nu(p)
=
a\int \frac{p}{p+a}\,d\sigma(p).
\]
Therefore the raw prior centers \(\theta_b:=\int T_b\,d\mathsf G\) obey
\[
\theta_1-\theta_0
=
k a\int \frac{p}{p+a}\,d\sigma(p).
\]
The alternation gap gives
\[
c_\epsilon\le \int \frac{p}{p+a}\,d\sigma(p),
\]
so the displayed difference is nonnegative and at least \(c_\epsilon k a\). Hence
\[
c_\epsilon k a\le |\theta_1-\theta_0|.
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.common_marginal_uniform_intensity

\item The support and mean bounds for \(\nu\) give
\[
\operatorname{Var}_{\mathsf G}(S)
=
\operatorname{Var}_{\mathsf G}\left(\sum_{i=1}^k\xi_i\right)
\le
\sum_{i=1}^k\mathbb E_\nu[p^2]
\le
kB\,\mathbb E_\nu[p]
\le
kBa
\le C_\epsilon kBa .
\]
For each branch define
\[
g_b(p):=
p\,\frac{1+(-1)^{1-b}h(p)}2 .
\]
Then \(0\le g_b(p)\le p\le B\) on the support of \(\nu\), and
\[
T_b(\boldsymbol\xi)=\sum_{i=1}^k g_b(\xi_i)
\quad\text{almost surely under }\mathsf G .
\]
Consequently,
\[
\operatorname{Var}_{\mathsf G}(T_b)
\le
\sum_{i=1}^k\mathbb E_\nu[g_b(p)^2]
\le
kB\,\mathbb E_\nu[g_b(p)]
\le
kB\,\mathbb E_\nu[p]
\le
kBa
\le
C_\epsilon kBa .
\]
Taking the maximum over \(b=0,1\) gives the asserted raw target-variance bound. The same bounded-support estimates also give the required square integrability of \(S\) and of both \(T_b\). % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.common_marginal_uniform_intensity

\item It remains to bound the prior-predictive distance in the raw Poisson experiment. For a single rare cell, let \(\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{rare}}\) be the finite product space consisting of the two labeled treated-outcome counts, the auxiliary treated count, and the labeled and auxiliary aggregate control counts, and let \(z_{\varnothing}\) be its all-zero element. For \(b\in\{0,1\}\) and \(p\in\mathbb R\), define the raw rare-cell kernel by
\[
\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,p}
:=
\begin{cases}
\delta_{z_{\varnothing}}, & p=0,\\
\mathsf S_{\alpha_{u,v}}\mathsf M_{b,p}, & p\ne0,
\end{cases}
\qquad
\alpha_{u,v}:=\frac{u}{u+v}\in[0,1],
\]
where \(\mathsf M_{b,p}\) is the branch-\(b\) marked-Poisson law associated with the certificate at latent mass \(p\), and \(\mathsf S_{\alpha_{u,v}}\) is the fixed splitting kernel that separates the marked treated counts into labeled and auxiliary pieces and carries along the corresponding control-count components. Let
\[
\mathsf Q_b:=\int \mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,p}\,d\nu(p),
\qquad
\widetilde{\mathsf Q}_b
:=\int \mathsf S_{\alpha_{u,v}}\mathsf M_{b,p}\,d\nu(p).
\]
For every measurable event \(E\subseteq\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{rare}}\) and every \(p\in\mathbb R\),
\[
\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{1,p}(E)-\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{0,p}(E)
=
(\mathsf S_{\alpha_{u,v}}\mathsf M_{1,p})(E)
-(\mathsf S_{\alpha_{u,v}}\mathsf M_{0,p})(E).
\]
Indeed, this is the definition when \(p\ne0\). When \(p=0\), the left side is zero because both raw kernels are \(\delta_{z_{\varnothing}}\). The right side is also zero: as shown above, \(0\) is not a certificate node, so \(h(0)=0\), and the two branch marked-Poisson laws agree at the zero fiber before the fixed splitting is applied. Integrating the displayed identity with respect to \(\nu\) gives, for every measurable \(E\),
\[
(\mathsf Q_1-\mathsf Q_0)(E)
=
(\widetilde{\mathsf Q}_1-\widetilde{\mathsf Q}_0)(E).
\]
Thus the signed finite measures \(\mathsf Q_1-\mathsf Q_0\) and \(\widetilde{\mathsf Q}_1-\widetilde{\mathsf Q}_0\) are equal, and their total variations are equal. Hence
\[
\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1)
=
\operatorname{TV}(\widetilde{\mathsf Q}_0,\widetilde{\mathsf Q}_1).
\]
Data processing under the splitting kernel and the one-coordinate marked-Poisson estimate give
\[
\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1)
\le
\bar C_\epsilon\,u\,a\,\rho_\epsilon^L .
\]
The certificate used here is precisely the finite-node certificate constructed above: its node set
is finite, its signed weights have total variation one, all nodes lie in
\([a/\kappa_\epsilon,B]\), and its moments through degree \(L\) vanish. Thus the normalized
finite-certificate and support hypotheses of the one-coordinate estimate are all discharged by the
displayed construction, rather than by an additional regularity convention.
Independence of the \(k\) rare coordinates yields
\[
\operatorname{TV}\!\left(\mathsf Q_0^{\otimes k},\mathsf Q_1^{\otimes k}\right)
\le
k\,\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1)
\le
\bar C_\epsilon\,u\,k\,a\,\rho_\epsilon^L .
\]
It remains to identify this product experiment with the raw Poisson experiment generated by the constructed laws. Let \(\mathsf R_b(\boldsymbol\xi)\) be the conditional raw Poisson law generated by \(P_b(\boldsymbol\xi)\) at raw scale \(S(\boldsymbol\xi)\). Let
\[
\mathcal Y_{\mathrm{cell}}
:=
\left\{y:\{0,1\}\to(\mathbb N\times\mathbb N)\times\mathbb N\right\}
\]
be the single-cell raw-count space; \(y(\alpha)=((y^{\mathrm{s}}_\alpha,y^{\mathrm{f}}_\alpha),y^{\mathrm{aux}}_\alpha)\) records labeled successes, labeled failures, and auxiliary counts in arm \(\alpha\). Let \(z_{\mathrm{cell},0}\in\mathcal Y_{\mathrm{cell}}\) be the all-zero cell observation, and let
\[
\mathfrak r:\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{rare}}\to\mathcal Y_{\mathrm{cell}}
\]
send the five rare-cell counts to the two arm-indexed cell observations: in the treated arm it records the treated success count, treated failure count, and auxiliary treated count, while in the control arm it records zero successes, the labeled control count, and the auxiliary control count. The common reservoir law is
\[
\mathsf Q_\star
:=
\bigotimes_{\alpha\in\{0,1\}}
\left
\{\operatorname{Poi}(0)\otimes
\operatorname{Poi}\left(\frac{u p_\star}{2}\right)\otimes
\operatorname{Poi}\left(\frac{v p_\star}{2}\right)\right\}.
\]
For fixed \(\boldsymbol\xi\), define rare-coordinate cell laws by
\[
\mathsf H_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,i}
:=
(\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\widehat\xi_i})_\#\mathfrak r,
\qquad i=1,\ldots,k,
\]
and define a zero law \(\mathsf H_0:=\delta_{z_{\mathrm{cell},0}}\). For \(\boldsymbol y=(y_1,\ldots,y_k)\in\mathcal Y_{\mathrm{cell}}^k\) and \(y_\star\in\mathcal Y_{\mathrm{cell}}\), define the deterministic assembly map by
\[
\bigl[\mathsf A(\boldsymbol y,y_\star)\bigr](x,\alpha)
:=
\begin{cases}
y_\star(\alpha), & x=x_\star,\\
y_i(\alpha), & x=x_i\text{ for the unique }i,\\
z_{\mathrm{cell},0}(\alpha), & x\notin\{x_\star,x_1,\ldots,x_k\}.
\end{cases}
\]
The middle line is unambiguous because the rare cells are distinct and none is the reservoir. By \cref{obj:lem:finite-product-padding-identity}, applied with the embedding \(i\mapsto x_i\), distinguished coordinate \(x_\star\), coordinate laws \(\mathsf H_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,i}\), reservoir law \(\mathsf Q_\star\), and filler \(z_{\mathrm{cell},0}\),
\[
\mathsf A_\#\left(\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^k
\mathsf H_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,i}\right)\otimes\mathsf Q_\star\right)
=
\bigotimes_{x=1}^d \mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x},
\]
where
\[
\mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x}
:=
\begin{cases}
\mathsf Q_\star, & x=x_\star,\\
\mathsf H_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,i}, & x=x_i\text{ for the unique }i,\\
\mathsf H_0, & x\notin\{x_\star,x_1,\ldots,x_k\}.
\end{cases}
\]
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.pi_prod_map_finitePadding
Equivalently, if \(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{rare}}\) denotes the map
\[
\mathsf A_{\mathrm{rare}}(\boldsymbol z,z_\star)
:=
\mathsf A\bigl((\mathfrak r(z_1),\ldots,\mathfrak r(z_k)),z_\star\bigr),
\]
then
\[
(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{rare}})_\#\left(\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^k
\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\widehat\xi_i}\right)\otimes\mathsf Q_\star\right)
=
\bigotimes_{x=1}^d \mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x},
\]
because pushforward by \(\mathfrak r\) in each rare coordinate turns \(\bigotimes_i\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\widehat\xi_i}\) into \(\bigotimes_i\mathsf H_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,i}\).
More explicitly, coordinatewise pushforward gives
\[
\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^k
\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\widehat\xi_i}\right)_\#
\bigl[(z_i)_i\mapsto(\mathfrak r(z_i))_i\bigr]
=
\bigotimes_{i=1}^k\mathsf H_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,i}.
\]
Taking the product with \(\mathsf Q_\star\), then composing this coordinate map with
\(\mathsf A\), gives \(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{rare}}\). Functoriality of pushforward therefore turns
the left side of the padding identity into the asserted assembled rare-coordinate law.
% lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.fiveProduct_map_rareObservationToCell

We now compare these coordinate laws with the conditional raw Poisson law cell by cell. At the reservoir, raw scale multiplication cancels the normalization:
\[
S(\boldsymbol\xi)\,p_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x_\star)=p_\star,
\qquad e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x_\star)=\frac12,
\qquad \mu^{(b)}_{1,\boldsymbol\xi}(x_\star)=\mu^{(b)}_{0,\boldsymbol\xi}(x_\star)=0.
\]
Thus each arm has raw treatment-covariate mass \(p_\star/2\), zero labeled-success rate, labeled-failure rate \(u p_\star/2\), and auxiliary rate \(v p_\star/2\). Therefore the reservoir coordinate law of \(\mathsf R_b(\boldsymbol\xi)\) is
\[
\mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x_\star}=\mathsf Q_\star .
\]
At a rare cell \(x_i\), put \(p=\widehat\xi_i\). If \(p=0\), then
\[
S(\boldsymbol\xi)\,p_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x_i)=0,
\]
so all labeled and auxiliary Poisson rates at this cell are zero. The conditional raw Poisson coordinate law is \(\delta_{z_{\mathrm{cell},0}}\). The raw rare-cell kernel is also \(\delta_{z_{\varnothing}}\), and \(\mathfrak r(z_{\varnothing})=z_{\mathrm{cell},0}\), hence
\[
(\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,0})_\#\mathfrak r
=\delta_{z_{\mathrm{cell},0}}
=\mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x_i} .
\]
If \(p>0\), define
\[
s_{\mathrm{tr}}(p):=\epsilon(p+a),
\qquad
s_{\mathrm{ct}}(p):=p-s_{\mathrm{tr}}(p),
\qquad
\eta_1:=1,
\quad
\eta_0:=-1.
\]
The construction gives
\[
S(\boldsymbol\xi)\,p_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x_i)=p,
\qquad
e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x_i)=\frac{s_{\mathrm{tr}}(p)}{p},
\qquad
\mu^{(b)}_{1,\boldsymbol\xi}(x_i)=\frac{1+\eta_b h(p)}2,
\qquad
\mu^{(b)}_{0,\boldsymbol\xi}(x_i)=0.
\]
Consequently the raw treatment-covariate masses in the treated and control arms are
\[
p\,e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x_i)=s_{\mathrm{tr}}(p),
\qquad
p\{1-e_{\boldsymbol\xi}(x_i)\}=s_{\mathrm{ct}}(p).
\]
The conditional raw Poisson coordinate law at \(x_i\) is therefore the product law on \(\mathcal Y_{\mathrm{cell}}\) with treated-arm rates
\[
\frac{u s_{\mathrm{tr}}(p)(1+\eta_b h(p))}{2},
\qquad
\frac{u s_{\mathrm{tr}}(p)(1-\eta_b h(p))}{2},
\qquad
v s_{\mathrm{tr}}(p)
\]
for labeled successes, labeled failures, and auxiliary counts, and with control-arm rates
\[
0,
\qquad
u s_{\mathrm{ct}}(p),
\qquad
v s_{\mathrm{ct}}(p)
\]
for labeled successes, labeled failures, and auxiliary counts. The split marked rare-cell law \(\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,p}\) is the fivefold product law whose coordinates have exactly the nonzero rates just displayed: treated success, treated failure, auxiliary treated, labeled control, and auxiliary control. Applying \(\mathfrak r\) places the first three counts in the treated arm and the last two in the control arm, with zero control successes. Product Poisson laws are determined by their coordinate rates, so
\[
(\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,p})_\#\mathfrak r
=
\mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x_i}
\qquad(p=\widehat\xi_i>0).
\]
Combining the two rare-cell cases gives
\[
(\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\widehat\xi_i})_\#\mathfrak r
=
\mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x_i}
\qquad(1\le i\le k).
\]
At an unused cell the raw mass is zero, so all rates are zero and its coordinate law is
\[
\mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x}=\mathsf H_0 .
\]
Thus the coordinate laws of the conditional raw Poisson experiment are precisely the padded product coordinate laws: reservoir law at \(x_\star\), the pushed-forward rare laws at the rare cells, and the zero law elsewhere. Since conditional raw Poisson counts are independent across cells,
\[
\mathsf R_b(\boldsymbol\xi)
=
\bigotimes_{x=1}^d \mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x}.
\]
The preceding coordinate identification, together with the padding identity 
\[
\mathsf A_\#\left(\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^k
\mathsf H_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,i}\right)\otimes\mathsf Q_\star\right)
=
\bigotimes_{x=1}^d \mathsf J_{b,\boldsymbol\xi,x},
\]
which follows from \cref{obj:lem:finite-product-padding-identity}, yields the pointwise fiber identity
\[
\mathsf R_b(\boldsymbol\xi)
=
(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{rare}})_\#\left(\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^k
\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\widehat\xi_i}\right)\otimes\mathsf Q_\star\right).
\]
This equality uses more than the abstract padding identity: the reservoir calculation fixes the
distinguished coordinate, the two rare-cell cases identify every pushed-forward rare law, and the
unused-cell calculation supplies the Dirac fillers. Independence of the raw Poisson counts then
identifies the conditional experiment with the resulting finite product. Hence every coordinate
law on the two sides is the same before finite-product uniqueness is invoked.
The identity holds for every \(\boldsymbol\xi\). Integrating it against \(\mathsf G\) and using \(\widehat{\boldsymbol\xi}=\boldsymbol\xi\) almost surely gives
\[
\int \mathsf R_b(\boldsymbol\xi)\,d\mathsf G(\boldsymbol\xi)
=
(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{rare}})_\#\left(
\left[\int \bigotimes_{i=1}^k
\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\xi_i}\,d\nu^{\otimes k}(\boldsymbol\xi)\right]
\otimes\mathsf Q_\star\right).
\]
For cylinder events \(E_1\times\cdots\times E_k\subseteq\mathcal Z_{\mathrm{rare}}^k\), independence under \(\nu^{\otimes k}\) gives
\[
\int \prod_{i=1}^k
\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\xi_i}(E_i)\,d\nu^{\otimes k}(\boldsymbol\xi)
=
\prod_{i=1}^k \int
\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,p}(E_i)\,d\nu(p)
=
\prod_{i=1}^k \mathsf Q_b(E_i),
\]
because each map \(p\mapsto\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,p}(E_i)\) is measurable and takes values in
\([0,1]\). Finite Fubini therefore factors the integral of their product under
\(\nu^{\otimes k}\) into the product of the coordinate integrals. Both sides define probability
measures on the finite product space, and measurable rectangles form a generating \(\pi\)-system;
the \(\pi\)-\(\lambda\) uniqueness theorem extends the displayed equality from rectangles to the
whole product \(\sigma\)-algebra. Thus
\[
\int \bigotimes_{i=1}^k
\mathsf Q^{\mathrm{raw}}_{b,\xi_i}\,d\nu^{\otimes k}(\boldsymbol\xi)
=
\mathsf Q_b^{\otimes k}.
\]
Consequently the branch-
\(b\) raw prior-predictive law is
\[
\mathsf R_b
:=
\int \mathsf R_b(\boldsymbol\xi)\,d\mathsf G(\boldsymbol\xi)
=
(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{rare}})_\#\left(\mathsf Q_b^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathsf Q_\star\right).
\]
Appending the common reservoir law and applying the deterministic assembly map cannot increase total variation, so
\[
\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf R_0,\mathsf R_1)
\le
\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0^{\otimes k},\mathsf Q_1^{\otimes k})
\le
\bar C_\epsilon\,u\,k\,a\,\rho_\epsilon^L
\le
C_\epsilon\,u\,k\,a\,\rho_\epsilon^L .
\]
The constructed recipe with priors \(\Pi_0,\Pi_1\) therefore satisfies all displayed calibration, support, common-table, mean, separation, variance, and mixture-distance conclusions. % lean: CausalSmith.Stat.SemisupervisedDiscreteAteAnnotationFrontier.common_marginal_uniform_intensity
\end{enumerate}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemmav}{lem:common-marginal-canonical-certificate-family}[Canonical certificate family]
There exist real constants \(C_{\mathrm{fixed}}\) and \(c_{\mathrm{risk}}\) with
\[
0<C_{\mathrm{fixed}},
\qquad
0<c_{\mathrm{risk}},
\]
such that, for every overlap level \(\epsilon\) with \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), there are a common-marginal calibration in the sense of \cref{obj:def:common-marginal-prior-handle} and real constants
\[
C_\epsilon,\rho_\epsilon,c_{\mathrm{small}},c_{\mathrm{floor}}
\]
with the following properties.  Write \(b_0\) for the calibration bandwidth constant, \(b_\epsilon\) for the uniform bandwidth constant, \(\gamma_\epsilon\) for the shift constant, \(c_\epsilon\) for the dual-gap constant, and \(C_d\) for the dimension constant supplied by the calibration.  These calibration constants are positive, and
\[
c_\epsilon\le \frac14 .
\]
The auxiliary constants satisfy
\[
0<C_\epsilon,
\qquad
\rho_\epsilon\in(0,1),
\qquad
0<c_{\mathrm{small}},
\qquad
0<c_{\mathrm{floor}},
\qquad
c_{\mathrm{floor}}\le c_{\mathrm{risk}} .
\]
Moreover:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Fixed-sample transfer.)}
For every \(n,m,d\) with \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge2\), and every common-marginal prior handle \(H\) at \((n,m,d)\) in the sense of \cref{obj:def:common-marginal-prior-handle}, let \(L,B,a,k\) denote the degree, bandwidth, shift, and rare-cell count components of \(H\), and let \(\theta_0(H)\) and \(\theta_1(H)\) denote the two raw prior centers of its recipe.  If the recipe of \(H\) carries the exact common-marginal Poisson-prior construction at bandwidth constant \(b_0\), information size \(n+m\), labeled intensity
\[
u=C_{\mathrm{fixed}}n,
\]
auxiliary intensity
\[
v=C_{\mathrm{fixed}}(n+m),
\]
and constants \((C_\epsilon,\rho_\epsilon)\), including the corresponding finite-support prior data, exact common treatment-covariate marginal table, moment-matching identities through degree \(L\), raw-center separation data, and Poisson likelihood-ratio bound at rate \(C_\epsilon u k a\rho_\epsilon^L\), if the two raw prior-predictive Poisson experiment laws supplied by that recipe have total variation distance at most \(1/8\), and if
\[
kBa
\le
c_{\mathrm{small}}
\left|\theta_1(H)-\theta_0(H)\right|^2,
\]
then
\[
c_{\mathrm{risk}}
\left|\theta_1(H)-\theta_0(H)\right|^2
\le
R_\epsilon(n,m,d),
\]
where \(R_\epsilon(n,m,d)\) is the minimax risk from \cref{obj:def:minimax-risk}.

\item \textbf{(Canonical handles.)}
For every \(n,m,d\) with \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge2\), there exists a common-marginal prior handle \(H\) at \((n,m,d)\).  Let \(L,B,a,k\) denote its degree, bandwidth, shift, and rare-cell count components, set
\[
\Delta_H=c_\epsilon ka,
\qquad
D(n,m,d)=\min\left\{1,\frac{d^2}{(n+m)^2\log(en)^2}\right\},
\]
and let \(\theta_0(H)\) and \(\theta_1(H)\) denote the two raw prior centers of its recipe.  The same constants satisfy
\[
L\ge2,
\qquad
2C_{\mathrm{fixed}}b_0\le b_\epsilon,
\]
\[
\bigl(C_{\mathrm{fixed}}n+C_{\mathrm{fixed}}(n+m)\bigr)B
\le
b_\epsilon L,
\]
\[
\mathcal L(P_{XA}\mid \Pi_0(H))
=
\mathcal L(P_{XA}\mid \Pi_1(H)),
\qquad
\frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}}{n}\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).
\]
On the finite-atom branch, the handle also satisfies
\[
ka\le c_\epsilon,
\]
and its recipe carries the exact common-marginal Poisson-prior construction at bandwidth constant \(b_0\), information size \(n+m\), labeled intensity \(u=C_{\mathrm{fixed}}n\), auxiliary intensity \(v=C_{\mathrm{fixed}}(n+m)\), and constants \((C_\epsilon,\rho_\epsilon)\), including the finite-support prior data, exact common treatment-covariate marginal table, moment-matching identities through degree \(L\), raw-center separation data, and Poisson likelihood-ratio bound at rate \(C_\epsilon u k a\rho_\epsilon^L\).  The recipe bounds hold at these intensities: both priors are supported on \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\) from \cref{obj:def:model-class}, they induce the same random \(P_{XA}\) law, their raw-center separation is at least \(c_\epsilon ka\), the raw total mass of the generator has mean one, the raw total mass and raw targets are square-integrable with generator variances at most \(C_\epsilon kBa\), and the raw mixture total variation is at most \(C_\epsilon u k a\rho_\epsilon^L\).  Moreover, the two raw prior-predictive Poisson experiment laws supplied by the recipe have total variation distance at most \(1/8\), and
\[
\Delta_H\le
\left|\theta_1(H)-\theta_0(H)\right|,
\qquad
kBa\le c_{\mathrm{small}}\Delta_H^2,
\]
\[
c_{\mathrm{floor}}\Delta_H^2\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d),
\qquad
C_d\,D(n,m,d)\le \Delta_H^2.
\]
On the parametric branch,
\[
C_d\,D(n,m,d)\le \frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}}{n}.
\]
\end{itemize}
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-canonical-certificate-family}]
Set
\[
C_{\mathrm{fixed}}=65536,
\qquad
c_{\mathrm{risk}}=\frac1{65536}.
\]
These constants are positive.  Fix \(\epsilon\in(0,1/2)\).  By \cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-uniform-intensity}, choose a common-marginal calibration and constants \(C_\epsilon,\rho_\epsilon\) with
\[
C_\epsilon>0,\qquad \rho_\epsilon\in(0,1),
\]
together with the uniform construction and closure bounds supplied there.  Define
\[
c_{\mathrm{small}}=\frac{1}{65536\,C_\epsilon},
\qquad
c_{\mathrm{floor}}=\frac1{65536}.
\]
Then
\[
c_{\mathrm{small}}>0,\qquad c_{\mathrm{floor}}>0,\qquad
c_{\mathrm{floor}}\le c_{\mathrm{risk}},
\]
and \cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-uniform-intensity} supplies positive
\(b_0,b_\epsilon,\gamma_\epsilon,q_\epsilon,c_\epsilon,C_d\), with
\(c_\epsilon\le 1/4\).  We retain that statement's notation: \(q_\epsilon\) is the
rare-count coefficient, \(c_\epsilon\) is the dual-gap constant, and \(C_d\) is
the dimension constant. % lean: common_marginal_canonical_certificate_family

First consider the fixed-sample transfer assertion.  Let \(n,m,d,H\) satisfy the hypotheses in that assertion, and write
\[
\Delta
=
\left|\theta_1(H)-\theta_0(H)\right|.
\]
By the hypotheses of the fixed-sample assertion, the recipe carried by \(H\) comes with an exact Poisson-prior witness at
\[
u=C_{\mathrm{fixed}}n,
\qquad
v=C_{\mathrm{fixed}}(n+m),
\]
with constants \(C_\epsilon,\rho_\epsilon\).  The same hypotheses give total variation at most \(1/8\) for its prior-predictive laws, and the assumed variance absorption is
\[
kBa\le c_{\mathrm{small}}\Delta^2
=
\frac{1}{65536\,C_\epsilon}\Delta^2 .
\]
Applying the fixed-sample recipe transfer bound \cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-recipe-transfer} at these intensities gives
\[
\frac1{65536}\Delta^2
\le
R_\epsilon(n,m,d).
\]
Since \(c_{\mathrm{risk}}=1/65536\), this is exactly the fixed-sample transfer conclusion. % lean: fixed_sample_common_marginal_transfer_rule

It remains to construct the canonical handle for arbitrary \(n,m,d\) with \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge2\).  Write
\[
N=n+m,\qquad \ell_n=\log(en),
\qquad
D(n,m,d)=\min\left\{1,\frac{d^2}{N^2\ell_n^2}\right\}.
\]
Let \(r_\epsilon\) denote the calibration regime constant, \(D_\epsilon\) the calibration degree constant, and \(q_\epsilon\) the calibration rare-count constant.  The proof splits according to whether the parametric regime holds:
\[
D(n,m,d)\le \frac{r_\epsilon}{n}.
\]
In both regimes set
\[
L=\left\lceil D_\epsilon\ell_n\right\rceil,\qquad
B=\frac{b_0L}{N},\qquad
a=\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{L^2},
\]
and
\[
k=\min\left\{d-1,\left\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\right\rfloor\right\}.
\]
The calibration closure in \cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-uniform-intensity} gives
\[
L\ge2
\]
and
\[
2C_{\mathrm{fixed}}b_0=131072\,b_0\le b_\epsilon .
\]
Moreover, since \(n\le N\),
\[
(C_{\mathrm{fixed}}n+C_{\mathrm{fixed}}N)B
\le
(131072\,N)\frac{b_0L}{N}
=
131072\,b_0L
\le b_\epsilon L.
\]
Thus the degree and bandwidth conclusions hold for either branch. % lean: common_marginal_canonical_certificate_family

Suppose first that
\[
D(n,m,d)\le \frac{r_\epsilon}{n}.
\]
The local testing gap used by the parametric handle is admissible because
\[
\frac{c_\epsilon}{\sqrt n}\le c_\epsilon\le \frac14,
\]
where \(\sqrt n\ge1\).  Let \(H\) be the parametric common-marginal prior handle with the above values of \(L,B,a,k\).  Its branch condition makes the finite-atom predicate false in this case.  Consequently all conclusions whose hypotheses include the finite-atom predicate are vacuous.  The common treatment-covariate marginal identity is the handle identity
\[
\mathcal L(P_{XA}\mid \Pi_0(H))
=
\mathcal L(P_{XA}\mid \Pi_1(H)).
\]
The risk floor follows from the parametric two-point floor:
\[
\frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}}{n}
=
\frac{1/65536}{n}
\le
\frac{1/100}{n}
\le
R_\epsilon(n,m,d).
\]
Finally, the calibration closure gives
\[
C_d\,r_\epsilon\le \frac1{65536}=c_{\mathrm{floor}},
\]
and hence, using the parametric-regime inequality,
\[
C_d\,D(n,m,d)
\le
C_d\,\frac{r_\epsilon}{n}
\le
\frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}}{n}.
\]
This proves all required canonical-handle properties on the parametric branch. % lean: common_marginal_canonical_certificate_family

Now suppose that the parametric regime fails:
\[
D(n,m,d)>\frac{r_\epsilon}{n}.
\]
With \(L,B,a,k\) as above, the quantities \(B\) and \(a\) are positive.  From
\[
k=\min\left\{d-1,\left\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\right\rfloor\right\}
\]
and the elementary inequality \(\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\rfloor\le q_\epsilon NL\), valid since \(q_\epsilon NL\ge0\), we have
\[
k\le q_\epsilon NL.
\]
Therefore
\[
ka
\le
q_\epsilon NL\cdot
\frac{\gamma_\epsilon b_0}{NL}
=
q_\epsilon\gamma_\epsilon b_0
\le c_\epsilon,
\]
where the last inequality is the rare-scale calibration inequality.  Hence the rare shift is small. % lean: common_marginal_canonical_certificate_family

Apply the finite-atom part of \cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-uniform-intensity} with
\[
u=65536\,n,\qquad v=65536\,N.
\]
The preceding bandwidth bound, positivity of \(B\), the identity \(a=\gamma_\epsilon B/L^2\), the rare-shift bound \(ka\le c_\epsilon\), and \(k+1\le d\) produce a recipe \(R\) at these intensities.  The bounds supplied for this recipe include the finite-atom construction flag, support in \(\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\), equality of the induced treatment-covariate marginal table, the raw total-mass mean-one identity, the raw-center separation, the raw total-mass and target variance bounds, and the raw mixture total-variation bound
\[
\operatorname{TV}
\bigl(\mathsf P_{0,R}^{\mathrm{Pois}},\mathsf P_{1,R}^{\mathrm{Pois}}\bigr)
\le
C_\epsilon u k a \rho_\epsilon^L.
\]
Together with \(n\ge1\), \(L\ge2\), \(d\ge2\), the nonnegativity of \(u,v\), the positivity of \(u+v\), the bandwidth and shift identities, and the rare-count fit \(k+1\le d\), these same bounds form the exact Poisson-prior witness used below.  Let \(H\) be the corresponding handle.  Its branch condition identifies this recipe with the finite-atom branch.  The common marginal identity again gives
\[
\mathcal L(P_{XA}\mid \Pi_0(H))
=
\mathcal L(P_{XA}\mid \Pi_1(H)).
\]
The recipe bounds also give
\[
\left|\theta_1(H)-\theta_0(H)\right|
\ge
c_\epsilon ka
=
\Delta_H,
\]
the raw total mass has mean one, the raw total mass and raw targets are square-integrable with variances at most \(C_\epsilon kBa\), and the raw mixture total variation is bounded by
\[
C_\epsilon u k a\rho_\epsilon^L.
\]
The calibration decay inequality and the bound \(ka\le c_\epsilon\) imply
\[
\operatorname{TV}
\bigl(\mathsf P_{0,H}^{\mathrm{Pois}},\mathsf P_{1,H}^{\mathrm{Pois}}\bigr)
\le
C_\epsilon(65536\,n)ka\rho_\epsilon^L
\le
C_\epsilon(65536\,n)c_\epsilon\rho_\epsilon^L
\le
\frac18 .
\]
Thus the prior-predictive total variation condition required by the transfer rule holds. % lean: common_marginal_canonical_certificate_family

It remains to record the two absorptions used by the handle.  The counting estimate used in the finite-atom branch is
\[
\frac{L^2}{c_{\mathrm{small}}c_\epsilon^2\gamma_\epsilon}\le k .
\]
Indeed, write \(N=n+m\) and \(\ell_n=\log(en)\).  Since the branch condition is
\[
D(n,m,d)>\frac{r_\epsilon}{n},
\]
the minimum in \(D(n,m,d)\) forces
\[
\frac{r_\epsilon}{n}
<
\frac{d^2}{N^2\ell_n^2}.
\]
Put
\[
K=c_{\mathrm{small}}c_\epsilon^2\gamma_\epsilon>0.
\]
The calibration inequality
\[
\left(
\frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)^2}{K}
\right)^2
\le r_\epsilon
\]
implies
\[
\frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)^2}{K}\le\sqrt{r_\epsilon}.
\]
Meanwhile, the failed-regime inequality above gives
\[
\frac{\sqrt{r_\epsilon}\,N\ell_n}{\sqrt n}<d.
\]
Using the ceiling bound \(L\le(D_\epsilon+1)\ell_n\) and
\[
\ell_n\sqrt n\le 2n\le 2N,
\]
we obtain the complete cross-multiplication
\[
\begin{aligned}
\frac{2L^2}{K}
&\le
\frac{2(D_\epsilon+1)^2\ell_n^2}{K} \\
&=
\left(\frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)^2}{K}\right)
\frac{N\ell_n}{\sqrt n}
\frac{\ell_n\sqrt n}{2N} \\
&\le
\frac{\sqrt{r_\epsilon}\,N\ell_n}{\sqrt n}
<d.
\end{aligned}
\]
Equivalently, the proof has compared the nonnegative quantities through the strict square inequality
\[
\left(\frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)^2}{K}N\ell_n\right)^2
<
(d\sqrt n)^2,
\]
and then divided by the positive factor \(\sqrt n\).  Weakening the strict bound gives
\[
\frac{2L^2}{K}
\le d.
\]
Now split according to the actual comparison defining the minimum in \(k\).  If
\[
d-1\le \left\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\right\rfloor,
\]
then
\[
k=d-1.
\]
Since \(d\ge2\),
\[
\frac d2\le d-1,
\]
and hence
\[
\frac{L^2}{K}
\le \frac d2\le d-1=k .
\]
If instead
\[
\left\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\right\rfloor<d-1,
\]
then
\[
k=\left\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\right\rfloor .
\]
The calibration gives
\[
\frac{4(D_\epsilon+1)}{K}
\le q_\epsilon,
\]
and, since \(q_\epsilon NL\ge1\), the elementary floor bound \(\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\rfloor\ge q_\epsilon NL/2\) gives
\[
k=\bigl\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\bigr\rfloor
\ge \frac{q_\epsilon NL}{2}
\ge
\frac{2(D_\epsilon+1)NL}{K}.
\]
Using \(L\le(D_\epsilon+1)\ell_n\) and \(\ell_n\le N\), this is at least
\[
\frac{L^2}{K}.
\]
Thus the displayed counting estimate holds in both subcases.  Recall here that \(c_\epsilon\) denotes the calibration dual-gap constant; the rare-count coefficient is \(q_\epsilon\).  Using \(a=\gamma_\epsilon B/L^2\), the counting estimate yields
\[
kBa
=
\left(\frac{k a^2}{\gamma_\epsilon}\right)L^2
\le
\frac{k a^2}{\gamma_\epsilon}
\bigl(kc_{\mathrm{small}}c_\epsilon^2\gamma_\epsilon\bigr)
=
c_{\mathrm{small}}(c_\epsilon ka)^2
=
c_{\mathrm{small}}\Delta_H^2.
\]
The recipe separation bound gives
\[
\Delta_H=c_\epsilon ka
\le
\left|\theta_1(H)-\theta_0(H)\right|.
\]
Consequently
\[
kBa
\le
c_{\mathrm{small}}
\left|\theta_1(H)-\theta_0(H)\right|^2 .
\]
The fixed-sample transfer already proved above therefore gives
\[
c_{\mathrm{floor}}
\left|\theta_1(H)-\theta_0(H)\right|^2
\le
R_\epsilon(n,m,d),
\]
and hence
\[
c_{\mathrm{floor}}\Delta_H^2\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).
\]
As before, the parametric floor gives
\[
\frac{c_{\mathrm{floor}}}{n}\le R_\epsilon(n,m,d).
\]
Finally we verify the dimension absorption for the above \(L,k,a\).  Since
\[
a=\frac{\gamma_\epsilon B}{L^2}
=\frac{\gamma_\epsilon b_0}{NL},
\]
it is enough to compare \(C_dD(n,m,d)\) with
\[
\left(c_\epsilon k\frac{\gamma_\epsilon b_0}{NL}\right)^2.
\]
Split according to which term realizes
\[
k=\min\left\{d-1,\left\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\right\rfloor\right\}.
\]
On the cap-active subcase, \(k=d-1\).  Because \(d\ge2\),
\[
\frac d2\le d-1=k.
\]
The definition of \(D(n,m,d)\) and the dimension calibration give
\[
\begin{aligned}
C_dD(n,m,d)
&\le C_d\frac{d^2}{N^2\ell_n^2} \\
&\le
\left(\frac{c_\epsilon\gamma_\epsilon b_0}{2(D_\epsilon+1)}\right)^2
\frac{d^2}{N^2\ell_n^2}.
\end{aligned}
\]
All factors here are nonnegative.  The two bounds
\(d\le2k\) and \(L\le(D_\epsilon+1)\ell_n\) therefore give, after
cross-multiplication by the positive denominators,
\[
0\le
\frac{c_\epsilon\gamma_\epsilon b_0d}
     {2(D_\epsilon+1)N\ell_n}
\le
c_\epsilon k\frac{\gamma_\epsilon b_0}{NL}
=c_\epsilon ka.
\]
Squaring this nonnegative comparison proves
\[
C_dD(n,m,d)\le(c_\epsilon ka)^2
\]
on the cap-active subcase.
On the floor-active subcase, \(k=\lfloor q_\epsilon NL\rfloor\).  The calibration includes the rare-count identity
\[
q_\epsilon=\frac{c_\epsilon}{2\gamma_\epsilon b_0}.
\]
The calibration has \(q_\epsilon\ge4\), while \(N\ge1\) and \(L\ge2\), so
\(q_\epsilon NL\ge1\).  The elementary floor bound therefore gives
\[
k\ge \frac{q_\epsilon NL}{2}.
\]
Thus, with every factor nonnegative,
\[
ka
\ge
\frac{q_\epsilon NL}{2}\cdot\frac{\gamma_\epsilon b_0}{NL}
=\frac{c_\epsilon}{4}.
\]
Since \(D(n,m,d)\le1\), the calibration gives
\(C_d\le c_\epsilon^4/16\), and squaring preserves the preceding comparison,
\[
C_dD(n,m,d)
\le C_d
\le \frac{c_\epsilon^4}{16}
=\left(c_\epsilon\frac{c_\epsilon}{4}\right)^2
\le (c_\epsilon ka)^2.
\]
Thus in both subcases
\[
C_d\,D(n,m,d)\le (c_\epsilon ka)^2=\Delta_H^2.
\] % lean: commonMarginal_dimension_absorption
This proves every required canonical-handle property on the finite-atom branch, and completes the construction for all \(n,m,d\). % lean: common_marginal_canonical_certificate_family
\end{proof}

The constants in \cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-uniform-intensity} coordinate the degree, bandwidth, rare-cell count, and mixture decay. The shared-law conclusion for the random treatment--covariate tables is the structural feature that makes the prior compatible with auxiliary annotation data, and the final total-variation display supplies the testing bound used by \cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-recipe-transfer,obj:lem:fixed-sample-common-marginal-transfer}. \Cref{obj:lem:common-marginal-canonical-certificate-family} then assembles the transfer constants, the canonical handle, the parametric label floor, and the finite-atom branch into the certificate invoked by \cref{obj:thm:common-marginal-converse}.

\paragraph{One-coordinate marked-Poisson input.}
The load-bearing analytic estimate is a one-coordinate marked-Poisson total-variation bound for normalized signed certificates. For a normalized signed certificate supported on \([a/\kappa_\epsilon,B]\), with moments through degree \(L\) equal to zero and \((u+v)B\le b_\epsilon L\), its two marked-Poisson predictive laws satisfy
\[
\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf Q_0,\mathsf Q_1)
\le C_\epsilon u a\rho_\epsilon^L,
\qquad 0<\rho_\epsilon<1.
\]
Expanding each marked-Poisson likelihood in the latent mass gives the intuition behind the bound: moment matching cancels the polynomial terms through degree \(L\), and the bandwidth condition leaves a geometrically controlled exponential-series tail. Tensorization over rare cells contributes the factor \(k\), while the common reservoir and deterministic splitting and assembly maps preserve or contract total variation.


\section{Known-marginal experiment and verification note}\label{sec:appendix-known}

The benchmark in \cref{obj:def:known-marginal-risk,obj:thm:known-marginal-boundary,obj:thm:known-marginal-limit} uses the same labeled experiment as the annotation problem, but supplies the treatment--covariate marginal as exact side information. This appendix records the corresponding experiment explicitly so that the comparison with the auxiliary-sample experiment in \cref{obj:def:annotation-experiment} has a fixed statistical object.

\begin{definitionv}{def:known-marginal-experiment}[Known-marginal experiment \(\mathcal E^{\mathrm{known}}_{n,d,\epsilon}\)]
For \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge2\), the known-marginal experiment is
\[
\mathcal E^{\mathrm{known}}_{n,d,\epsilon}
:=
\{(P^{\otimes n},P_{XA}):P\in\mathcal M_{d,\epsilon}\}.
\]
The first coordinate generates \(\mathcal L_n\), and the second coordinate is the exact \(2d\)-cell table observed as deterministic side information.
\end{definitionv}

The deterministic table \leanref{sym:P_{XA}}{\ensuremath{P_{XA}}} contains the full joint distribution of treatment and covariates under the same observed-data law \(P\) used for the labeled sample. Thus the benchmark isolates the contribution of outcome labels after the treatment--covariate frequencies are known exactly. This is the population analogue of having unlimited annotation data for \((X,A)\), and it is the object compared with finite-\(m\) annotation in \cref{obj:thm:known-marginal-limit}.

The risk \leanref{sym:R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d)}{\ensuremath{R_\epsilon^{\mathrm{known}}(n,d)}} in \cref{obj:def:known-marginal-risk} takes the infimum over \leanref{sym:\mathcal T^{\mathrm{known}}_{n,d}}{\ensuremath{\mathcal T^{\mathrm{known}}_{n,d}}}, the estimators measurable with respect to the labeled sample and this exact table. The boundary result in \cref{obj:thm:known-marginal-boundary} therefore describes the benchmark rate for the known-marginal problem, while \cref{obj:thm:known-marginal-limit} identifies the annotation regimes in which the finite auxiliary sample attains the same limiting benchmark. The construction is aligned with the finite-alphabet causal model in \cref{obj:def:model-class} and the average treatment effect functional in \cref{obj:def:ate-functional}; the overlap and sampling conditions remain those stated in \cref{obj:ass:overlap,obj:ass:labeled-iid}.

The next statements record the finite side-information comparison underlying that benchmark. The finite-cover statement is the compactness input that turns exact side information into finitely many stable decision rules; the causal specialization then yields convergence of empirical side samples to the exact-side benchmark; and the measurable comparison formalizes exact side information as at least as informative as an empirical side sample.

\begin{lemmav}{lem:finite-side-cover-approximate-decision}[Finite side cover]
Let \(\Theta\) be a nonempty compact topological space, let \(\mathsf Z\) be a finite set, let \(\mathsf C\) be a finite nonempty set, and let \(l\le u\). Suppose the following conditions hold.
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Laws.)} For each \(\theta\in\Theta\), \(\Lambda_\theta\) is a probability vector on \(\mathsf Z\) and \(\Xi_\theta\) is a probability vector on \(\mathsf C\).
\item \textbf{(Target.)} For each \(\theta\in\Theta\), \(\upsilon_\theta\in[l,u]\).
\item \textbf{(Continuity.)} For every \(z\in\mathsf Z\) and \(c\in\mathsf C\), the maps \(\theta\mapsto\Lambda_\theta(z)\), \(\theta\mapsto\Xi_\theta(c)\), and \(\theta\mapsto\upsilon_\theta\) are continuous.
\end{itemize}
For probability vectors \(\nu,\nu'\) on \(\mathsf C\), set
\[
D_\infty(\nu,\nu')=\max_{c\in\mathsf C}|\nu(c)-\nu'(c)|.
\]
Define
\[
\mathfrak R_\star
=
\inf_{\psi}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}
\sum_{z\in\mathsf Z}\Lambda_\theta(z)
\bigl(\psi(z,\Xi_\theta)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2,
\]
where \(\psi\) ranges over all maps
\[
\psi:\mathsf Z\times\mathsf{Prob}(\mathsf C)\to[l,u].
\]
Then for every \(\delta>0\), there exist an integer \(k\ge1\), centers
\(\theta^\circ_1,\ldots,\theta^\circ_k\in\Theta\), decisions
\(\beta_1,\ldots,\beta_k:\mathsf Z\to[l,u]\), radii
\(\operatorname{rad}_1,\ldots,\operatorname{rad}_k\), and a margin \(\eta>0\) such that
\[
\eta<\operatorname{rad}_j
\]
for every \(j\), every \(\theta\in\Theta\) admits some \(j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\) with
\[
D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta^\circ_j})<\operatorname{rad}_j-\eta,
\]
and, for every \(j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\) and every \(\theta\in\Theta\),
\[
D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta^\circ_j})<\operatorname{rad}_j
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\sum_{z\in\mathsf Z}\Lambda_\theta(z)
\bigl(\beta_j(z)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2
\le
\mathfrak R_\star+\delta.
\]
\end{lemmav}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{obj:lem:finite-side-cover-approximate-decision}]
\begin{enumerate}
\item For a decision \(b:\mathsf Z\to[l,u]\), write
\[
R_\theta(b)
=
\sum_{z\in\mathsf Z}\Lambda_\theta(z)\bigl(b(z)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2 .
\]
Since \(b(z),\upsilon_\theta\in[l,u]\), each squared term is at most \((u-l)^2\), and since \(\Lambda_\theta\) is a probability vector,
\[
0\le R_\theta(b)\le (u-l)^2
\qquad\text{for every }\theta\in\Theta .
\]
In particular the constant decision \(b_0(z)=l\) is admissible. % lean: finiteSquaredRisk_bounds

\item Fix \(\theta_0\in\Theta\). Let
\[
\mathcal F(\theta_0)
=
\{\theta\in\Theta:\Xi_\theta=\Xi_{\theta_0}\}
\]
and let
\[
V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)
=
\inf_{\beta:\mathsf Z\to[l,u]}\ \sup_{\theta\in\mathcal F(\theta_0)} R_\theta(\beta).
\]
The fiber is nonempty because it contains \(\theta_0\). By the previous bound, the worst-case risk of the constant decision \(b_0\) over this fiber is at most \((u-l)^2\), hence \(V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)\le (u-l)^2\). Thus the set of all fiber values is bounded above.

For an exact-side rule \(\psi:\mathsf Z\times\mathsf{Prob}(\mathsf C)\to[l,u]\), write
\[
R^{\mathrm{ex}}_\theta(\psi)
=
\sum_{z\in\mathsf Z}\Lambda_\theta(z)
\bigl(\psi(z,\Xi_\theta)-\upsilon_\theta\bigr)^2,
\qquad
\mathfrak R_\star
=
\inf_\psi\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}R^{\mathrm{ex}}_\theta(\psi).
\]
For any such \(\psi\), define the fiber decision
\[
\beta_{\psi,\theta_0}(z)=\psi(z,\Xi_{\theta_0}).
\]
If \(\theta\in\mathcal F(\theta_0)\), then \(\Xi_\theta=\Xi_{\theta_0}\), so
\[
R_\theta(\beta_{\psi,\theta_0})=R^{\mathrm{ex}}_\theta(\psi).
\]
Consequently
\[
V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)
\le
\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal F(\theta_0)}R_\theta(\beta_{\psi,\theta_0})
\le
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}R^{\mathrm{ex}}_\theta(\psi).
\]
Taking the infimum over \(\psi\) gives
\[
V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)\le \mathfrak R_\star,
\qquad
\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\vartheta)\le \mathfrak R_\star.
\]

For the reverse inequality, set
\[
\mathfrak s=\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\vartheta).
\]
Let \(\mathfrak a\) be any real number with \(\mathfrak s<\mathfrak a\). Then every fiber value is strictly below \(\mathfrak a\). Since the fiber risks are nonnegative, the strict infimum property gives, for each \(\theta_0\), a decision \(\beta_{\theta_0}:\mathsf Z\to[l,u]\) such that
\[
\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal F(\theta_0)}R_\theta(\beta_{\theta_0})<\mathfrak a.
\]
Define a representative map on the whole simplex by
\[
\operatorname{rep}(\nu)=
\begin{cases}
\text{one }\theta\in\Theta\text{ with }\Xi_\theta=\nu, & \text{if such a }\theta\text{ exists},\\
\text{an arbitrary point of }\Theta, & \text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\]
This is defined for every \(\nu\in\mathsf{Prob}(\mathsf C)\) because \(\Theta\) is nonempty. Now assemble the exact-side rule
\[
\psi_{\mathfrak a}(z,\nu)=\beta_{\operatorname{rep}(\nu)}(z).
\]
For each \(\theta\in\Theta\), the representative \(\operatorname{rep}(\Xi_\theta)\) has side law \(\Xi_\theta\), so \(\theta\in\mathcal F(\operatorname{rep}(\Xi_\theta))\). Hence
\[
R^{\mathrm{ex}}_\theta(\psi_{\mathfrak a})
=
R_\theta\bigl(\beta_{\operatorname{rep}(\Xi_\theta)}\bigr)
<\mathfrak a.
\]
Therefore
\[
\mathfrak R_\star
\le
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}R^{\mathrm{ex}}_\theta(\psi_{\mathfrak a})
\le \mathfrak a.
\]
Since this holds for every \(\mathfrak a>\mathfrak s\),
\[
\mathfrak R_\star\le\mathfrak s.
\]
Combining the two inequalities yields
\[
\mathfrak R_\star=\sup_{\vartheta\in\Theta}V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\vartheta),
\]
and in particular
\[
V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)\le \mathfrak R_\star .
\]
% lean: exactSideMinimaxValue_eq_iSup_fiber

\item For \(\alpha>0\), define the closed side-law neighborhood value
\[
V_\alpha(\theta_0)
=
\inf_{\beta:\mathsf Z\to[l,u]}\ 
\sup_{\theta:\,D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta_0})\le \alpha} R_\theta(\beta).
\]
The required shrinking-neighborhood fact is the reciprocal-radius convergence
\[
V_{1/(N+1)}(\theta_0)\longrightarrow V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)
\qquad\text{as }N\to\infty .
\]
Indeed, for every \(\alpha\ge0\), the exact fiber \(\mathcal F(\theta_0)\) is contained in the closed \(\alpha\)-neighborhood, and therefore
\[
V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)\le V_\alpha(\theta_0).
\]
This gives the lower side of the limit. For the upper side, fix a real number \(\mathfrak b>V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)\) and put
\[
\mathfrak c=\frac{V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)+\mathfrak b}{2},
\qquad
V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)<\mathfrak c<\mathfrak b.
\]
Since the fiber risks are nonnegative, the strict infimum property gives a decision \(\beta^\sharp:\mathsf Z\to[l,u]\) with
\[
\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal F(\theta_0)}R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)<\mathfrak c,
\]
so
\[
\theta\in\mathcal F(\theta_0)
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)<\mathfrak c.
\]
For this fixed decision the map \(\theta\mapsto R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)\) is continuous, because \(\mathsf Z\) is finite and the coordinates \(\Lambda_\theta(z)\) and \(\upsilon_\theta\) are continuous. Hence the set
\[
\mathcal B=
\{\theta\in\Theta:\mathfrak c\le R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)\}
\]
is closed, and compactness of \(\Theta\) makes \(\mathcal B\) compact. The side-law map \(\theta\mapsto\Xi_\theta\) is continuous, so its image \(\Xi(\mathcal B)=\{\Xi_\theta:\theta\in\mathcal B\}\) is compact and therefore closed in the finite simplex. The preceding fiber inequality implies
\[
\Xi_{\theta_0}\notin \Xi(\mathcal B),
\]
for otherwise some \(\theta\in\mathcal B\) would satisfy \(\Xi_\theta=\Xi_{\theta_0}\) and hence both \(\mathfrak c\le R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)\) and \(R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)<\mathfrak c\). Since a closed set misses \(\Xi_{\theta_0}\), there is \(\alpha_0>0\) such that
\[
D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta_0})<\alpha_0
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)<\mathfrak c .
\]
For all sufficiently large \(N\), \(1/(N+1)<\alpha_0\). For such \(N\), every \(\theta\) with
\[
D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta_0})\le \frac1{N+1}
\]
satisfies \(R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)<\mathfrak c\), and therefore
\[
V_{1/(N+1)}(\theta_0)
\le
\sup_{\theta:\,D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta_0})\le 1/(N+1)}
R_\theta(\beta^\sharp)
\le \mathfrak c
<\mathfrak b.
\]
Together with \(V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)\le V_{1/(N+1)}(\theta_0)\), this proves the displayed convergence.

Apply the convergence with \(\mathfrak b=V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)+\delta/2\). Choose an integer \(N_{\theta_0}\ge0\) with
\[
V_{1/(N_{\theta_0}+1)}(\theta_0)
<
V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)+\delta/2 .
\]
Set
\[
r(\theta_0)=\frac1{N_{\theta_0}+1}>0 .
\]
Together with \(V_{\mathrm{fib}}(\theta_0)\le\mathfrak R_\star\), this gives
\[
V_{r(\theta_0)}(\theta_0)<\mathfrak R_\star+\delta .
\]
Since all risks are nonnegative, the range of worst-case risks over admissible decisions is bounded below by \(0\). Hence the strict inequality for the infimum yields a decision
\[
\beta_{\theta_0}:\mathsf Z\to[l,u]
\]
such that
\[
\sup_{\theta:\,D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta_0})\le r(\theta_0)}
R_\theta(\beta_{\theta_0})
<
\mathfrak R_\star+\delta .
\]
Consequently, whenever
\[
D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta_0})<r(\theta_0),
\]
the same \(\theta\) belongs to the closed neighborhood, and therefore
\[
R_\theta(\beta_{\theta_0})\le \mathfrak R_\star+\delta .
\]
% lean: localMinimaxValue_tendsto_fiber

\item The map \(\theta\mapsto\Xi_\theta\) is continuous because each coordinate \(\theta\mapsto\Xi_\theta(c)\) is continuous and \(\mathsf C\) is finite. For each \(\theta_0\), define
\[
U_{\theta_0}
=
\left\{\theta\in\Theta:
D_\infty(\Xi_\theta,\Xi_{\theta_0})<\frac{r(\theta_0)}2
\right\}.
\]
Each \(U_{\theta_0}\) is open, and \(\theta_0\in U_{\theta_0}\). Thus \(\{U_{\theta_0}:\theta_0\in\Theta\}\) is an open cover of \(\Theta\). Compactness gives finitely many centers
\[
\theta^\circ_1,\ldots,\theta^\circ_k
\]
such that these sets still cover \(\Theta\). Since \(\Theta\) is nonempty, this finite subcover has \(k\ge1\). % lean: IsCompact.elim_finite_subcover

\item For each selected center set
\[
\operatorname{rad}_j=r(\theta^\circ_j),
\qquad
\beta_j=\beta_{\theta^\circ_j}.
\]
Define
\[
\eta=\min_{1\le j\le k}\frac{\operatorname{rad}_j}{2}.
\]
Every \(\operatorname{rad}_j\) is positive, so \(\eta>0\), and
\[
\eta\le \frac{\operatorname{rad}_j}{2}<\operatorname{rad}_j
